    Glossary¶

    The key terms of every chapter, in alphabetical order. Each entry ends with a link to the chapter where the term is defined.

    A¶

    abdominal aorta
    portion of the aorta inferior to the aortic hiatus and superior to the common iliac arteries (Chapter 20)
    abdominopelvic cavity
    division of the anterior (ventral) cavity that houses the abdominal and pelvic viscera (Chapter 1)
    abducens nerve
    sixth cranial nerve; responsible for contraction of one of the extraocular muscles (Chapter 13)
    abduct
    move away from midline in the sagittal plane (Chapter 11)
    abduction
    movement in the coronal plane that moves a limb laterally away from the body; spreading of the fingers (Chapter 9)
    abductor
    moves the bone away from the midline (Chapter 11)
    abductor digiti minimi
    muscle that abducts the little finger (Chapter 11)
    abductor pollicis brevis
    muscle that abducts the thumb (Chapter 11)
    abductor pollicis longus
    muscle that inserts into the first metacarpal (Chapter 11)
    ABO blood group
    blood-type classification based on the presence or absence of A and B glycoproteins on the erythrocyte membrane surface (Chapter 18)
    absolute refractory period
    time during an action period when another action potential cannot be generated because the voltage-gated Na+ channel is inactivated (Chapter 12)
    absorption
    passage of digested products from the intestinal lumen through mucosal cells and into the bloodstream or lacteals (Chapter 23)
    absorptive state
    also called the fed state; the metabolic state occurring during the first few hours after ingesting food in which the body is digesting food and absorbing the nutrients (Chapter 24)
    accessory digestive organ
    includes teeth, tongue, salivary glands, gallbladder, liver, and pancreas (Chapter 23)
    accessory duct
    (also, duct of Santorini) duct that runs from the pancreas into the duodenum (Chapter 23)
    acclimatization
    process of adjustment that the respiratory system makes due to chronic exposure to high altitudes (Chapter 22)
    accommodation
    in vision, a change in the ability of the eye to focus on objects at different distances (Chapter 16)
    accommodation–convergence reflex
    coordination of somatic control of the medial rectus muscles of either eye with the parasympathetic control of the ciliary bodies to maintain focus while the eyes converge on visual stimuli near to the face (Chapter 16)
    acetabular labrum
    lip of fibrocartilage that surrounds outer margin of the acetabulum on the hip bone (Chapter 9)
    acetabulum
    large, cup-shaped cavity located on the lateral side of the hip bone; formed by the junction of the ilium, pubis, and ischium portions of the hip bone (Chapter 8)
    acetyl coenzyme A (acetyl CoA)
    starting molecule of the Krebs cycle (Chapter 24)
    acetylcholine (ACh)
    neurotransmitter that binds at a motor end-plate to trigger depolarization (Chapter 10)
    acid
    compound that releases hydrogen ions (H+) in solution (Chapter 2)
    acinus
    cluster of glandular epithelial cells in the pancreas that secretes pancreatic juice in the pancreas (Chapter 23)
    acne
    skin condition due to infected sebaceous glands (Chapter 5)
    acromegaly
    disorder in adults caused when abnormally high levels of GH trigger growth of bones in the face, hands, and feet (Chapter 17)
    acromial end of the clavicle
    lateral end of the clavicle that articulates with the acromion of the scapula (Chapter 8)
    acromial process
    acromion of the scapula (Chapter 8)
    acromioclavicular joint
    articulation between the acromion of the scapula and the acromial end of the clavicle (Chapter 8)
    acromion
    flattened bony process that extends laterally from the scapular spine to form the bony tip of the shoulder (Chapter 8)
    acrosomal reaction
    release of digestive enzymes by sperm that enables them to burrow through the corona radiata and penetrate the zona pellucida of an oocyte prior to fertilization (Chapter 28)
    acrosome
    cap-like vesicle located at the anterior-most region of a sperm that is rich with lysosomal enzymes capable of digesting the protective layers surrounding the oocyte (Chapter 28)
    actin
    protein that makes up most of the thin myofilaments in a sarcomere muscle fiber (Chapter 10)
    action potential
    change in voltage of a cell membrane in response to a stimulus that results in transmission of an electrical signal; unique to neurons and muscle fibers (Chapter 10)
    activation energy
    amount of energy greater than the energy contained in the reactants, which must be overcome for a reaction to proceed (Chapter 2)
    activation gate
    part of the voltage-gated Na+ channel that opens when the membrane voltage reaches threshold (Chapter 12)
    active immunity
    immunity developed from an individual’s own immune system (Chapter 21)
    active transport
    form of transport across the cell membrane that requires input of cellular energy (Chapter 3)
    acute inflammation
    inflammation occurring for a limited time period; rapidly developing (Chapter 21)
    acute mountain sickness (AMS)
    condition that occurs a result of acute exposure to high altitude due to a low partial pressure of oxygen (Chapter 22)
    adaptive immune response
    relatively slow but very specific and effective immune response controlled by lymphocytes (Chapter 21)
    adduction
    movement in the coronal plane that moves a limb medially toward or across the midline of the body; bringing fingers together (Chapter 9)
    adductor
    moves the bone toward the midline (Chapter 11)
    adductor brevis
    muscle that adducts and medially rotates the thigh (Chapter 11)
    adductor longus
    muscle that adducts, medially rotates, and flexes the thigh (Chapter 11)
    adductor magnus
    muscle with an anterior fascicle that adducts, medially rotates and flexes the thigh, and a posterior fascicle that assists in thigh extension (Chapter 11)
    adductor pollicis
    muscle that adducts the thumb (Chapter 11)
    adductor tubercle
    small, bony bump located on the superior aspect of the medial epicondyle of the femur (Chapter 8)
    adenosine triphosphate (ATP)
    nucleotide containing ribose and an adenine base that is essential in energy transfer (Chapter 2)
    adenylyl cyclase
    membrane-bound enzyme that converts ATP to cyclic AMP, creating cAMP, as a result of G-protein activation (Chapter 17)
    adipocytes
    lipid storage cells (Chapter 4)
    adipose tissue
    specialized areolar tissue rich in stored fat (Chapter 4)
    adrenal artery
    branch of the abdominal aorta; supplies blood to the adrenal (suprarenal) glands (Chapter 20)
    adrenal cortex
    outer region of the adrenal glands consisting of multiple layers of epithelial cells and capillary networks that produces mineralocorticoids and glucocorticoids (Chapter 17)
    adrenal glands
    endocrine glands located at the top of each kidney that are important for the regulation of the stress response, blood pressure and blood volume, water homeostasis, and electrolyte levels (Chapter 17)
    adrenal medulla
    interior portion of the adrenal (or suprarenal) gland that releases epinephrine and norepinephrine into the bloodstream as hormones (Chapter 15)
    adrenal medulla
    inner layer of the adrenal glands that plays an important role in the stress response by producing epinephrine and norepinephrine (Chapter 17)
    adrenal vein
    drains the adrenal or suprarenal glands that are immediately superior to the kidneys; the right adrenal vein enters the inferior vena cava directly and the left adrenal vein enters the left renal vein (Chapter 20)
    adrenergic
    synapse where norepinephrine is released, which binds to α- or β-adrenergic receptors (Chapter 15)
    adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)
    anterior pituitary hormone that stimulates the adrenal cortex to secrete corticosteroid hormones (also called corticotropin) (Chapter 17)
    aerobic respiration
    production of ATP in the presence of oxygen (Chapter 10)
    afferent branch
    component of a reflex arc that represents the input from a sensory neuron, for either a special or general sense (Chapter 15)
    afferent lymphatic vessels
    lead into a lymph node (Chapter 21)
    afterbirth
    third stage of childbirth in which the placenta and associated fetal membranes are expelled (Chapter 28)
    afterload
    force the ventricles must develop to effectively pump blood against the resistance in the vessels (Chapter 19)
    agglutination
    clustering of cells into masses linked by antibodies (Chapter 18)
    agonist
    (also, prime mover) muscle whose contraction is responsible for producing a particular motion (Chapter 11)
    agonist
    any exogenous substance that binds to a receptor and produces a similar effect to the endogenous ligand (Chapter 15)
    agranular leukocytes
    leukocytes with few granules in their cytoplasm; specifically, monocytes, lymphocytes, and NK cells (Chapter 18)
    ala
    (plural = alae) small, flaring structure of a nostril that forms the lateral side of the nares (Chapter 22)
    alar cartilage
    cartilage that supports the apex of the nose and helps shape the nares; it is connected to the septal cartilage and connective tissue of the alae (Chapter 22)
    alar plate
    developmental region of the spinal cord that gives rise to the posterior horn of the gray matter (Chapter 13)
    alarm reaction
    the short-term stress, or the fight-or-flight response, of stage one of the general adaptation syndrome mediated by the hormones epinephrine and norepinephrine (Chapter 17)
    albinism
    genetic disorder that affects the skin, in which there is no melanin production (Chapter 5)
    albumin
    most abundant plasma protein, accounting for most of the osmotic pressure of plasma (Chapter 18)
    aldosterone
    hormone produced and secreted by the adrenal cortex that stimulates sodium and fluid retention and increases blood volume and blood pressure (Chapter 17)
    alimentary canal
    continuous muscular digestive tube that extends from the mouth to the anus (Chapter 23)
    alkaloid
    substance, usually from a plant source, that is chemically basic with respect to pH and will stimulate bitter receptors (Chapter 14)
    allantois
    finger-like outpocketing of yolk sac forms the primitive excretory duct of the embryo; precursor to the urinary bladder (Chapter 28)
    allele
    alternative forms of a gene that occupy a specific locus on a specific gene (Chapter 28)
    alpha (α)-adrenergic receptor
    one of the receptors to which epinephrine and norepinephrine bind, which comes in two subtypes: α1 and α2 (Chapter 15)
    alpha cell
    pancreatic islet cell type that produces the hormone glucagon (Chapter 17)
    alveolar dead space
    air space within alveoli that are unable to participate in gas exchange (Chapter 22)
    alveolar duct
    small tube that leads from the terminal bronchiole to the respiratory bronchiole and is the point of attachment for alveoli (Chapter 22)
    alveolar macrophage
    immune system cell of the alveolus that removes debris and pathogens (Chapter 22)
    alveolar pore
    opening that allows airflow between neighboring alveoli (Chapter 22)
    alveolar process of the mandible
    upper border of mandibular body that contains the lower teeth (Chapter 7)
    alveolar process of the maxilla
    curved, inferior margin of the maxilla that supports and anchors the upper teeth (Chapter 7)
    alveolar sac
    cluster of alveoli (Chapter 22)
    alveoli
    (of the breast) milk-secreting cells in the mammary gland (Chapter 27)
    alveolus
    small, grape-like sac that performs gas exchange in the lungs (Chapter 22)
    amacrine cell
    type of cell in the retina that connects to the bipolar cells near the outer synaptic layer and provides the basis for early image processing within the retina (Chapter 14)
    amino acid
    building block of proteins; characterized by an amino and carboxyl functional groups and a variable side-chain (Chapter 2)
    aminopeptidase
    brush border enzyme that acts on proteins (Chapter 23)
    amnion
    transparent membranous sac that encloses the developing fetus and fills with amniotic fluid (Chapter 28)
    amniotic cavity
    cavity that opens up between the inner cell mass and the trophoblast; develops into amnion (Chapter 28)
    amphiarthrosis
    slightly mobile joint (Chapter 9)
    amphipathic
    describes a molecule that exhibits a difference in polarity between its two ends, resulting in a difference in water solubility (Chapter 3)
    ampulla
    in the ear, the structure at the base of a semicircular canal that contains the hair cells and cupula for transduction of rotational movement of the head (Chapter 14)
    ampulla
    (of the uterine tube) middle portion of the uterine tube in which fertilization often occurs (Chapter 27)
    amygdala
    nucleus deep in the temporal lobe of the cerebrum that is related to memory and emotional behavior (Chapter 13)
    anabolic hormones
    hormones that stimulate the synthesis of new, larger molecules (Chapter 24)
    anabolic reactions
    reactions that build smaller molecules into larger molecules (Chapter 24)
    anabolism
    assembly of more complex molecules from simpler molecules (Chapter 1)
    anagen
    active phase of the hair growth cycle (Chapter 5)
    anal canal
    final segment of the large intestine (Chapter 23)
    anal column
    long fold of mucosa in the anal canal (Chapter 23)
    anal sinus
    recess between anal columns (Chapter 23)
    anal triangle
    posterior triangle of the perineum that includes the anus (Chapter 11)
    anaphase
    third stage of mitosis (and meiosis), during which sister chromatids separate into two new nuclear regions of a dividing cell (Chapter 3)
    anaphylactic shock
    type of shock that follows a severe allergic reaction and results from massive vasodilation (Chapter 20)
    anastomosis
    (plural = anastomoses) area where vessels unite to allow blood to circulate even if there may be partial blockage in another branch (Chapter 19)
    anatomical dead space
    air space present in the airway that never reaches the alveoli and therefore never participates in gas exchange (Chapter 22)
    anatomical neck
    line on the humerus located around the outside margin of the humeral head (Chapter 8)
    anatomical position
    standard reference position used for describing locations and directions on the human body (Chapter 1)
    anatomical sphincter
    smooth or skeletal muscle surrounding the lumen of a vessel or hollow organ that can restrict flow when contracted (Chapter 25)
    anatomy
    science that studies the form and composition of the body’s structures (Chapter 1)
    anchoring junction
    mechanically attaches adjacent cells to each other or to the basement membrane (Chapter 4)
    anconeus
    small muscle on the lateral posterior elbow that extends the forearm (Chapter 11)
    anemia
    deficiency of red blood cells or hemoglobin (Chapter 18)
    angioblasts
    stem cells that give rise to blood vessels (Chapter 20)
    angiogenesis
    formation of blood capillary networks (Chapter 10)
    angiogenesis
    development of new blood vessels from existing vessels (Chapter 20)
    angiotensin I
    protein produced by the enzymatic action of renin on angiotensinogen; inactive precursor of angiotensin II (Chapter 25)
    angiotensin II
    protein produced by the enzymatic action of ACE on inactive angiotensin I; actively causes vasoconstriction and stimulates aldosterone release by the adrenal cortex (Chapter 25)
    angiotensin-converting enzyme
    the enzyme that converts angiotensin I to angiotensin II (Chapter 17)
    angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE)
    enzyme produced by the lungs that catalyzes the reaction of inactive angiotensin I into active angiotensin II (Chapter 25)
    angiotensinogen
    inactive protein in the circulation produced by the liver; precursor of angiotensin I; must be modified by the enzymes renin and ACE to be activated (Chapter 25)
    angle of the mandible
    rounded corner located at outside margin of the body and ramus junction (Chapter 7)
    angle of the rib
    portion of rib with greatest curvature; together, the rib angles form the most posterior extent of the thoracic cage (Chapter 7)
    anion
    atom with a negative charge (Chapter 2)
    ankle joint
    joint that separates the leg and foot portions of the lower limb; formed by the articulations between the talus bone of the foot inferiorly, and the distal end of the tibia, medial malleolus of the tibia, and lateral malleolus of the fibula superiorly (Chapter 8)
    annular ligament
    intrinsic ligament of the elbow articular capsule that surrounds and supports the head of the radius at the proximal radioulnar joint (Chapter 9)
    anosmia
    loss of the sense of smell; usually the result of physical disruption of the first cranial nerve (Chapter 14)
    antagonist
    muscle that opposes the action of an agonist (Chapter 11)
    antagonist
    any exogenous substance that binds to a receptor and produces an opposing effect to the endogenous ligand (Chapter 15)
    anterior
    describes the front or direction toward the front of the body; also referred to as ventral (Chapter 1)
    anterior (ventral) sacral foramen
    one of the series of paired openings located on the anterior (ventral) side of the sacrum (Chapter 7)
    anterior arch
    anterior portion of the ring-like C1 (atlas) vertebra (Chapter 7)
    anterior border of the tibia
    narrow, anterior margin of the tibia that extends inferiorly from the tibial tuberosity (Chapter 8)
    anterior cardiac veins
    vessels that parallel the small cardiac arteries and drain the anterior surface of the right ventricle; bypass the coronary sinus and drain directly into the right atrium (Chapter 19)
    anterior cavity
    larger body cavity located anterior to the posterior (dorsal) body cavity; includes the serous membrane-lined pleural cavities for the lungs, pericardial cavity for the heart, and peritoneal cavity for the abdominal and pelvic organs; also referred to as ventral cavity (Chapter 1)
    anterior cerebral artery
    arises from the internal carotid artery; supplies the frontal lobe of the cerebrum (Chapter 20)
    anterior column
    white matter between the anterior horns of the spinal cord composed of many different groups of axons of both ascending and descending tracts (Chapter 13)
    anterior communicating artery
    anastomosis of the right and left internal carotid arteries; supplies blood to the brain (Chapter 20)
    anterior compartment of the arm
    (anterior flexor compartment of the arm) the biceps brachii, brachialis, brachioradialis, and their associated blood vessels and nerves (Chapter 11)
    anterior compartment of the forearm
    (anterior flexor compartment of the forearm) deep and superficial muscles that originate on the humerus and insert into the hand (Chapter 11)
    anterior compartment of the leg
    region that includes muscles that dorsiflex the foot (Chapter 11)
    anterior compartment of the thigh
    region that includes muscles that flex the thigh and extend the leg (Chapter 11)
    anterior corticospinal tract
    division of the corticospinal pathway that travels through the ventral (anterior) column of the spinal cord and controls axial musculature through the medial motor neurons in the ventral (anterior) horn (Chapter 14)
    anterior cranial fossa
    shallowest and most anterior cranial fossa of the cranial base that extends from the frontal bone to the lesser wing of the sphenoid bone (Chapter 7)
    anterior cruciate ligament
    intracapsular ligament of the knee; extends from anterior, superior surface of the tibia to the inner aspect of the lateral condyle of the femur; resists hyperextension of knee (Chapter 9)
    anterior horn
    gray matter of the spinal cord containing multipolar motor neurons, sometimes referred to as the ventral horn (Chapter 13)
    anterior inferior iliac spine
    small, bony projection located on the anterior margin of the ilium, below the anterior superior iliac spine (Chapter 8)
    anterior interventricular artery
    (also, left anterior descending artery or LAD) major branch of the left coronary artery that follows the anterior interventricular sulcus (Chapter 19)
    anterior interventricular sulcus
    sulcus located between the left and right ventricles on the anterior surface of the heart (Chapter 19)
    anterior longitudinal ligament
    ligament that runs the length of the vertebral column, uniting the anterior aspects of the vertebral bodies (Chapter 7)
    anterior median fissure
    deep midline feature of the anterior spinal cord, marking the separation between the right and left sides of the cord (Chapter 13)
    anterior sacroiliac ligament
    strong ligament between the sacrum and the ilium portions of the hip bone that supports the anterior side of the sacroiliac joint (Chapter 8)
    anterior scalene
    a muscle anterior to the middle scalene (Chapter 11)
    anterior spinal artery
    blood vessel from the merged branches of the vertebral arteries that runs along the anterior surface of the spinal cord (Chapter 13)
    anterior superior iliac spine
    rounded, anterior end of the iliac crest (Chapter 8)
    anterior talofibular ligament
    intrinsic ligament located on the lateral side of the ankle joint, between talus bone and lateral malleolus of fibula; supports talus at the talocrural joint and resists excess inversion of the foot (Chapter 9)
    anterior tibial artery
    branches from the popliteal artery; supplies blood to the anterior tibial region; becomes the dorsalis pedis artery (Chapter 20)
    anterior tibial vein
    forms from the dorsal venous arch; drains the area near the tibialis anterior muscle and leads to the popliteal vein (Chapter 20)
    anterograde amnesia
    inability to form new memories from a particular time forward (Chapter 16)
    antibodies
    (also, immunoglobulins or gamma globulins) antigen-specific proteins produced by specialized B lymphocytes that protect the body by binding to foreign objects such as bacteria and viruses (Chapter 18)
    antibody
    antigen-specific protein secreted by plasma cells; immunoglobulin (Chapter 21)
    anticholinergic drugs
    drugs that interrupt or reduce the function of the parasympathetic system (Chapter 15)
    anticoagulant
    substance such as heparin that opposes coagulation (Chapter 18)
    anticodon
    consecutive sequence of three nucleotides on a tRNA molecule that is complementary to a specific codon on an mRNA molecule (Chapter 3)
    antidiuretic hormone (ADH)
    hypothalamic hormone that is stored by the posterior pituitary and that signals the kidneys to reabsorb water (Chapter 17)
    antidiuretic hormone (ADH)
    also known as vasopressin, a hormone that increases the volume of water reabsorbed from the collecting tubules of the kidney (Chapter 26)
    antigen
    molecule recognized by the receptors of B and T lymphocytes (Chapter 21)
    antigen presentation
    binding of processed antigen to the protein-binding cleft of a major histocompatibility complex molecule (Chapter 21)
    antigen processing
    internalization and digestion of antigen in an antigen-presenting cell (Chapter 21)
    antigen receptor
    two-chain receptor by which lymphocytes recognize antigen (Chapter 21)
    antigenic determinant
    (also, epitope) one of the chemical groups recognized by a single type of lymphocyte antigen receptor (Chapter 21)
    antithrombin
    anticoagulant that inactivates factor X and opposes the conversion of prothrombin (factor II) into thrombin in the common pathway (Chapter 18)
    antrum
    fluid-filled chamber that characterizes a mature tertiary (antral) follicle (Chapter 27)
    anulus fibrosus
    tough, fibrous outer portion of an intervertebral disc, which is strongly anchored to the bodies of the adjacent vertebrae (Chapter 7)
    anuria
    absence of urine produced; production of 50 mL or less per day (Chapter 25)
    aorta
    largest artery in the body, originating from the left ventricle and descending to the abdominal region where it bifurcates into the common iliac arteries at the level of the fourth lumbar vertebra; arteries originating from the aorta distribute blood to virtually all tissues of the body (Chapter 20)
    aortic arch
    arc that connects the ascending aorta to the descending aorta; ends at the intervertebral disk between the fourth and fifth thoracic vertebrae (Chapter 20)
    aortic hiatus
    opening in the diaphragm that allows passage of the thoracic aorta into the abdominal region where it becomes the abdominal aorta (Chapter 20)
    aortic sinuses
    small pockets in the ascending aorta near the aortic valve that are the locations of the baroreceptors (stretch receptors) and chemoreceptors that trigger a reflex that aids in the regulation of vascular homeostasis (Chapter 20)
    aortic valve
    (also, aortic semilunar valve) valve located at the base of the aorta (Chapter 19)
    apex
    tip of the external nose (Chapter 22)
    aphasia
    loss of language function (Chapter 16)
    apical
    that part of a cell or tissue which, in general, faces an open space (Chapter 4)
    apical ectodermal ridge
    enlarged ridge of ectoderm at the distal end of a limb bud that stimulates growth and elongation of the limb (Chapter 8)
    apneustic center
    network of neurons within the pons that stimulate the neurons in the dorsal respiratory group; controls the depth of inspiration (Chapter 22)
    apocrine secretion
    release of a substance along with the apical portion of the cell (Chapter 4)
    apocrine sweat gland
    type of sweat gland that is associated with hair follicles in the armpits and genital regions (Chapter 5)
    aponeurosis
    broad, tendon-like sheet of connective tissue that attaches a skeletal muscle to another skeletal muscle or to a bone (Chapter 10)
    apoptosis
    programmed cell death (Chapter 4)
    appendicular
    of the arms and legs (Chapter 11)
    appendicular skeleton
    all bones of the upper and lower limbs, plus the girdle bones that attach each limb to the axial skeleton (Chapter 7)
    appendix
    (vermiform appendix) coiled tube attached to the cecum (Chapter 23)
    aquaporin
    protein-forming water channels through the lipid bilayer of the cell; allows water to cross; activation in the collecting ducts is under the control of ADH (Chapter 25)
    aqueous humor
    watery fluid that fills the anterior chamber containing the cornea, iris, ciliary body, and lens of the eye (Chapter 14)
    arachnoid granulation
    outpocket of the arachnoid membrane into the dural sinuses that allows for reabsorption of CSF into the blood (Chapter 13)
    arachnoid mater
    middle layer of the meninges named for the spider-web–like trabeculae that extend between it and the pia mater (Chapter 13)
    arachnoid trabeculae
    filaments between the arachnoid and pia mater within the subarachnoid space (Chapter 13)
    arcuate line of the ilium
    smooth ridge located at the inferior margin of the iliac fossa; forms the lateral portion of the pelvic brim (Chapter 8)
    areola
    highly pigmented, circular area surrounding the raised nipple and containing areolar glands that secrete fluid important for lubrication during suckling (Chapter 27)
    areolar tissue
    (also, loose connective tissue) a type of connective tissue proper that shows little specialization with cells dispersed in the matrix (Chapter 4)
    arm
    region of the upper limb located between the shoulder and elbow joints; contains the humerus bone (Chapter 8)
    arrector pili
    smooth muscle that is activated in response to external stimuli that pull on hair follicles and make the hair “stand up” (Chapter 5)
    arterial circle
    (also, circle of Willis) anastomosis located at the base of the brain that ensures continual blood supply; formed from branches of the internal carotid and vertebral arteries; supplies blood to the brain (Chapter 20)
    arteriole
    (also, resistance vessel) very small artery that leads to a capillary (Chapter 20)
    arteriovenous anastomosis
    short vessel connecting an arteriole directly to a venule and bypassing the capillary beds (Chapter 20)
    artery
    blood vessel that conducts blood away from the heart; may be a conducting or distributing vessel (Chapter 20)
    articular capsule
    connective tissue structure that encloses the joint cavity of a synovial joint (Chapter 9)
    articular cartilage
    thin layer of cartilage covering an epiphysis; reduces friction and acts as a shock absorber (Chapter 6)
    articular cartilage
    thin layer of hyaline cartilage that covers the articulating surfaces of bones at a synovial joint (Chapter 9)
    articular disc
    meniscus; a fibrocartilage structure found between the bones of some synovial joints; provides padding or smooths movements between the bones; strongly unites the bones together (Chapter 9)
    articular tubercle
    smooth ridge located on the inferior skull, immediately anterior to the mandibular fossa (Chapter 7)
    articulation
    where two bone surfaces meet (Chapter 6)
    articulation
    joint of the body (Chapter 9)
    artificial pacemaker
    medical device that transmits electrical signals to the heart to ensure that it contracts and pumps blood to the body (Chapter 19)
    ascending aorta
    initial portion of the aorta, rising from the left ventricle for a distance of approximately 5 cm (Chapter 20)
    ascending colon
    first region of the colon (Chapter 23)
    ascending pathway
    fiber structure that relays sensory information from the periphery through the spinal cord and brain stem to other structures of the brain (Chapter 14)
    ascending tract
    central nervous system fibers carrying sensory information from the spinal cord or periphery to the brain (Chapter 13)
    association area
    region of cortex connected to a primary sensory cortical area that further processes the information to generate more complex sensory perceptions (Chapter 14)
    astrocyte
    star-shaped cell in the central nervous system that regulates ions and uptake and/or breakdown of some neurotransmitters and contributes to the formation of the blood-brain barrier (Chapter 4)
    astrocyte
    glial cell type of the CNS that provides support for neurons and maintains the blood-brain barrier (Chapter 12)
    ataxia
    movement disorder related to damage of the cerebellum characterized by loss of coordination in voluntary movements (Chapter 16)
    atlanto-occipital joint
    articulation between the occipital condyles of the skull and the superior articular processes of the atlas (C1 vertebra) (Chapter 9)
    atlantoaxial joint
    series of three articulations between the atlas (C1) vertebra and the axis (C2) vertebra, consisting of the joints between the inferior articular processes of C1 and the superior articular processes of C2, and the articulation between the dens of C2 and the anterior arch of C1 (Chapter 9)
    atlas
    first cervical (C1) vertebra (Chapter 7)
    atmospheric pressure
    amount of force that is exerted by gases in the air surrounding any given surface (Chapter 22)
    atom
    smallest unit of an element that retains the unique properties of that element (Chapter 2)
    atomic number
    number of protons in the nucleus of an atom (Chapter 2)
    ATP synthase
    protein pore complex that creates ATP (Chapter 24)
    ATPase
    enzyme that hydrolyzes ATP to ADP (Chapter 10)
    atrial natriuretic peptide (ANP)
    peptide hormone produced by the walls of the atria in response to high blood pressure, blood volume, or blood sodium that reduces the reabsorption of sodium and water in the kidneys and promotes vasodilation (Chapter 17)
    atrial reflex
    (also, called Bainbridge reflex) autonomic reflex that responds to stretch receptors in the atria that send impulses to the cardioaccelerator area to increase HR when venous flow into the atria increases (Chapter 19)
    atrial reflex
    mechanism for maintaining vascular homeostasis involving atrial baroreceptors: if blood is returning to the right atrium more rapidly than it is being ejected from the left ventricle, the atrial receptors will stimulate the cardiovascular centers to increase sympathetic firing and increase cardiac output until the situation is reversed; the opposite is also true (Chapter 20)
    atrioventricular (AV) node
    clump of myocardial cells located in the inferior portion of the right atrium within the atrioventricular septum; receives the impulse from the SA node, pauses, and then transmits it into specialized conducting cells within the interventricular septum (Chapter 19)
    atrioventricular bundle
    (also, bundle of His) group of specialized myocardial conductile cells that transmit the impulse from the AV node through the interventricular septum; form the left and right atrioventricular bundle branches (Chapter 19)
    atrioventricular bundle branches
    (also, left or right bundle branches) specialized myocardial conductile cells that arise from the bifurcation of the atrioventricular bundle and pass through the interventricular septum; lead to the Purkinje fibers and also to the right papillary muscle via the moderator band (Chapter 19)
    atrioventricular septum
    cardiac septum located between the atria and ventricles; atrioventricular valves are located here (Chapter 19)
    atrioventricular valves
    one-way valves located between the atria and ventricles; the valve on the right is called the tricuspid valve, and the one on the left is the mitral or bicuspid valve (Chapter 19)
    atrium
    (plural = atria) upper or receiving chamber of the heart that pumps blood into the lower chambers just prior to their contraction; the right atrium receives blood from the systemic circuit that flows into the right ventricle; the left atrium receives blood from the pulmonary circuit that flows into the left ventricle (Chapter 19)
    atrophy
    loss of mass and function (Chapter 4)
    atrophy
    loss of structural proteins from muscle fibers (Chapter 10)
    audition
    sense of hearing (Chapter 14)
    auricle
    fleshy external structure of the ear (Chapter 14)
    auricle
    extension of an atrium visible on the superior surface of the heart (Chapter 19)
    auricular surface of the ilium
    roughened area located on the posterior, medial side of the ilium of the hip bone; articulates with the auricular surface of the sacrum to form the sacroiliac joint (Chapter 8)
    autocrine
    chemical signal that elicits a response in the same cell that secreted it (Chapter 17)
    autolysis
    breakdown of cells by their own enzymatic action (Chapter 3)
    autonomic nervous system (ANS)
    functional division of the nervous system that is responsible for homeostatic reflexes that coordinate control of cardiac and smooth muscle, as well as glandular tissue (Chapter 12)
    autonomic tone
    tendency of an organ system to be governed by one division of the autonomic nervous system over the other, such as heart rate being lowered by parasympathetic input at rest (Chapter 15)
    autonomic tone
    contractile state during resting cardiac activity produced by mild sympathetic and parasympathetic stimulation (Chapter 19)
    autophagy
    lysosomal breakdown of a cell’s own components (Chapter 3)
    autorhythmicity
    heart’s ability to control its own contractions (Chapter 10)
    autorhythmicity
    ability of cardiac muscle to initiate its own electrical impulse that triggers the mechanical contraction that pumps blood at a fixed pace without nervous or endocrine control (Chapter 19)
    autosomal chromosome
    in humans, the 22 pairs of chromosomes that are not the sex chromosomes (XX or XY) (Chapter 28)
    autosomal dominant
    pattern of dominant inheritance that corresponds to a gene on one of the 22 autosomal chromosomes (Chapter 28)
    autosomal recessive
    pattern of recessive inheritance that corresponds to a gene on one of the 22 autosomal chromosomes (Chapter 28)
    axial
    of the trunk and head (Chapter 11)
    axial skeleton
    central, vertical axis of the body, including the skull, vertebral column, and thoracic cage (Chapter 7)
    axillary artery
    continuation of the subclavian artery as it penetrates the body wall and enters the axillary region; supplies blood to the region near the head of the humerus (humeral circumflex arteries); the majority of the vessel continues into the brachium and becomes the brachial artery (Chapter 20)
    axillary nerve
    systemic nerve of the arm that arises from the brachial plexus (Chapter 13)
    axillary vein
    major vein in the axillary region; drains the upper limb and becomes the subclavian vein (Chapter 20)
    axis
    second cervical (C2) vertebra (Chapter 7)
    axon
    single process of the neuron that carries an electrical signal (action potential) away from the cell body toward a target cell (Chapter 12)
    axon hillock
    tapering of the neuron cell body that gives rise to the axon (Chapter 12)
    axon segment
    single stretch of the axon insulated by myelin and bounded by nodes of Ranvier at either end (except for the first, which is after the initial segment, and the last, which is followed by the axon terminal) (Chapter 12)
    axon terminal
    end of the axon, where there are usually several branches extending toward the target cell (Chapter 12)
    axoplasm
    cytoplasm of an axon, which is different in composition than the cytoplasm of the neuronal cell body (Chapter 12)
    azygos vein
    originates in the lumbar region and passes through the diaphragm into the thoracic cavity on the right side of the vertebral column; drains blood from the intercostal veins, esophageal veins, bronchial veins, and other veins draining the mediastinal region; leads to the superior vena cava (Chapter 20)

    B¶

    B cells
    lymphocytes that act by differentiating into an antibody-secreting plasma cell (Chapter 21)
    B lymphocytes
    (also, B cells) lymphocytes that defend the body against specific pathogens and thereby provide specific immunity (Chapter 18)
    Babinski sign
    dorsiflexion of the foot with extension and splaying of the toes in response to the plantar reflex, normally suppressed by corticospinal input (Chapter 16)
    Bachmann’s bundle
    (also, interatrial band) group of specialized conducting cells that transmit the impulse directly from the SA node in the right atrium to the left atrium (Chapter 19)
    bacterial flora
    bacteria in the large intestine (Chapter 23)
    Bainbridge reflex
    (also, called atrial reflex) autonomic reflex that responds to stretch receptors in the atria that send impulses to the cardioaccelerator area to increase HR when venous flow into the atria increases (Chapter 19)
    ball-and-socket joint
    synovial joint formed between the spherical end of one bone (the ball) that fits into the depression of a second bone (the socket); found at the hip and shoulder joints; functionally classified as a multiaxial joint (Chapter 9)
    baroreceptor
    mechanoreceptor that senses the stretch of blood vessels to indicate changes in blood pressure (Chapter 15)
    baroreceptor reflex
    autonomic reflex in which the cardiac centers monitor signals from the baroreceptor stretch receptors and regulate heart function based on blood flow (Chapter 19)
    barrier defenses
    antipathogen defenses deriving from a barrier that physically prevents pathogens from entering the body to establish an infection (Chapter 21)
    Bartholin’s glands
    (also, greater vestibular glands) glands that produce a thick mucus that maintains moisture in the vulva area; also referred to as the greater vestibular glands (Chapter 27)
    basal cell
    type of stem cell found in the stratum basale and in the hair matrix that continually undergoes cell division, producing the keratinocytes of the epidermis (Chapter 5)
    basal cell carcinoma
    cancer that originates from basal cells in the epidermis of the skin (Chapter 5)
    basal forebrain
    nuclei of the cerebrum related to modulation of sensory stimuli and attention through broad projections to the cerebral cortex, loss of which is related to Alzheimer’s disease (Chapter 13)
    basal lamina
    thin extracellular layer that lies underneath epithelial cells and separates them from other tissues (Chapter 4)
    basal metabolic rate (BMR)
    amount of energy expended by the body at rest (Chapter 24)
    basal nuclei
    nuclei of the cerebrum (with a few components in the upper brain stem and diencephalon) that are responsible for assessing cortical movement commands and comparing them with the general state of the individual through broad modulatory activity of dopamine neurons; largely related to motor functions, as evidenced through the symptoms of Parkinson’s and Huntington’s diseases (Chapter 13)
    basal plate
    developmental region of the spinal cord that gives rise to the lateral and anterior horns of gray matter (Chapter 13)
    base
    compound that accepts hydrogen ions (H+) in solution (Chapter 2)
    base of the metatarsal bone
    expanded, proximal end of each metatarsal bone (Chapter 8)
    basement membrane
    in epithelial tissue, a thin layer of fibrous material that anchors the epithelial tissue to the underlying connective tissue; made up of the basal lamina and reticular lamina (Chapter 4)
    basilar artery
    blood vessel from the merged vertebral arteries that runs along the dorsal surface of the brain stem (Chapter 13)
    basilar artery
    formed from the fusion of the two vertebral arteries; sends branches to the cerebellum, brain stem, and the posterior cerebral arteries; the main blood supply to the brain stem (Chapter 20)
    basilar membrane
    in the ear, the floor of the cochlear duct on which the organ of Corti sits (Chapter 14)
    basilic vein
    superficial vein of the arm that arises from the palmar venous arches, intersects with the median cubital vein, parallels the ulnar vein, and continues into the upper arm; along with the brachial vein, it leads to the axillary vein (Chapter 20)
    basophils
    granulocytes that stain with a basic (alkaline) stain and store histamine and heparin (Chapter 18)
    bedsore
    sore on the skin that develops when regions of the body start necrotizing due to constant pressure and lack of blood supply; also called decubitus ulcers (Chapter 5)
    belly
    bulky central body of a muscle (Chapter 11)
    beta (β)-adrenergic receptor
    one of the receptors to which epinephrine and norepinephrine bind, which comes in three subtypes: β1, β2, and β3 (Chapter 15)
    beta (β)-hydroxybutyrate
    primary ketone body produced in the body (Chapter 24)
    beta (β)-oxidation
    fatty acid oxidation (Chapter 24)
    beta cell
    pancreatic islet cell type that produces the hormone insulin (Chapter 17)
    Betz cells
    output cells of the primary motor cortex that cause musculature to move through synapses on cranial and spinal motor neurons (Chapter 14)
    bi
    two (Chapter 11)
    biaxial joint
    type of diarthrosis; a joint that allows for movements within two planes (two axes) (Chapter 9)
    biceps brachii
    two-headed muscle that crosses the shoulder and elbow joints to flex the forearm while assisting in supinating it and flexing the arm at the shoulder (Chapter 11)
    biceps femoris
    hamstring muscle (Chapter 11)
    bicipital groove
    intertubercular groove; narrow groove located between the greater and lesser tubercles of the humerus (Chapter 8)
    bicuspid valve
    (also, mitral valve or left atrioventricular valve) valve located between the left atrium and ventricle; consists of two flaps of tissue (Chapter 19)
    bile
    alkaline solution produced by the liver and important for the emulsification of lipids (Chapter 23)
    bile canaliculus
    small duct between hepatocytes that collects bile (Chapter 23)
    bile salts
    salts that are released from the liver in response to lipid ingestion and surround the insoluble triglycerides to aid in their conversion to monoglycerides and free fatty acids (Chapter 24)
    bilirubin
    yellowish bile pigment produced when iron is removed from heme and is further broken down into waste products (Chapter 18)
    bilirubin
    main bile pigment, which is responsible for the brown color of feces (Chapter 23)
    biliverdin
    green bile pigment produced when the non-iron portion of heme is degraded into a waste product; converted to bilirubin in the liver (Chapter 18)
    binocular depth cues
    indications of the distance of visual stimuli on the basis of slight differences in the images projected onto either retina (Chapter 14)
    biogenic amine
    class of neurotransmitters that are enzymatically derived from amino acids but no longer contain a carboxyl group (Chapter 12)
    biosynthesis reactions
    reactions that create new molecules, also called anabolic reactions (Chapter 24)
    bipennate
    pennate muscle that has fascicles that are located on both sides of the tendon (Chapter 11)
    bipolar
    shape of a neuron with two processes extending from the neuron cell body—the axon and one dendrite (Chapter 12)
    bipolar cell
    cell type in the retina that connects the photoreceptors to the RGCs (Chapter 14)
    blastocoel
    fluid-filled cavity of the blastocyst (Chapter 28)
    blastocyst
    term for the conceptus at the developmental stage that consists of about 100 cells shaped into an inner cell mass that is fated to become the embryo and an outer trophoblast that is fated to become the associated fetal membranes and placenta (Chapter 28)
    blastomere
    daughter cell of a cleavage (Chapter 28)
    blood
    liquid connective tissue composed of formed elements—erythrocytes, leukocytes, and platelets—and a fluid extracellular matrix called plasma; component of the cardiovascular system (Chapter 18)
    blood colloidal osmotic pressure (BCOP)
    pressure exerted by colloids suspended in blood within a vessel; a primary determinant is the presence of plasma proteins (Chapter 20)
    blood flow
    movement of blood through a vessel, tissue, or organ that is usually expressed in terms of volume per unit of time (Chapter 20)
    blood hydrostatic pressure
    force blood exerts against the walls of a blood vessel or heart chamber (Chapter 20)
    blood islands
    masses of developing blood vessels and formed elements from mesodermal cells scattered throughout the embryonic disc (Chapter 20)
    blood pressure
    force exerted by the blood against the wall of a vessel or heart chamber; can be described with the more generic term hydrostatic pressure (Chapter 20)
    blood-brain barrier (BBB)
    physiological barrier between the circulatory system and the central nervous system that establishes a privileged blood supply, restricting the flow of substances into the CNS (Chapter 12)
    blood–testis barrier
    tight junctions between Sertoli cells that prevent bloodborne pathogens from gaining access to later stages of spermatogenesis and prevent the potential for an autoimmune reaction to haploid sperm (Chapter 27)
    body
    mid-portion of the stomach (Chapter 23)
    body mass index (BMI)
    relative amount of body weight compared to the overall height; a BMI ranging from 18–24.9 is considered normal weight, 25–29.9 is considered overweight, and greater than 30 is considered obese (Chapter 24)
    body of the rib
    shaft portion of a rib (Chapter 7)
    body of uterus
    middle section of the uterus (Chapter 27)
    Bohr effect
    relationship between blood pH and oxygen dissociation from hemoglobin (Chapter 22)
    bolus
    mass of chewed food (Chapter 23)
    bond
    electrical force linking atoms (Chapter 2)
    bone
    hard, dense connective tissue that forms the structural elements of the skeleton (Chapter 6)
    bone marrow
    tissue found inside bones; the site of all blood cell differentiation and maturation of B lymphocytes (Chapter 21)
    bone marrow biopsy
    diagnostic test of a sample of red bone marrow (Chapter 18)
    bone marrow transplant
    treatment in which a donor’s healthy bone marrow with its stem cells replaces diseased or damaged bone marrow of a patient (Chapter 18)
    Bowman’s capsule
    cup-shaped sack lined by a simple squamous epithelium (parietal surface) and specialized cells called podocytes (visceral surface) that participate in the filtration process; receives the filtrate which then passes on to the PCTs (Chapter 25)
    Boyle’s law
    relationship between volume and pressure as described by the formula: P1V1 = P2V2 (Chapter 22)
    brachial artery
    continuation of the axillary artery in the brachium; supplies blood to much of the brachial region; gives off several smaller branches that provide blood to the posterior surface of the arm in the region of the elbow; bifurcates into the radial and ulnar arteries at the coronoid fossa (Chapter 20)
    brachial plexus
    nerve plexus associated with the lower cervical spinal nerves and first thoracic spinal nerve (Chapter 13)
    brachial vein
    deeper vein of the arm that forms from the radial and ulnar veins in the lower arm; leads to the axillary vein (Chapter 20)
    brachialis
    muscle deep to the biceps brachii that provides power in flexing the forearm. (Chapter 11)
    brachiocephalic artery
    single vessel located on the right side of the body; the first vessel branching from the aortic arch; gives rise to the right subclavian artery and the right common carotid artery; supplies blood to the head, neck, upper limb, and wall of the thoracic region (Chapter 20)
    brachiocephalic vein
    one of a pair of veins that form from a fusion of the external and internal jugular veins and the subclavian vein; subclavian, external and internal jugulars, vertebral, and internal thoracic veins lead to it; drains the upper thoracic region and flows into the superior vena cava (Chapter 20)
    brachioradialis
    muscle that can flex the forearm quickly or help lift a load slowly (Chapter 11)
    brain
    the large organ of the central nervous system composed of white and gray matter, contained within the cranium and continuous with the spinal cord (Chapter 12)
    brain case
    portion of the skull that contains and protects the brain, consisting of the eight bones that form the cranial base and rounded upper skull (Chapter 7)
    brain stem
    region of the adult brain that includes the midbrain, pons, and medulla oblongata and develops from the mesencephalon, metencephalon, and myelencephalon of the embryonic brain (Chapter 13)
    Braxton Hicks contractions
    weak and irregular peristaltic contractions that can occur in the second and third trimesters; they do not indicate that childbirth is imminent (Chapter 28)
    brevis
    short (Chapter 11)
    bridge
    portion of the external nose that lies in the area of the nasal bones (Chapter 22)
    broad ligament
    wide ligament that supports the uterus by attaching laterally to both sides of the uterus and pelvic wall (Chapter 27)
    Broca’s area
    region of the frontal lobe associated with the motor commands necessary for speech production and located only in the cerebral hemisphere responsible for language production, which is the left side in approximately 95 percent of the population (Chapter 13)
    Broca’s area
    region of the frontal lobe associated with the motor commands necessary for speech production (Chapter 14)
    Brodmann’s areas
    mapping of regions of the cerebral cortex based on microscopic anatomy that relates specific areas to functional differences, as described by Brodmann in the early 1900s (Chapter 13)
    bronchial artery
    systemic branch from the aorta that provides oxygenated blood to the lungs in addition to the pulmonary circuit (Chapter 20)
    bronchial bud
    structure in the developing embryo that forms when the laryngotracheal bud extends and branches to form two bulbous structures (Chapter 22)
    bronchial tree
    collective name for the multiple branches of the bronchi and bronchioles of the respiratory system (Chapter 22)
    bronchial vein
    drains the systemic circulation from the lungs and leads to the azygos vein (Chapter 20)
    bronchiole
    branch of bronchi that are 1 mm or less in diameter and terminate at alveolar sacs (Chapter 22)
    bronchoconstriction
    decrease in the size of the bronchiole due to contraction of the muscular wall (Chapter 22)
    bronchodilation
    increase in the size of the bronchiole due to relaxation of the muscular wall (Chapter 22)
    bronchus
    tube connected to the trachea that branches into many subsidiaries and provides a passageway for air to enter and leave the lungs (Chapter 22)
    bronchus-associated lymphoid tissue (BALT)
    lymphoid nodule associated with the respiratory tract (Chapter 21)
    brown adipose tissue
    highly vascularized fat tissue that is packed with mitochondria; these properties confer the ability to oxidize fatty acids to generate heat (Chapter 28)
    bruise
    localized bleeding under the skin due to damaged blood vessels (Chapter 18)
    brush border
    fuzzy appearance of the small intestinal mucosa created by microvilli (Chapter 23)
    brush border
    formed by microvilli on the surface of certain cuboidal cells; in the kidney it is found in the PCT; increases surface area for absorption in the kidney (Chapter 25)
    buccinator
    muscle that compresses the cheek (Chapter 11)
    buffer
    solution containing a weak acid or a weak base that opposes wide fluctuations in the pH of body fluids (Chapter 2)
    buffy coat
    thin, pale layer of leukocytes and platelets that separates the erythrocytes from the plasma in a sample of centrifuged blood (Chapter 18)
    bulbourethral glands
    (also, Cowper’s glands) glands that secrete a lubricating mucus that cleans and lubricates the urethra prior to and during ejaculation (Chapter 27)
    bulbus cordis
    portion of the primitive heart tube that will eventually develop into the right ventricle (Chapter 19)
    bundle of His
    (also, atrioventricular bundle) group of specialized myocardial conductile cells that transmit the impulse from the AV node through the interventricular septum; form the left and right atrioventricular bundle branches (Chapter 19)
    bursa
    connective tissue sac containing lubricating fluid that prevents friction between adjacent structures, such as skin and bone, tendons and bone, or between muscles (Chapter 9)

    C¶

    calcaneal tendon
    (also, Achilles tendon) strong tendon that inserts into the calcaneal bone of the ankle (Chapter 11)
    calcaneofibular ligament
    intrinsic ligament located on the lateral side of the ankle joint, between the calcaneus bone and lateral malleolus of the fibula; supports the talus bone at the ankle joint and resists excess inversion of the foot (Chapter 9)
    calcaneus
    heel bone; posterior, inferior tarsal bone that forms the heel of the foot (Chapter 8)
    calcitonin
    peptide hormone produced and secreted by the parafollicular cells (C cells) of the thyroid gland that functions to decrease blood calcium levels (Chapter 17)
    callus
    thickened area of skin that arises due to constant abrasion (Chapter 5)
    calmodulin
    regulatory protein that facilitates contraction in smooth muscles (Chapter 10)
    calorie
    amount of heat it takes to raise 1 kg (1000 g) of water by 1 °C (Chapter 24)
    calvaria
    (also, skullcap) rounded top of the skull (Chapter 7)
    calyces
    cup-like structures receiving urine from the collecting ducts where it passes on to the renal pelvis and ureter (Chapter 25)
    canaliculi
    (singular = canaliculus) channels within the bone matrix that house one of an osteocyte’s many cytoplasmic extensions that it uses to communicate and receive nutrients (Chapter 6)
    capacitance
    ability of a vein to distend and store blood (Chapter 20)
    capacitance vessels
    veins (Chapter 20)
    capacitation
    process that occurs in the female reproductive tract in which sperm are prepared for fertilization; leads to increased motility and changes in their outer membrane that improve their ability to release enzymes capable of digesting an oocyte’s outer layers (Chapter 28)
    capillary
    smallest of blood vessels where physical exchange occurs between the blood and tissue cells surrounded by interstitial fluid (Chapter 20)
    capillary bed
    network of 10–100 capillaries connecting arterioles to venules (Chapter 20)
    capillary hydrostatic pressure (CHP)
    force blood exerts against a capillary (Chapter 20)
    capitate
    from the lateral side, the third of the four distal carpal bones; articulates with the scaphoid and lunate proximally, the trapezoid laterally, the hamate medially, and primarily with the third metacarpal distally (Chapter 8)
    capitulum
    knob-like bony structure located anteriorly on the lateral, distal end of the humerus (Chapter 8)
    capsaicin
    molecule that activates nociceptors by interacting with a temperature-sensitive ion channel and is the basis for “hot” sensations in spicy food (Chapter 14)
    carbaminohemoglobin
    compound of carbon dioxide and hemoglobin, and one of the ways in which carbon dioxide is carried in the blood (Chapter 18)
    carbaminohemoglobin
    bound form of hemoglobin and carbon dioxide (Chapter 22)
    carbohydrate
    class of organic compounds built from sugars, molecules containing carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen in a 1-2-1 ratio (Chapter 2)
    carbonic anhydrase (CA)
    enzyme that catalyzes the reaction that causes carbon dioxide and water to form carbonic acid (Chapter 22)
    cardia
    (also, cardiac region) part of the stomach surrounding the cardiac orifice (esophageal hiatus) (Chapter 23)
    cardiac accelerator nerves
    preganglionic sympathetic fibers that cause the heart rate to increase when the cardiovascular center in the medulla initiates a signal (Chapter 15)
    cardiac cycle
    period of time between the onset of atrial contraction (atrial systole) and ventricular relaxation (ventricular diastole) (Chapter 19)
    cardiac muscle
    heart muscle, under involuntary control, composed of striated cells that attach to form fibers, each cell contains a single nucleus, contracts autonomously (Chapter 4)
    cardiac muscle
    striated muscle found in the heart; joined to one another at intercalated discs and under the regulation of pacemaker cells, which contract as one unit to pump blood through the circulatory system. Cardiac muscle is under involuntary control. (Chapter 10)
    cardiac notch
    depression in the medial surface of the superior lobe of the left lung where the apex of the heart is located (Chapter 19)
    cardiac notch
    indentation on the surface of the left lung that allows space for the heart (Chapter 22)
    cardiac output (CO)
    amount of blood pumped by each ventricle during one minute; equals HR multiplied by SV (Chapter 19)
    cardiac plexus
    paired complex network of nerve fibers near the base of the heart that receive sympathetic and parasympathetic stimulations to regulate HR (Chapter 19)
    cardiac reflexes
    series of autonomic reflexes that enable the cardiovascular centers to regulate heart function based upon sensory information from a variety of visceral sensors (Chapter 19)
    cardiac reserve
    difference between maximum and resting CO (Chapter 19)
    cardiac skeleton
    (also, skeleton of the heart) reinforced connective tissue located within the atrioventricular septum; includes four rings that surround the openings between the atria and ventricles, and the openings to the pulmonary trunk and aorta; the point of attachment for the heart valves (Chapter 19)
    cardiogenic area
    area near the head of the embryo where the heart begins to develop 18–19 days after fertilization (Chapter 19)
    cardiogenic cords
    two strands of tissue that form within the cardiogenic area (Chapter 19)
    cardiogenic shock
    type of shock that results from the inability of the heart to maintain cardiac output (Chapter 20)
    cardiomyocyte
    muscle cell of the heart (Chapter 19)
    cardiovascular center
    region in the medulla that controls the cardiovascular system through cardiac accelerator nerves and vasomotor nerves, which are components of the sympathetic division of the autonomic nervous system (Chapter 15)
    carotid canal
    zig-zag tunnel providing passage through the base of the skull for the internal carotid artery to the brain; begins anteromedial to the styloid process and terminates in the middle cranial cavity, near the posterior-lateral base of the sella turcica (Chapter 7)
    carotid canal
    opening in the temporal bone through which the internal carotid artery enters the cranium (Chapter 13)
    carotid sinuses
    small pockets near the base of the internal carotid arteries that are the locations of the baroreceptors and chemoreceptors that trigger a reflex that aids in the regulation of vascular homeostasis (Chapter 20)
    carpal bone
    one of the eight small bones that form the wrist and base of the hand; these are grouped as a proximal row consisting of (from lateral to medial) the scaphoid, lunate, triquetrum, and pisiform bones, and a distal row containing (from lateral to medial) the trapezium, trapezoid, capitate, and hamate bones (Chapter 8)
    carpal tunnel
    passageway between the anterior forearm and hand formed by the carpal bones and flexor retinaculum (Chapter 8)
    carpometacarpal joint
    articulation between one of the carpal bones in the distal row and a metacarpal bone of the hand (Chapter 8)
    carrier
    heterozygous individual who does not display symptoms of a recessive genetic disorder but can transmit the disorder to their offspring (Chapter 28)
    cartilage
    semi-rigid connective tissue found on the skeleton in areas where flexibility and smooth surfaces support movement (Chapter 6)
    cartilaginous joint
    joint at which the bones are united by hyaline cartilage (synchondrosis) or fibrocartilage (symphysis) (Chapter 9)
    catabolic hormones
    hormones that stimulate the breakdown of larger molecules (Chapter 24)
    catabolic reactions
    reactions that break down larger molecules into their constituent parts (Chapter 24)
    catabolism
    breaking down of more complex molecules into simpler molecules (Chapter 1)
    catagen
    transitional phase marking the end of the anagen phase of the hair growth cycle (Chapter 5)
    catalyst
    substance that increases the rate of a chemical reaction without itself being changed in the process (Chapter 2)
    cation
    atom with a positive charge (Chapter 2)
    cauda equina
    bundle of spinal nerve roots that descend from the lower spinal cord below the first lumbar vertebra and lie within the vertebral cavity; has the appearance of a horse's tail (Chapter 13)
    caudal
    describes a position below or lower than another part of the body proper; near or toward the tail (in humans, the coccyx, or lowest part of the spinal column); also referred to as inferior (Chapter 1)
    caudate
    nucleus deep in the cerebrum that is part of the basal nuclei; along with the putamen, it is part of the striatum (Chapter 13)
    caval opening
    opening in the diaphragm that allows the inferior vena cava to pass through; foramen for the vena cava (Chapter 11)
    cavernous sinus
    enlarged vein that receives blood from most of the other cerebral veins and the eye socket, and leads to the petrosal sinus (Chapter 20)
    cecum
    pouch forming the beginning of the large intestine (Chapter 23)
    celiac ganglion
    one of the collateral ganglia of the sympathetic system that projects to the digestive system (Chapter 15)
    celiac trunk
    (also, celiac artery) major branch of the abdominal aorta; gives rise to the left gastric artery, the splenic artery, and the common hepatic artery that forms the hepatic artery to the liver, the right gastric artery to the stomach, and the cystic artery to the gall bladder (Chapter 20)
    cell
    smallest independently functioning unit of all organisms; in animals, a cell contains cytoplasm, composed of fluid and organelles (Chapter 1)
    cell cycle
    life cycle of a single cell, from its birth until its division into two new daughter cells (Chapter 3)
    cell junction
    point of cell-to-cell contact that connects one cell to another in a tissue (Chapter 4)
    cell membrane
    membrane surrounding all animal cells, composed of a lipid bilayer interspersed with various molecules; also known as plasma membrane (Chapter 3)
    cellular respiration
    production of ATP from glucose oxidation via glycolysis, the Krebs cycle, and oxidative phosphorylation (Chapter 24)
    cementum
    bone-like tissue covering the root of a tooth (Chapter 23)
    central canal
    longitudinal channel in the center of each osteon; contains blood vessels, nerves, and lymphatic vessels; also known as the Haversian canal (Chapter 6)
    central canal
    hollow space within the spinal cord that is the remnant of the center of the neural tube (Chapter 13)
    central chemoreceptor
    one of the specialized receptors that are located in the brain that sense changes in hydrogen ion, oxygen, or carbon dioxide concentrations in the brain (Chapter 22)
    central nervous system (CNS)
    anatomical division of the nervous system located within the cranial and vertebral cavities, namely the brain and spinal cord (Chapter 12)
    central neuron
    specifically referring to the cell body of a neuron in the autonomic system that is located in the central nervous system, specifically the lateral horn of the spinal cord or a brain stem nucleus (Chapter 15)
    central sulcus
    surface landmark of the cerebral cortex that marks the boundary between the frontal and parietal lobes (Chapter 13)
    central tolerance
    B cell tolerance induced in immature B cells of the bone marrow (Chapter 21)
    central vein
    vein that receives blood from hepatic sinusoids (Chapter 23)
    centriole
    small, self-replicating organelle that provides the origin for microtubule growth and moves DNA during cell division (Chapter 3)
    centromere
    region of attachment for two sister chromatids (Chapter 3)
    centrosome
    cellular structure that organizes microtubules during cell division (Chapter 3)
    cephalic flexure
    curve in midbrain of the embryo that positions the forebrain ventrally (Chapter 13)
    cephalic phase
    (also, reflex phase) initial phase of gastric secretion that occurs before food enters the stomach (Chapter 23)
    cephalic vein
    superficial vessel in the upper arm; leads to the axillary vein (Chapter 20)
    cerebellum
    region of the adult brain connected primarily to the pons that developed from the metencephalon (along with the pons) and is largely responsible for comparing information from the cerebrum with sensory feedback from the periphery through the spinal cord (Chapter 13)
    cerebral aqueduct
    connection of the ventricular system between the third and fourth ventricles located in the midbrain (Chapter 13)
    cerebral cortex
    outermost layer of gray matter in the brain, where conscious perception takes place (Chapter 12)
    cerebral cortex
    outer gray matter covering the forebrain, marked by wrinkles and folds known as gyri and sulci (Chapter 13)
    cerebral hemisphere
    one half of the bilaterally symmetrical cerebrum (Chapter 13)
    cerebral peduncles
    segments of the descending motor pathway that make up the white matter of the ventral midbrain (Chapter 14)
    cerebrocerebellum
    lateral regions of the cerebellum; named for the significant input from the cerebral cortex (Chapter 16)
    cerebrospinal fluid (CSF)
    circulatory medium within the CNS that is produced by ependymal cells in the choroid plexus filtering the blood (Chapter 12)
    cerebrovascular accident (CVA)
    blockage of blood flow to the brain; also called a stroke (Chapter 20)
    cerebrum
    region of the adult brain that develops from the telencephalon and is responsible for higher neurological functions such as memory, emotion, and consciousness (Chapter 13)
    cervical curve
    posteriorly concave curvature of the cervical vertebral column region; a secondary curve of the vertebral column (Chapter 7)
    cervical enlargement
    region of the ventral (anterior) horn of the spinal cord that has a larger population of motor neurons for the greater number of and finer control of muscles of the upper limb (Chapter 14)
    cervical plexus
    nerve plexus associated with the upper cervical spinal nerves (Chapter 13)
    cervical vertebrae
    seven vertebrae numbered as C1–C7 that are located in the neck region of the vertebral column (Chapter 7)
    cervix
    elongate inferior end of the uterus where it connects to the vagina (Chapter 27)
    channel protein
    membrane-spanning protein that has an inner pore which allows the passage of one or more substances (Chapter 3)
    check reflex
    response to a release in resistance so that the contractions stop, or check, movement (Chapter 16)
    checkpoint
    progress point in the cell cycle during which certain conditions must be met in order for the cell to proceed to a subsequence phase (Chapter 3)
    chemical digestion
    enzymatic breakdown of food (Chapter 23)
    chemical energy
    form of energy that is absorbed as chemical bonds form, stored as they are maintained, and released as they are broken (Chapter 2)
    chemical synapse
    connection between two neurons, or between a neuron and its target, where a neurotransmitter diffuses across a very short distance (Chapter 12)
    chemokine
    soluble, long-range, cell-to-cell communication molecule (Chapter 21)
    chemoreceptor
    sensory receptor cell that is sensitive to chemical stimuli, such as in taste, smell, or pain (Chapter 14)
    chief cell
    gastric gland cell that secretes pepsinogen (Chapter 23)
    chief sensory nucleus
    component of the trigeminal nuclei that is found in the pons (Chapter 14)
    chloride shift
    facilitated diffusion that exchanges bicarbonate (HCO3–) with chloride (Cl–) ions (Chapter 22)
    cholecystokinin (CCK)
    hormone that stimulates the release of pancreatic lipase and the contraction of the gallbladder to release bile salts (Chapter 24)
    cholinergic
    synapse at which acetylcholine is released and binds to the nicotinic or muscarinic receptor (Chapter 15)
    cholinergic system
    neurotransmitter system of acetylcholine, which includes its receptors and the enzyme acetylcholinesterase (Chapter 12)
    chondrocytes
    cells of the cartilage (Chapter 4)
    chordae tendineae
    string-like extensions of tough connective tissue that extend from the flaps of the atrioventricular valves to the papillary muscles (Chapter 19)
    chorion
    membrane that develops from the syncytiotrophoblast, cytotrophoblast, and mesoderm; surrounds the embryo and forms the fetal portion of the placenta through the chorionic villi (Chapter 28)
    chorionic membrane
    precursor to the chorion; forms from extra-embryonic mesoderm cells (Chapter 28)
    chorionic villi
    projections of the chorionic membrane that burrow into the endometrium and develop into the placenta (Chapter 28)
    choroid
    highly vascular tissue in the wall of the eye that supplies the outer retina with blood (Chapter 14)
    choroid plexus
    specialized structure containing ependymal cells that line blood capillaries and filter blood to produce CSF in the four ventricles of the brain (Chapter 12)
    choroid plexus
    specialized structures containing ependymal cells lining blood capillaries that filter blood to produce CSF in the four ventricles of the brain (Chapter 13)
    chromaffin
    neuroendocrine cells of the adrenal medulla (Chapter 17)
    chromaffin cells

    chronic inflammation
    inflammation occurring for long periods of time (Chapter 21)
    chyle
    lipid-rich lymph inside the lymphatic capillaries of the small intestine (Chapter 21)

    large lipid-transport compound made up of triglycerides, phospholipids, cholesterol, and proteins (Chapter 23)

    chyme

    chymotrypsinogen
    proenzyme that is activated by trypsin into chymotrypsin (Chapter 24)
    cilia
    small appendage on certain cells formed by microtubules and modified for movement of materials across the cellular surface (Chapter 3)
    ciliary body
    smooth muscle structure on the interior surface of the iris that controls the shape of the lens through the zonule fibers (Chapter 14)
    ciliary ganglion
    one of the terminal ganglia of the parasympathetic system, located in the posterior orbit, axons from which project to the iris (Chapter 15)
    circadian rhythm
    internal perception of the daily cycle of light and dark based on retinal activity related to sunlight (Chapter 14)
    circle of Willis
    unique anatomical arrangement of blood vessels around the base of the brain that maintains perfusion of blood into the brain even if one component of the structure is blocked or narrowed (Chapter 13)
    circle of Willis
    (also, arterial circle) anastomosis located at the base of the brain that ensures continual blood supply; formed from branches of the internal carotid and vertebral arteries; supplies blood to the brain (Chapter 20)
    circular
    (also, sphincter) fascicles that are concentrically arranged around an opening (Chapter 11)
    circular fold
    (also, plica circulare) deep fold in the mucosa and submucosa of the small intestine (Chapter 23)
    circulatory shock
    also simply called shock; a life-threatening medical condition in which the circulatory system is unable to supply enough blood flow to provide adequate oxygen and other nutrients to the tissues to maintain cellular metabolism (Chapter 20)
    circumduction
    circular motion of the arm, thigh, hand, thumb, or finger that is produced by the sequential combination of flexion, abduction, extension, and adduction (Chapter 9)
    circumflex artery
    branch of the left coronary artery that follows coronary sulcus (Chapter 19)
    cisterna chyli
    bag-like vessel that forms the beginning of the thoracic duct (Chapter 21)
    citric acid cycle
    also called the Krebs cycle or the tricarboxylic acid cycle; converts pyruvate into CO2 and high-energy FADH2, NADH, and ATP molecules (Chapter 24)
    clasp-knife response
    sign of UMN disease when a patient initially resists passive movement of a muscle but will quickly release to a lower state of resistance (Chapter 16)
    class switching
    ability of B cells to change the class of antibody they produce without altering the specificity for antigen (Chapter 21)
    clavicle
    collarbone; elongated bone that articulates with the manubrium of the sternum medially and the acromion of the scapula laterally (Chapter 8)
    clavicular notch
    paired notches located on the superior-lateral sides of the sternal manubrium, for articulation with the clavicle (Chapter 7)
    cleavage
    form of mitotic cell division in which the cell divides but the total volume remains unchanged; this process serves to produce smaller and smaller cells (Chapter 28)
    cleavage furrow
    contractile ring that forms around a cell during cytokinesis that pinches the cell into two halves (Chapter 3)
    clitoris
    (also, glans clitoris) nerve-rich area of the vulva that contributes to sexual sensation during intercourse (Chapter 27)
    clonal anergy
    process whereby B cells that react to soluble antigens in bone marrow are made nonfunctional (Chapter 21)
    clonal deletion
    removal of self-reactive B cells by inducing apoptosis (Chapter 21)
    clonal expansion
    growth of a clone of selected lymphocytes (Chapter 21)
    clonal selection
    stimulating growth of lymphocytes that have specific receptors (Chapter 21)
    clone
    group of lymphocytes sharing the same antigen receptor (Chapter 21)
    closed reduction
    manual manipulation of a broken bone to set it into its natural position without surgery (Chapter 6)
    clotting
    also called coagulation; complex process by which blood components form a plug to stop bleeding (Chapter 4)
    clotting factors
    group of 12 identified substances active in coagulation (Chapter 18)
    coagulation
    formation of a blood clot; part of the process of hemostasis (Chapter 18)
    coccyx
    small bone located at inferior end of the adult vertebral column that is formed by the fusion of four coccygeal vertebrae; also referred to as the “tailbone” (Chapter 7)
    cochlea
    auditory portion of the inner ear containing structures to transduce sound stimuli (Chapter 14)
    cochlear duct
    space within the auditory portion of the inner ear that contains the organ of Corti and is adjacent to the scala tympani and scala vestibuli on either side (Chapter 14)
    codominance
    pattern of inheritance that corresponds to the equal, distinct, and simultaneous expression of two different alleles (Chapter 28)
    codon
    consecutive sequence of three nucleotides on an mRNA molecule that corresponds to a specific amino acid (Chapter 3)
    collagen fiber
    flexible fibrous proteins that give connective tissue tensile strength (Chapter 4)
    collateral ganglia

    colloid
    liquid mixture in which the solute particles consist of clumps of molecules large enough to scatter light (Chapter 2)
    colloid
    viscous fluid in the central cavity of thyroid follicles, containing the glycoprotein thyroglobulin (Chapter 17)
    colon
    part of the large intestine between the cecum and the rectum (Chapter 23)
    colony-stimulating factors (CSFs)
