    colostrum
    thick, yellowish substance secreted from a mother’s breasts in the first postpartum days; rich in immunoglobulins (Chapter 28)
    common bile duct
    structure formed by the union of the common hepatic duct and the gallbladder’s cystic duct (Chapter 23)
    common carotid artery
    blood vessel that branches off the aorta (or the brachiocephalic artery on the right) and supplies blood to the head and neck (Chapter 13)
    common carotid artery
    right common carotid artery arises from the brachiocephalic artery, and the left common carotid arises from the aortic arch; gives rise to the external and internal carotid arteries; supplies the respective sides of the head and neck (Chapter 20)
    common hepatic artery
    branch of the celiac trunk that forms the hepatic artery, the right gastric artery, and the cystic artery (Chapter 20)
    common hepatic duct
    duct formed by the merger of the two hepatic ducts (Chapter 23)
    common iliac artery
    branch of the aorta that leads to the internal and external iliac arteries (Chapter 20)
    common iliac vein
    one of a pair of veins that flows into the inferior vena cava at the level of L5; the left common iliac vein drains the sacral region; divides into external and internal iliac veins near the inferior portion of the sacroiliac joint (Chapter 20)
    common pathway
    final coagulation pathway activated either by the intrinsic or the extrinsic pathway, and ending in the formation of a blood clot (Chapter 18)
    compact bone
    dense osseous tissue that can withstand compressive forces (Chapter 6)
    complement
    enzymatic cascade of constitutive blood proteins that have antipathogen effects, including the direct killing of bacteria (Chapter 21)
    compliance
    degree to which a blood vessel can stretch as opposed to being rigid (Chapter 20)
    compound
    substance composed of two or more different elements joined by chemical bonds (Chapter 2)
    compressor urethrae
    deep perineal muscle in females (Chapter 11)
    computed tomography (CT)
    medical imaging technique in which a computer-enhanced cross-sectional X-ray image is obtained (Chapter 1)
    concentration
    number of particles within a given space (Chapter 2)
    concentration gradient
    difference in the concentration of a substance between two regions (Chapter 3)
    concentric contraction
    muscle contraction that shortens the muscle to move a load (Chapter 10)
    conceptus
    pre-implantation stage of a fertilized egg and its associated membranes (Chapter 28)
    conducting zone
    region of the respiratory system that includes the organs and structures that provide passageways for air and are not directly involved in gas exchange (Chapter 22)
    conduction
    transfer of heat through physical contact (Chapter 24)
    conduction aphasia
    loss of language function related to connecting the understanding of speech with the production of speech, without either specific function being lost (Chapter 16)
    conductive hearing
    hearing dependent on the conduction of vibrations of the tympanic membrane through the ossicles of the middle ear (Chapter 16)
    condylar process of the mandible
    thickened upward projection from posterior margin of mandibular ramus (Chapter 7)
    condyle
    oval-shaped process located at the top of the condylar process of the mandible (Chapter 7)
    condyloid joint
    synovial joint in which the shallow depression at the end of one bone receives a rounded end from a second bone or a rounded structure formed by two bones; found at the metacarpophalangeal joints of the fingers or the radiocarpal joint of the wrist; functionally classified as a biaxial joint (Chapter 9)
    cone photoreceptor
    one of the two types of retinal receptor cell that is specialized for color vision through the use of three photopigments distributed through three separate populations of cells (Chapter 14)
    conjugate gaze
    coordinated movement of the two eyes simultaneously in the same direction (Chapter 16)
    connective tissue
    type of tissue that serves to hold in place, connect, and integrate the body’s organs and systems (Chapter 4)
    connective tissue membrane
    connective tissue that encapsulates organs and lines movable joints (Chapter 4)
    connective tissue proper
    connective tissue containing a viscous matrix, fibers, and cells. (Chapter 4)
    constant region domain
    part of a lymphocyte antigen receptor that does not vary much between different receptor types (Chapter 21)
    continuous capillary
    most common type of capillary, found in virtually all tissues except epithelia and cartilage; contains very small gaps in the endothelial lining that permit exchange (Chapter 20)
    continuous conduction
    slow propagation of an action potential along an unmyelinated axon owing to voltage-gated Na+ channels located along the entire length of the cell membrane (Chapter 12)
    contractility
    ability to shorten (contract) forcibly (Chapter 10)
    contraction phase
    twitch contraction phase when tension increases (Chapter 10)
    contralateral
    word meaning “on the opposite side,” as in axons that cross the midline in a fiber tract (Chapter 14)
    control center
    compares values to their normal range; deviations cause the activation of an effector (Chapter 1)
    convection
    transfer of heat between the skin and air or water (Chapter 24)
    convergence
    in vision, the movement of the eyes so that they are both pointed at the same point in space, which increases for stimuli that are closer to the subject (Chapter 16)
    convergent
    fascicles that extend over a broad area and converge on a common attachment site (Chapter 11)
    coordination exam
    major section of the neurological exam that assesses complex, coordinated motor functions of the cerebellum and associated motor pathways (Chapter 16)
    coracobrachialis
    muscle that flexes and adducts the arm (Chapter 11)
    coracoclavicular ligament
    strong band of connective tissue that anchors the coracoid process of the scapula to the lateral clavicle; provides important indirect support for the acromioclavicular joint (Chapter 8)
    coracohumeral ligament
    intrinsic ligament of the shoulder joint; runs from the coracoid process of the scapula to the anterior humerus (Chapter 9)
    coracoid process
    short, hook-like process that projects anteriorly and laterally from the superior margin of the scapula (Chapter 8)
    corn
    type of callus that is named for its shape and the elliptical motion of the abrasive force (Chapter 5)
    cornea
    fibrous covering of the anterior region of the eye that is transparent so that light can pass through it (Chapter 14)
    corneal reflex
    protective response to stimulation of the cornea causing contraction of the orbicularis oculi muscle resulting in blinking of the eye (Chapter 14)
    corona radiata
    in an oocyte, a layer of granulosa cells that surrounds the oocyte and that must be penetrated by sperm before fertilization can occur (Chapter 28)
    coronal suture
    joint that unites the frontal bone to the right and left parietal bones across the top of the skull (Chapter 7)
    coronary arteries
    branches of the ascending aorta that supply blood to the heart; the left coronary artery feeds the left side of the heart, the left atrium and ventricle, and the interventricular septum; the right coronary artery feeds the right atrium, portions of both ventricles, and the heart conduction system (Chapter 19)
    coronary sinus
    large, thin-walled vein on the posterior surface of the heart that lies within the atrioventricular sulcus and drains the heart myocardium directly into the right atrium (Chapter 19)
    coronary sulcus
    sulcus that marks the boundary between the atria and ventricles (Chapter 19)
    coronary veins
    vessels that drain the heart and generally parallel the large surface arteries (Chapter 19)
    coronoid fossa
    depression on the anterior surface of the humerus above the trochlea; this space receives the coronoid process of the ulna when the elbow is maximally flexed (Chapter 8)
    coronoid process of the mandible
    flattened upward projection from the anterior margin of the mandibular ramus (Chapter 7)
    coronoid process of the ulna
    projecting bony lip located on the anterior, proximal ulna; forms the inferior margin of the trochlear notch (Chapter 8)
    corpus albicans
    nonfunctional structure remaining in the ovarian stroma following structural and functional regression of the corpus luteum (Chapter 27)
    corpus callosum
    large white matter structure that connects the right and left cerebral hemispheres (Chapter 13)
    corpus cavernosum
    (plural = corpora cavernosa) either of two columns of erectile tissue in the penis that fill with blood during an erection (Chapter 27)
    corpus luteum
    transformed follicle after ovulation that secretes progesterone (Chapter 27)
    corpus spongiosum
    column of erectile tissue in the penis that fills with blood during an erection and surrounds the penile urethra on the ventral portion of the penis (Chapter 27)
    corrugator supercilii
    prime mover of the eyebrows (Chapter 11)
    cortex
    in hair, the second or middle layer of keratinocytes originating from the hair matrix, as seen in a cross-section of the hair bulb (Chapter 5)
    cortical nephrons
    nephrons with loops of Henle that do not extend into the renal medulla (Chapter 25)
    cortical reaction
    following fertilization, the release of cortical granules from the oocyte’s plasma membrane into the zona pellucida creating a fertilization membrane that prevents any further attachment or penetration of sperm; part of the slow block to polyspermy (Chapter 28)
    cortico-ponto-cerebellar pathway
    projection from the cerebral cortex to the cerebellum by way of the gray matter of the pons (Chapter 16)
    corticobulbar tract
    connection between the cortex and the brain stem responsible for generating movement (Chapter 14)
    corticospinal tract

    cortisol
    glucocorticoid important in gluconeogenesis, the catabolism of glycogen, and downregulation of the immune system (Chapter 17)
    costal cartilage

    costal facet
    site on the lateral sides of a thoracic vertebra for articulation with the head of a rib (Chapter 7)
    costal groove

    costoclavicular ligament

    countercurrent multiplier system
    involves the descending and ascending loops of Henle directing forming urine in opposing directions to create a concentration gradient when combined with variable permeability and sodium pumping (Chapter 25)
    covalent bond

    coxal bone
    hip bone (Chapter 8)
    cranial
    describes a position above or higher than another part of the body proper; also referred to as superior (Chapter 1)
    cranial cavity

    cranial cavity
    interior space of the skull that houses the brain (Chapter 7)
    cranial nerve
    one of twelve nerves connected to the brain that are responsible for sensory or motor functions of the head and neck (Chapter 13)
    cranial nerve exam
    major section of the neurological exam that assesses sensory and motor functions of the cranial nerves and their associated central and peripheral structures (Chapter 16)
    cranial nerve ganglion

    craniosacral system
    alternate name for the parasympathetic division of the autonomic nervous system that is based on the anatomical location of central neurons in brain-stem nuclei and the lateral horn of the sacral spinal cord; also referred to as craniosacral outflow (Chapter 15)
    cranium
    skull (Chapter 7)
    creatine phosphate
    phosphagen used to store energy from ATP and transfer it to muscle (Chapter 10)
    cribriform plate
    small, flattened areas with numerous small openings, located to either side of the midline in the floor of the anterior cranial fossa; formed by the ethmoid bone (Chapter 7)
    cricoid cartilage
    portion of the larynx composed of a ring of cartilage with a wide posterior region and a thinner anterior region; attached to the esophagus (Chapter 22)
    crista galli
    small upward projection located at the midline in the floor of the anterior cranial fossa; formed by the ethmoid bone (Chapter 7)
    cross matching
    blood test for identification of blood type using antibodies and small samples of blood (Chapter 18)
    crown
    portion of tooth visible superior to the gum line (Chapter 23)
    cuboid
    tarsal bone that articulates posteriorly with the calcaneus bone, medially with the lateral cuneiform bone, and anteriorly with the fourth and fifth metatarsal bones (Chapter 8)
    cupula
    specialized structure within the base of a semicircular canal that bends the stereocilia of hair cells when the head rotates by way of the relative movement of the enclosed fluid (Chapter 14)
    cuspid
    (also, canine) pointed tooth used for tearing and shredding food (Chapter 23)
    cutaneous membrane
    skin; epithelial tissue made up of a stratified squamous epithelial cells that cover the outside of the body (Chapter 4)
    cuticle
    in hair, the outermost layer of keratinocytes originating from the hair matrix, as seen in a cross-section of the hair bulb (Chapter 5)
    cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP)

    cyclin
    one of a group of proteins that function in the progression of the cell cycle (Chapter 3)
    cyclin-dependent kinase (CDK)
    one of a group of enzymes associated with cyclins that help them perform their functions (Chapter 3)
    cystic artery
    branch of the common hepatic artery; supplies blood to the gall bladder (Chapter 20)
    cystic duct
    duct through which bile drains and enters the gallbladder (Chapter 23)
    cytoarchitecture
    study of a tissue based on the structure and organization of its cellular components; related to the broader term, histology (Chapter 16)
    cytokine
    soluble, short-range, cell-to-cell communication molecule (Chapter 21)
    cytokines
    class of proteins that act as autocrine or paracrine signaling molecules; in the cardiovascular system, they stimulate the proliferation of progenitor cells and help to stimulate both nonspecific and specific resistance to disease (Chapter 18)
    cytokinesis
    final stage in cell division, where the cytoplasm divides to form two separate daughter cells (Chapter 3)
    cytoplasm
    internal material between the cell membrane and nucleus of a cell, mainly consisting of a water-based fluid called cytosol, within which are all the other organelles and cellular solute and suspended materials (Chapter 3)
    cytoskeleton
    “skeleton” of a cell; formed by rod-like proteins that support the cell’s shape and provide, among other functions, locomotive abilities (Chapter 3)
    cytosol
    clear, semi-fluid medium of the cytoplasm, made up mostly of water (Chapter 3)
    cytotoxic T cells (Tc)
    T lymphocytes with the ability to induce apoptosis in target cells (Chapter 21)

    D¶

    Dalton’s law
    statement of the principle that a specific gas type in a mixture exerts its own pressure, as if that specific gas type was not part of a mixture of gases (Chapter 22)
    deciduous tooth
    one of 20 “baby teeth” (Chapter 23)
    decomposition reaction
    type of catabolic reaction in which one or more bonds within a larger molecule are broken, resulting in the release of smaller molecules or atoms (Chapter 2)
    decussate
    to cross the midline, as in fibers that project from one side of the body to the other (Chapter 14)
    deep
    describes a position farther from the surface of the body (Chapter 1)
    deep anterior compartment
    flexor pollicis longus, flexor digitorum profundus, and their associated blood vessels and nerves (Chapter 11)
    deep femoral artery
    branch of the femoral artery; gives rise to the lateral circumflex arteries (Chapter 20)
    deep femoral vein
    drains blood from the deeper portions of the thigh and leads to the femoral vein (Chapter 20)
    deep posterior compartment of the forearm
    (deep posterior extensor compartment of the forearm) the abductor pollicis longus, extensor pollicis brevis, extensor pollicis longus, extensor indicis, and their associated blood vessels and nerves (Chapter 11)
    deep tendon reflex
    another term for stretch reflex, based on the elicitation through deep stimulation of the tendon at the insertion (Chapter 16)
    deep transverse perineal
    deep perineal muscle in males (Chapter 11)
    defecation
    elimination of undigested substances from the body in the form of feces (Chapter 23)
    defensins
    antimicrobial proteins released from neutrophils and macrophages that create openings in the plasma membranes to kill cells (Chapter 18)
    deglutition
    swallowing (Chapter 11)
    deglutition
    three-stage process of swallowing (Chapter 23)
    dehydration
    state of containing insufficient water in blood and other tissues (Chapter 26)
    delayed hypersensitivity
    (type IV) T cell-mediated immune response against pathogens infiltrating interstitial tissues, causing cellular infiltrate (Chapter 21)
    delta cell
    minor cell type in the pancreas that secretes the hormone somatostatin (Chapter 17)
    deltoid
    shoulder muscle that abducts the arm as well as flexes and medially rotates it, and extends and laterally rotates it (Chapter 11)
    deltoid ligament
    broad intrinsic ligament located on the medial side of the ankle joint; supports the talus at the talocrural joint and resists excess eversion of the foot (Chapter 9)
    deltoid tuberosity
    roughened, V-shaped region located laterally on the mid-shaft of the humerus (Chapter 8)
    denaturation
    change in the structure of a molecule through physical or chemical means (Chapter 2)
    dendrite
    one of many branchlike processes that extends from the neuron cell body and functions as a contact for incoming signals (synapses) from other neurons or sensory cells (Chapter 12)
    dens
    bony projection (odontoid process) that extends upward from the body of the C2 (axis) vertebra (Chapter 7)
    dens
    tooth (Chapter 23)
    dense body
    sarcoplasmic structure that attaches to the sarcolemma and shortens the muscle as thin filaments slide past thick filaments (Chapter 10)
    dense connective tissue
    connective tissue proper that contains many fibers that provide both elasticity and protection (Chapter 4)
    dentin
    bone-like tissue immediately deep to the enamel of the crown or cementum of the root of a tooth (Chapter 23)
    dentition
    set of teeth (Chapter 23)

    dermal papilla
    (plural = dermal papillae) extension of the papillary layer of the dermis that increases surface contact between the epidermis and dermis (Chapter 5)
    dermis

    development
    changes an organism goes through during its life (Chapter 1)
    diabetes mellitus

    (also, emigration) process by which leukocytes squeeze through adjacent cells in a blood vessel wall to enter tissues (Chapter 18)

    lower number recorded when measuring arterial blood pressure; represents the minimal value corresponding to the pressure that remains during ventricular relaxation (Chapter 20)

    describes a trait that is expressed both in homozygous and heterozygous form (Chapter 28)

    downregulation
    decrease in the number of hormone receptors, typically in response to chronically excessive levels of a hormone (Chapter 17)

    ductus arteriosus
    shunt in the pulmonary trunk that diverts oxygenated blood back to the aorta (Chapter 28)

    (also, vas deferens) duct that transports sperm from the epididymis through the spermatic cord and into the ejaculatory duct; also referred as the vas deferens (Chapter 27)

    duodenal gland

    dura mater

    F¶

    facet

    G¶

    G cell
    gastrin-secreting enteroendocrine cell (Chapter 23)
    G protein
    guanosine triphosphate (GTP) hydrolase that physically moves from the receptor protein to the effector protein to activate the latter (Chapter 12)
    G protein
    protein associated with a cell membrane hormone receptor that initiates the next step in a second messenger system upon activation by hormone–receptor binding (Chapter 17)
    G protein–coupled receptor
    membrane protein complex that consists of a receptor protein that binds to a signaling molecule—a G protein—that is activated by that binding and in turn activates an effector protein (enzyme) that creates a second-messenger molecule in the cytoplasm of the target cell (Chapter 15)
    G0 phase
    phase of the cell cycle, usually entered from the G1 phase; characterized by long or permanent periods where the cell does not move forward into the DNA synthesis phase (Chapter 3)
    G1 phase
    first phase of the cell cycle, after a new cell is born (Chapter 3)
    G2 phase
    third phase of the cell cycle, after the DNA synthesis phase (Chapter 3)
    gait

    (also, greater pelvic cavity or false pelvis) broad space above the pelvic brim defined laterally by the fan-like portion of the upper ilium (Chapter 8)

    head of the metatarsal bone
    expanded, distal end of each metatarsal bone (Chapter 8)
    head of the radius
    disc-shaped structure that forms the proximal end of the radius; articulates with the capitulum of the humerus as part of the elbow joint, and with the radial notch of the ulna as part of the proximal radioulnar joint (Chapter 8)
    head of the rib
    posterior end of a rib that articulates with the bodies of thoracic vertebrae (Chapter 7)
    head of the ulna
    small, rounded distal end of the ulna; articulates with the ulnar notch of the distal radius, forming the distal radioulnar joint (Chapter 8)
    heart block
    interruption in the normal conduction pathway (Chapter 19)
    heart bulge
    prominent feature on the anterior surface of the heart, reflecting early cardiac development (Chapter 19)
    heart rate (HR)
    number of times the heart contracts (beats) per minute (Chapter 19)
    heart sounds
    sounds heard via auscultation with a stethoscope of the closing of the atrioventricular valves (“lub”) and semilunar valves (“dub”) (Chapter 19)
    heavy chain
    larger protein chain of an antibody (Chapter 21)
    helicase
    enzyme that functions to separate the two DNA strands of a double helix during DNA replication (Chapter 3)
    helper T cells (Th)
    T cells that secrete cytokines to enhance other immune responses, involved in activation of both B and T cell lymphocytes (Chapter 21)
    hemangioblasts
    embryonic stem cells that appear in the mesoderm and give rise to both angioblasts and pluripotent stem cells (Chapter 20)
    hematocrit
    (also, packed cell volume) volume percentage of erythrocytes in a sample of centrifuged blood (Chapter 18)
    hematopoiesis
    production of blood cells, which occurs in the red marrow of the bones (Chapter 6)
    hematopoietic stem cell
    type of pluripotent stem cell that gives rise to the formed elements of blood (hemocytoblast) (Chapter 18)
    heme
    red, iron-containing pigment to which oxygen binds in hemoglobin (Chapter 18)
    hemiazygos vein
    smaller vein complementary to the azygos vein; drains the esophageal veins from the esophagus and the left intercostal veins, and leads to the brachiocephalic vein via the superior intercostal vein (Chapter 20)
    hemisection
    cut through half of a structure, such as the spinal cord (Chapter 16)
    hemocytoblast
    hematopoietic stem cell that gives rise to the formed elements of blood (Chapter 18)
    hemoglobin
    oxygen-carrying compound in erythrocytes (Chapter 18)
    hemolysis
    destruction (lysis) of erythrocytes and the release of their hemoglobin into circulation (Chapter 18)
    hemolytic disease of the newborn (HDN)
    (also, erythroblastosis fetalis) disorder causing agglutination and hemolysis in an Rh+ fetus or newborn of an Rh− person (Chapter 18)
    hemophilia
    genetic disorder characterized by inadequate synthesis of clotting factors (Chapter 18)
    hemopoiesis
    production of the formed elements of blood (Chapter 18)
    hemopoietic growth factors
    chemical signals including erythropoietin, thrombopoietin, colony-stimulating factors, and interleukins that regulate the differentiation and proliferation of particular blood progenitor cells (Chapter 18)
    hemorrhage
    excessive bleeding (Chapter 18)
    hemorrhagic stroke
    disruption of blood flow to the brain caused by bleeding within the cranial vault (Chapter 16)
    hemosiderin
    protein-containing storage form of iron found in the bone marrow, liver, and spleen (Chapter 18)
    hemostasis
    physiological process by which bleeding ceases (Chapter 18)
    Henry’s law
    statement of the principle that the concentration of gas in a liquid is directly proportional to the solubility and partial pressure of that gas (Chapter 22)
    heparin
    short-acting anticoagulant stored in mast cells and released when tissues are injured, opposes prothrombin (Chapter 18)
    hepatic artery
    artery that supplies oxygenated blood to the liver (Chapter 23)
    hepatic artery proper
    branch of the common hepatic artery; supplies systemic blood to the liver (Chapter 20)
    hepatic lobule
    hexagonal-shaped structure composed of hepatocytes that radiate outward from a central vein (Chapter 23)
    hepatic portal system
    specialized circulatory pathway that carries blood from digestive organs to the liver for processing before being sent to the systemic circulation (Chapter 20)
    hepatic portal vein
    vein that supplies deoxygenated nutrient-rich blood to the liver (Chapter 23)
    hepatic sinusoid
    blood capillaries between rows of hepatocytes that receive blood from the hepatic portal vein and the branches of the hepatic artery (Chapter 23)
    hepatic vein
    drains systemic blood from the liver and flows into the inferior vena cava (Chapter 20)
    hepatic vein
    vein that drains into the inferior vena cava (Chapter 23)
    hepatocytes
    major functional cells of the liver (Chapter 23)
    hepatopancreatic ampulla
    (also, ampulla of Vater) bulb-like point in the wall of the duodenum where the bile duct and main pancreatic duct unite (Chapter 23)
    hepatopancreatic sphincter
    (also, sphincter of Oddi) sphincter regulating the flow of bile and pancreatic juice into the duodenum (Chapter 23)
    heterozygous
    having two different alleles for a given gene (Chapter 28)
    hexokinase
    cellular enzyme, found in most tissues, that converts glucose into glucose-6-phosphate upon uptake into the cell (Chapter 24)
    high endothelial venules
    vessels containing unique endothelial cells specialized to allow migration of lymphocytes from the blood to the lymph node (Chapter 21)
    hilum
    concave structure on the mediastinal surface of the lungs where blood vessels, lymphatic vessels, nerves, and a bronchus enter the lung (Chapter 22)
    hindbrain
    posterior region of the adult brain that develops from the rhombencephalon and includes the pons, medulla oblongata, and cerebellum (Chapter 13)
    hindmilk
    opaque, creamy breast milk delivered toward the end of a feeding; rich in fat; satisfies the infant’s appetite (Chapter 28)
    hinge joint
    synovial joint at which the convex surface of one bone articulates with the concave surface of a second bone; includes the elbow, knee, ankle, and interphalangeal joints; functionally classified as a uniaxial joint (Chapter 9)
    hip bone
    coxal bone; single bone that forms the pelvic girdle; consists of three areas, the ilium, ischium, and pubis (Chapter 8)
    hip joint
    joint located at the proximal end of the lower limb; formed by the articulation between the acetabulum of the hip bone and the head of the femur (Chapter 8)
    hippocampus
    gray matter deep in the temporal lobe that is very important for long-term memory formation (Chapter 13)
    histamine
    chemical compound released by mast cells in response to injury that causes vasodilation and endothelium permeability (Chapter 4)
    histamine
    vasoactive mediator in granules of mast cells and is the primary cause of allergies and anaphylactic shock (Chapter 21)
    histology
    microscopic study of tissue architecture, organization, and function (Chapter 4)
    histone
    family of proteins that associate with DNA in the nucleus to form chromatin (Chapter 3)
    hole
    opening or depression in a bone (Chapter 6)
    holocrine secretion
    release of a substance caused by the rupture of a gland cell, which becomes part of the secretion (Chapter 4)
    homeostasis
    steady state of body systems that living organisms maintain (Chapter 1)
    homologous
    describes two copies of the same chromosome (not identical), one inherited from each parent (Chapter 3)
    homozygous
    having two identical alleles for a given gene (Chapter 28)
    hook of the hamate bone
    bony extension located on the anterior side of the hamate carpal bone (Chapter 8)
    horizontal plate
    medial extension from the palatine bone that forms the posterior quarter of the hard palate (Chapter 7)
    hormone
    secretion of an endocrine organ that travels via the bloodstream or lymphatics to induce a response in target cells or tissues in another part of the body (Chapter 17)
    hormone receptor
    protein within a cell or on the cell membrane that binds a hormone, initiating the target cell response (Chapter 17)
    human chorionic gonadotropin (hCG)
    hormone that directs the corpus luteum to survive, enlarge, and continue producing progesterone and estrogen to suppress menses and secure an environment suitable for the developing embryo (Chapter 28)
    humeroradial joint
    articulation between the capitulum of the humerus and head of the radius (Chapter 9)
    humeroulnar joint
    articulation between the trochlea of humerus and the trochlear notch of the ulna; uniaxial hinge joint that allows for flexion/extension of the forearm (Chapter 9)
    humerus
    single bone of the upper arm (Chapter 8)
    hyaline cartilage
    most common type of cartilage, smooth and made of short collagen fibers embedded in a chondroitin sulfate ground substance (Chapter 4)
    hydrochloric acid (HCl)
    digestive acid secreted by parietal cells in the stomach (Chapter 23)
    hydrogen bond
    dipole-dipole bond in which a hydrogen atom covalently bonded to an electronegative atom is weakly attracted to a second electronegative atom (Chapter 2)
    hydrophilic
    describes a substance or structure attracted to water (Chapter 3)
    hydrophobic
    describes a substance or structure repelled by water (Chapter 3)
    hydrostatic pressure
    pressure exerted by a fluid against a wall, caused by its own weight or pumping force (Chapter 26)
    hydroxymethylglutaryl CoA (HMG CoA)
    molecule created in the first step of the creation of ketone bodies from acetyl CoA (Chapter 24)
    hymen
    membrane that covers part of the opening of the vagina (Chapter 27)
    hyoglossus
    muscle that originates on the hyoid bone to move the tongue downward and flatten it (Chapter 11)
    hyoid bone
    small, U-shaped bone located in upper neck that does not contact any other bone (Chapter 7)
    hypercalcemia
    condition characterized by abnormally high levels of calcium (Chapter 6)
    hypercalcemia
    abnormally increased blood levels of calcium (Chapter 26)
    hypercapnia
    abnormally elevated blood levels of CO2 (Chapter 26)
    hyperchloremia
    higher-than-normal blood chloride levels (Chapter 26)
    hyperextension
    excessive extension of joint, beyond the normal range of movement (Chapter 9)
    hyperflexia
    overly flexed joints (Chapter 16)
    hyperflexion
    excessive flexion of joint, beyond the normal range of movement (Chapter 9)
    hyperglycemia
    abnormally high blood glucose levels (Chapter 17)
    hyperkalemia
    higher-than-normal blood potassium levels (Chapter 26)
    hypernatremia
    abnormal increase in blood sodium levels (Chapter 26)
    hyperparathyroidism
    disorder caused by overproduction of PTH that results in abnormally elevated blood calcium (Chapter 17)
    hyperphosphatemia
    abnormally increased blood phosphate levels (Chapter 26)
    hyperplasia
    process in which one cell splits to produce new cells (Chapter 10)
    hyperpnea
    increased rate and depth of ventilation due to an increase in oxygen demand that does not significantly alter blood oxygen or carbon dioxide levels (Chapter 22)
    hypertension
    chronic and persistent blood pressure measurements of 140/90 mm Hg or above (Chapter 20)
    hyperthyroidism
    clinically abnormal, elevated level of thyroid hormone in the blood; characterized by an increased metabolic rate, excess body heat, sweating, diarrhea, weight loss, and increased heart rate (Chapter 17)
    hypertonia
    abnormally high muscle tone (Chapter 10)
    hypertonic
    describes a solution concentration that is higher than a reference concentration (Chapter 3)
    hypertrophic cardiomyopathy
    pathological enlargement of the heart, generally for no known reason (Chapter 19)
    hypertrophy
    addition of structural proteins to muscle fibers (Chapter 10)
    hyperventilation
    increased ventilation rate that leads to abnormally low blood carbon dioxide levels and high (alkaline) blood pH (Chapter 22)
    hypervolemia
    abnormally high levels of fluid and blood within the body (Chapter 20)
    hypoblast
    lower layer of cells of the embryonic disc that extend into the blastocoel to form the yolk sac (Chapter 28)
    hypocalcemia
    condition characterized by abnormally low levels of calcium (Chapter 6)
    hypocalcemia
    abnormally low blood levels of calcium (Chapter 26)
    hypocapnia
    abnormally low blood levels of CO2 (Chapter 26)
    hypochloremia
    lower-than-normal blood chloride levels (Chapter 26)
    hypodermis
    connective tissue connecting the integument to the underlying bone and muscle (Chapter 5)
    hypoglossal canal
    paired openings that pass anteriorly from the anterior-lateral margins of the foramen magnum deep to the occipital condyles (Chapter 7)
    hypoglossal nerve
    twelfth cranial nerve; responsible for contraction of muscles of the tongue (Chapter 13)
    hypokalemia
    abnormally decreased blood levels of potassium (Chapter 26)
    hyponatremia
    lower-than-normal levels of sodium in the blood (Chapter 26)
    hyponychium
    thickened layer of stratum corneum that lies below the free edge of the nail (Chapter 5)
    hypoparathyroidism
    disorder caused by underproduction of PTH that results in abnormally low blood calcium (Chapter 17)
    hypophosphatemia
    abnormally low blood phosphate levels (Chapter 26)
    hypophyseal (pituitary) fossa
    shallow depression on top of the sella turcica that houses the pituitary (hypophyseal) gland (Chapter 7)
    hypophyseal portal system
    network of blood vessels that enables hypothalamic hormones to travel into the anterior lobe of the pituitary without entering the systemic circulation (Chapter 17)
    hypothalamus
    major region of the diencephalon that is responsible for coordinating autonomic and endocrine control of homeostasis (Chapter 13)
    hypothalamus
    region of the diencephalon inferior to the thalamus that functions in neural and endocrine signaling (Chapter 17)
    hypothenar
    group of muscles on the medial aspect of the palm (Chapter 11)
    hypothenar eminence
    rounded contour of muscle at the base of the little finger (Chapter 11)
    hypothyroidism
    clinically abnormal, low level of thyroid hormone in the blood; characterized by low metabolic rate, weight gain, cold extremities, constipation, and reduced mental activity (Chapter 17)
    hypotonia
    abnormally low muscle tone caused by the absence of low-level contractions (Chapter 10)
    hypotonic
    describes a solution concentration that is lower than a reference concentration (Chapter 3)
    hypotonicity
    low muscle tone, a sign of LMN disease (Chapter 16)
    hypovolemia
    decrease in blood volume (Chapter 16)
    hypovolemia
    abnormally low levels of fluid and blood within the body (Chapter 20)
    hypovolemic shock
    type of circulatory shock caused by excessive loss of blood volume due to hemorrhage or possibly dehydration (Chapter 20)
    hypoxemia
    below-normal level of oxygen saturation of blood (typically <95 percent) (Chapter 18)
    hypoxia
    lack of oxygen supply to the tissues (Chapter 20)

    I¶

    IgA
    antibody whose dimer is secreted by exocrine glands, is especially effective against digestive and respiratory pathogens, and can pass immunity to an infant through breastfeeding (Chapter 21)
    IgD
    class of antibody whose only known function is as a receptor on naive B cells; important in B cell activation (Chapter 21)
    IgE
    antibody that binds to mast cells and causes antigen-specific degranulation during an allergic response (Chapter 21)
    IgG
    main blood antibody of late primary and early secondary responses; passed from carrier to unborn child via placenta (Chapter 21)
    IgM
    antibody whose monomer is a surface receptor of naive B cells; the pentamer is the first antibody made blood plasma during primary responses (Chapter 21)
    ileocecal sphincter
    sphincter located where the small intestine joins with the large intestine (Chapter 23)
    ileum
    end of the small intestine between the jejunum and the large intestine (Chapter 23)
    iliac crest
    curved, superior margin of the ilium (Chapter 8)
    iliac fossa
    shallow depression found on the anterior and medial surfaces of the upper ilium (Chapter 8)
    iliacus
    muscle that, along with the psoas major, makes up the iliopsoas (Chapter 11)
    iliococcygeus
    muscle that makes up the levator ani along with the pubococcygeus (Chapter 11)
    iliocostalis cervicis
    muscle of the iliocostalis group associated with the cervical region (Chapter 11)
    iliocostalis group
    laterally placed muscles of the erector spinae (Chapter 11)
    iliocostalis lumborum
    muscle of the iliocostalis group associated with the lumbar region (Chapter 11)
    iliocostalis thoracis
    muscle of the iliocostalis group associated with the thoracic region (Chapter 11)
    iliofemoral ligament
    intrinsic ligament spanning from the ilium of the hip bone to the femur, on the superior-anterior aspect of the hip joint (Chapter 9)
    iliopsoas group
    muscle group consisting of iliacus and psoas major muscles, that flexes the thigh at the hip, rotates it laterally, and flexes the trunk of the body onto the hip (Chapter 11)
    iliotibial tract
    muscle that inserts onto the tibia; made up of the gluteus maximus and connective tissues of the tensor fasciae latae (Chapter 11)
    ilium
    superior portion of the hip bone (Chapter 8)
    immediate hypersensitivity
    (type I) IgE-mediated mast cell degranulation caused by crosslinking of surface IgE by antigen (Chapter 21)
    immune system
    series of barriers, cells, and soluble mediators that combine to response to infections of the body with pathogenic organisms (Chapter 21)
    immunoglobulin
    protein antibody; occurs as one of five main classes (Chapter 21)
    immunoglobulins
    (also, antibodies or gamma globulins) antigen-specific proteins produced by specialized B lymphocytes that protect the body by binding to foreign objects such as bacteria and viruses (Chapter 18)
    immunological memory
    ability of the adaptive immune response to mount a stronger and faster immune response upon re-exposure to a pathogen (Chapter 21)
    implantation
    process by which a blastocyst embeds itself in the uterine endometrium (Chapter 28)
    inactivation gate
    part of a voltage-gated Na+ channel that closes when the membrane potential reaches +30 mV (Chapter 12)
    inactive proenzymes
    forms in which proteases are stored and released to prevent the inappropriate digestion of the native proteins of the stomach, pancreas, and small intestine (Chapter 24)
    incisor
    midline, chisel-shaped tooth used for cutting into food (Chapter 23)
    incomplete dominance
    pattern of inheritance in which a heterozygous genotype expresses a phenotype intermediate between dominant and recessive phenotypes (Chapter 28)
    incontinence
    loss of ability to control micturition (Chapter 25)
    incus
    (also, anvil) ossicle of the middle ear that connects the malleus to the stapes (Chapter 14)
    indirect pathway
    connections within the basal nuclei from the striatum through the globus pallidus external segment and subthalamic nucleus to the globus pallidus internal segment/substantia nigra pars compacta that result in inhibition of the thalamus to decrease cortical control of movement (Chapter 13)
    inferior
    describes a position below or lower than another part of the body proper; near or toward the tail (in humans, the coccyx, or lowest part of the spinal column); also referred to as caudal (Chapter 1)
    inferior angle of the scapula
    inferior corner of the scapula located where the medial and lateral borders meet (Chapter 8)
    inferior articular process
    bony process that extends downward from the vertebral arch of a vertebra that articulates with the superior articular process of the next lower vertebra (Chapter 7)
    inferior cerebellar peduncle (ICP)
    input to the cerebellum, largely from the inferior olive, that represents sensory feedback from the periphery (Chapter 16)
    inferior colliculus
    half of the midbrain tectum that is part of the brain stem auditory pathway (Chapter 13)
    inferior colliculus
    last structure in the auditory brainstem pathway that projects to the thalamus and superior colliculus (Chapter 14)
    inferior extensor retinaculum
    cruciate ligament of the ankle (Chapter 11)
    inferior gemellus
    muscle deep to the gluteus maximus on the lateral surface of the thigh that laterally rotates the femur at the hip (Chapter 11)
    inferior mesenteric artery
    branch of the abdominal aorta; supplies blood to the distal segment of the large intestine and rectum (Chapter 20)
    inferior mesenteric ganglion
    one of the collateral ganglia of the sympathetic system that projects to the digestive system (Chapter 15)
    inferior nasal concha
    one of the paired bones that project from the lateral walls of the nasal cavity to form the largest and most inferior of the nasal conchae (Chapter 7)
    inferior oblique
    extraocular muscle responsible for lateral rotation of the eye (Chapter 14)
    inferior olive
    nucleus in the medulla that is involved in processing information related to motor control (Chapter 13)
    inferior olive
    large nucleus in the medulla that receives input from sensory systems and projects into the cerebellar cortex (Chapter 16)
    inferior phrenic artery
    branch of the abdominal aorta; supplies blood to the inferior surface of the diaphragm (Chapter 20)
    inferior pubic ramus
    narrow segment of bone that passes inferiorly and laterally from the pubic body; joins with the ischial ramus to form the ischiopubic ramus (Chapter 8)
    inferior rectus
    extraocular muscle responsible for looking down (Chapter 14)
    inferior rotation
    movement of the scapula during upper limb adduction in which the glenoid cavity of the scapula moves in a downward direction as the medial end of the scapular spine moves in an upward direction (Chapter 9)
    inferior vena cava
    large systemic vein that returns blood to the heart from the inferior portion of the body (Chapter 19)
    inferior vena cava
    large systemic vein that drains blood from areas largely inferior to the diaphragm; empties into the right atrium (Chapter 20)
    inflammation
    response of tissue to injury (Chapter 4)
    inflammation
    basic innate immune response characterized by heat, redness, pain, and swelling (Chapter 21)
    infraglenoid tubercle
    small bump or roughened area located on the lateral border of the scapula, near the inferior margin of the glenoid cavity (Chapter 8)
    infrahyoid muscles
    anterior neck muscles that are attached to, and inferior to the hyoid bone (Chapter 11)
    infraorbital foramen
    opening located on anterior skull, below the orbit (Chapter 7)
    infraspinatus
    muscle that laterally rotates the arm (Chapter 11)
    infraspinous fossa
    broad depression located on the posterior scapula, inferior to the spine (Chapter 8)
    infratemporal fossa
    space on lateral side of skull, below the level of the zygomatic arch and deep (medial) to the ramus of the mandible (Chapter 7)
    infundibulum
    stalk containing vasculature and neural tissue that connects the pituitary gland to the hypothalamus (also called the pituitary stalk) (Chapter 17)
    infundibulum
    (of the uterine tube) wide, distal portion of the uterine tube terminating in fimbriae (Chapter 27)
    ingestion
    taking food into the GI tract through the mouth (Chapter 23)
    inguinal canal
    opening in abdominal wall that connects the testes to the abdominal cavity (Chapter 27)
    inhibin
    hormone secreted by the male and female gonads that inhibits FSH production by the anterior pituitary (Chapter 17)
    inhibitory postsynaptic potential (IPSP)
    graded potential in the postsynaptic membrane that is the result of hyperpolarization and makes an action potential less likely to occur (Chapter 12)
    initial segment
    first part of the axon as it emerges from the axon hillock, where the electrical signals known as action potentials are generated (Chapter 12)
    innate immune response
    rapid but relatively nonspecific immune response (Chapter 21)
    inner cell mass
    cluster of cells within the blastocyst that is fated to become the embryo (Chapter 28)
    inner ear
    structure within the temporal bone that contains the sensory apparati of hearing and balance (Chapter 14)
    inner segment
    in the eye, the section of a photoreceptor that contains the nucleus and other major organelles for normal cellular functions (Chapter 14)
    inner synaptic layer
    layer in the retina where bipolar cells connect to RGCs (Chapter 14)
    innermost intercostal
    the deepest intercostal muscles that draw the ribs together (Chapter 11)
    inorganic compound
    substance that does not contain both carbon and hydrogen (Chapter 2)
    inositol triphosphate (IP3)
    molecule that initiates the release of calcium ions from intracellular stores (Chapter 17)
    insertion
    end of a skeletal muscle that is attached to the structure (usually a bone) that is moved when the muscle contracts (Chapter 11)
    inspiration
    (also, inhalation) process that causes air to enter the lungs (Chapter 22)
    inspiratory capacity (IC)
    sum of the TV and IRV, which is the amount of air that can maximally be inhaled past a tidal expiration (Chapter 22)
    inspiratory reserve volume (IRV)
    amount of air that enters the lungs due to deep inhalation past the tidal volume (Chapter 22)
    insulin
    pancreatic hormone that enhances the cellular uptake and utilization of glucose, thereby decreasing blood glucose levels (Chapter 17)
    insulin
    hormone secreted by the pancreas that stimulates the uptake of glucose into the cells (Chapter 24)
    insulin-like growth factors (IGF)
    protein that enhances cellular proliferation, inhibits apoptosis, and stimulates the cellular uptake of amino acids for protein synthesis (Chapter 17)
    integral protein
    membrane-associated protein that spans the entire width of the lipid bilayer (Chapter 3)
    integration
    nervous system function that combines sensory perceptions and higher cognitive functions (memories, learning, emotion, etc.) to produce a response (Chapter 12)
    integumentary system
    skin and its accessory structures (Chapter 5)
    interatrial band
    (also, Bachmann’s bundle) group of specialized conducting cells that transmit the impulse directly from the SA node in the right atrium to the left atrium (Chapter 19)
    interatrial septum
    cardiac septum located between the two atria; contains the fossa ovalis after birth (Chapter 19)
    interaural intensity difference
    cue used to aid sound localization in the horizontal plane that compares the relative loudness of sounds at the two ears, because the ear closer to the sound source will hear a slightly more intense sound (Chapter 14)
    interaural time difference
    cue used to help with sound localization in the horizontal plane that compares the relative time of arrival of sounds at the two ears, because the ear closer to the sound source will receive the stimulus microseconds before the other ear (Chapter 14)
    intercalated cell
    specialized cell of the collecting ducts that secrete or absorb acid or bicarbonate; important in acid–base balance (Chapter 25)
    intercalated disc
    part of the sarcolemma that connects cardiac tissue, and contains gap junctions and desmosomes (Chapter 10)
    intercalated disc
    physical junction between adjacent cardiac muscle cells; consisting of desmosomes, specialized linking proteoglycans, and gap junctions that allow passage of ions between the two cells (Chapter 19)
    intercondylar eminence
    irregular elevation on the superior end of the tibia, between the articulating surfaces of the medial and lateral condyles (Chapter 8)
    intercondylar fossa
    deep depression on the posterior side of the distal femur that separates the medial and lateral condyles (Chapter 8)
    intercostal artery
    branch of the thoracic aorta; supplies blood to the muscles of the thoracic cavity and vertebral column (Chapter 20)
    intercostal muscles
    muscles that span the spaces between the ribs (Chapter 11)
    intercostal nerve
    systemic nerve in the thoracic cavity that is found between two ribs (Chapter 13)
    intercostal vein
    drains the muscles of the thoracic wall and leads to the azygos vein (Chapter 20)
    interferons
    early induced proteins made in virally infected cells that cause nearby cells to make antiviral proteins (Chapter 21)
    interleukins
    signaling molecules that may function in hemopoiesis, inflammation, and specific immune responses (Chapter 18)
    intermediate
    group of midpalmar muscles (Chapter 11)
    intermediate cuneiform
    middle of the three cuneiform tarsal bones; articulates posteriorly with the navicular bone, medially with the medial cuneiform bone, laterally with the lateral cuneiform bone, and anteriorly with the second metatarsal bone (Chapter 8)
    intermediate filament
    type of cytoskeletal filament made of keratin, characterized by an intermediate thickness, and playing a role in resisting cellular tension (Chapter 3)
    internal acoustic meatus
    opening into petrous ridge, located on the lateral wall of the posterior cranial fossa (Chapter 7)
    internal anal sphincter
    involuntary smooth muscle sphincter in the anal canal (Chapter 23)
    internal callus
    fibrocartilaginous matrix, in the endosteal region, between the two ends of a broken bone (Chapter 6)
    internal capsule
    segment of the descending motor pathway that passes between the caudate nucleus and the putamen (Chapter 14)
    internal carotid artery
    branch from the common carotid artery that enters the cranium and supplies blood to the brain (Chapter 13)
    internal carotid artery
    arises from the common carotid artery and begins with the carotid sinus; goes through the carotid canal of the temporal bone to the base of the brain; combines with branches of the vertebral artery forming the arterial circle; supplies blood to the brain (Chapter 20)
    internal elastic membrane
    membrane composed of elastic fibers that separates the tunica intima from the tunica media; seen in larger arteries (Chapter 20)
    internal iliac artery
    branch from the common iliac arteries; supplies blood to the urinary bladder, walls of the pelvis, external genitalia, and the medial portion of the femoral region; in females, also provide blood to the uterus and vagina (Chapter 20)
    internal iliac vein
    drains the pelvic organs and integument; formed from several smaller veins in the region; leads to the common iliac vein (Chapter 20)
    internal intercostal
    muscles the intermediate intercostal muscles that draw the ribs together (Chapter 11)
    internal jugular vein
    one of a pair of major veins located in the neck region that passes through the jugular foramen and canal, flows parallel to the common carotid artery that is more or less its counterpart; primarily drains blood from the brain, receives the superficial facial vein, and empties into the subclavian vein (Chapter 20)
    internal oblique
    flat, intermediate abdominal muscle with fascicles that run perpendicular to those of the external oblique (Chapter 11)
    internal respiration
    gas exchange that occurs at the level of body tissues (Chapter 22)
    internal root sheath
    innermost layer of keratinocytes in the hair follicle that surround the hair root up to the hair shaft (Chapter 5)
    internal thoracic artery
    (also, mammary artery) arises from the subclavian artery; supplies blood to the thymus, pericardium of the heart, and the anterior chest wall (Chapter 20)
    internal thoracic vein
    (also, internal mammary vein) drains the anterior surface of the chest wall and leads to the brachiocephalic vein (Chapter 20)
    internal urinary sphincter
    smooth muscle at the juncture of the bladder and urethra; relaxes as the bladder fills to allow urine into the urethra (Chapter 25)
    internodal pathways
    specialized conductile cells within the atria that transmit the impulse from the SA node throughout the myocardial cells of the atrium and to the AV node (Chapter 19)
    internuclear ophthalmoplegia
    deficit of conjugate lateral gaze because the lateral rectus muscle of one eye does not contract resulting from damage to the abducens nerve or the MLF (Chapter 16)
    interoceptor
    sensory receptor that is positioned to interpret stimuli from internal organs, such as stretch receptors in the wall of blood vessels (Chapter 14)
    interosseous border of the fibula
    small ridge running down the medial side of the fibular shaft; for attachment of the interosseous membrane between the fibula and tibia (Chapter 8)
    interosseous border of the radius
    narrow ridge located on the medial side of the radial shaft; for attachment of the interosseous membrane between the ulna and radius bones (Chapter 8)
    interosseous border of the tibia
    small ridge running down the lateral side of the tibial shaft; for attachment of the interosseous membrane between the tibia and fibula (Chapter 8)
    interosseous border of the ulna
    narrow ridge located on the lateral side of the ulnar shaft; for attachment of the interosseous membrane between the ulna and radius (Chapter 8)
    interosseous membrane
    wide sheet of fibrous connective tissue that fills the gap between two parallel bones, forming a syndesmosis; found between the radius and ulna of the forearm and between the tibia and fibula of the leg (Chapter 9)
    interosseous membrane of the forearm
    sheet of dense connective tissue that unites the radius and ulna bones (Chapter 8)
    interosseous membrane of the leg

    entire life cycle of a cell, excluding mitosis (Chapter 3)

    interstitial fluid hydrostatic pressure (IFHP)

    intertrochanteric crest

    intertubercular groove (sulcus)

    intervertebral foramen
    opening located between adjacent vertebrae for exit of a spinal nerve (Chapter 7)
    intestinal gland
    (also, crypt of Lieberkühn) gland in the small intestinal mucosa that secretes intestinal juice (Chapter 23)

    phase of gastric secretion that begins when chyme enters the intestine (Chapter 23)
    intorsion

    (intrapulmonary pressure) pressure of the air within the alveoli (Chapter 22)

    terminal ganglia of the parasympathetic system that are found within the walls of the target effector (Chapter 15)
    intrapleural pressure

    intrinsic factor

    intrinsic ligament
    ligament that is fused to or incorporated into the wall of the articular capsule of a synovial joint (Chapter 9)
    intrinsic muscles of the hand

    intrinsic muscles of the tongue
    muscles that originate out of, and insert into, other tissues within the tongue and control the shape of the tongue (Chapter 16)
    intrinsic pathway
    initial coagulation pathway that begins with vascular damage or contact with foreign substances, and results in the activation of the common pathway (Chapter 18)
    intron
    non-coding regions of a pre-mRNA transcript that may be removed during splicing (Chapter 3)
    inulin
    plant polysaccharide injected to determine GFR; is neither secreted nor absorbed by the kidney, so its appearance in the urine is directly proportional to its filtration rate (Chapter 25)
    inversion
    foot movement involving the intertarsal joints of the foot in which the bottom of the foot is turned toward the midline (Chapter 9)
    involution
    postpartum shrinkage of the uterus back to its pre-pregnancy volume (Chapter 28)
    ion
    atom with an overall positive or negative charge (Chapter 2)
    ionic bond
    attraction between an anion and a cation (Chapter 2)
    ionotropic receptor
    neurotransmitter receptor that acts as an ion channel gate, and opens by the binding of the neurotransmitter (Chapter 12)
    ipsilateral
    word meaning on the same side, as in axons that do not cross the midline in a fiber tract (Chapter 14)
    iris
    colored portion of the anterior eye that surrounds the pupil (Chapter 14)
    irregular bone
    bone of complex shape; protects internal organs from compressive forces (Chapter 6)
    ischemia
    insufficient blood flow to the tissues (Chapter 20)
    ischemic stroke
    disruption of blood flow to the brain because blood cannot flow through blood vessels as a result of a blockage or narrowing of the vessel (Chapter 16)
    ischial ramus
    bony extension projecting anteriorly and superiorly from the ischial tuberosity; joins with the inferior pubic ramus to form the ischiopubic ramus (Chapter 8)

    ischial tuberosity

    ischium
    posteroinferior portion of the hip bone (Chapter 8)
    isometric contraction
    muscle contraction that occurs with no change in muscle length (Chapter 10)
    isotonic
    describes a solution concentration that is the same as a reference concentration (Chapter 3)
    isotonic contraction
    muscle contraction that involves changes in muscle length (Chapter 10)
    isotope
    one of the variations of an element in which the number of neutrons differ from each other (Chapter 2)
    isovolumic contraction
    (also, isovolumetric contraction) initial phase of ventricular contraction in which tension and pressure in the ventricle increase, but no blood is pumped or ejected from the heart (Chapter 19)
    isovolumic ventricular relaxation phase
    initial phase of the ventricular diastole when pressure in the ventricles drops below pressure in the two major arteries, the pulmonary trunk, and the aorta, and blood attempts to flow back into the ventricles, producing the dicrotic notch of the ECG and closing the two semilunar valves (Chapter 19)
    isthmus
    narrow, medial portion of the uterine tube that joins the uterus (Chapter 27)

    jaundice

    joint

    joint cavity
    space enclosed by the articular capsule of a synovial joint that is filled with synovial fluid and contains the articulating surfaces of the adjacent bones (Chapter 9)

    juxtaglomerular apparatus (JGA)

    juxtaglomerular cell
    modified smooth muscle cells of the afferent arteriole; secretes renin in response to a drop in blood pressure (Chapter 25)
    juxtamedullary nephrons

    kinesthesia

    kinesthesia

    kinetochore
    region of a centromere where microtubules attach to a pair of sister chromatids (Chapter 3)
    knee joint
    joint that separates the thigh and leg portions of the lower limb; formed by the articulations between the medial and lateral condyles of the femur, and the medial and lateral condyles of the tibia (Chapter 8)
    Korotkoff sounds
    noises created by turbulent blood flow through the vessels (Chapter 20)
    Krebs cycle
    also called the citric acid cycle or the tricarboxylic acid cycle, converts pyruvate into CO2 and high-energy FADH2, NADH, and ATP molecules (Chapter 24)
    kyphosis
    (also, humpback or hunchback) excessive posterior curvature of the thoracic vertebral column region (Chapter 7)

    lymph

    network of lymphatic vessels, lymph nodes, and ducts that carries lymph from the tissues and back to the bloodstream. (Chapter 21)
    lymphatic trunks

    lymphocytes

    lymphoid nodules

    lymphoid stem cells

    lymphoma
    form of cancer in which masses of malignant T and/or B lymphocytes collect in lymph nodes, the spleen, the liver, and other tissues (Chapter 18)
    lysosome
    membrane-bound cellular organelle originating from the Golgi apparatus and containing digestive enzymes (Chapter 3)

    magnetic resonance imaging (MRI)

    NADH

    portion of the pharynx flanked by the conchae and oropharynx that serves as an airway (Chapter 22)
    natural killer (NK) cells

    cytotoxic lymphocyte of innate immune response (Chapter 21)
    navicular
    tarsal bone that articulates posteriorly with the talus bone, laterally with the cuboid bone, and anteriorly with the medial, intermediate, and lateral cuneiform bones (Chapter 8)

    necrosis
    accidental death of cells and tissues (Chapter 4)
    negative feedback
    homeostatic mechanism that tends to stabilize an upset in the body’s physiological condition by preventing an excessive response to a stimulus, typically as the stimulus is removed (Chapter 1)

    factors that negatively impact or lower heart contractility (Chapter 19)
    negative selection
    selection against thymocytes in the thymus that react with self-antigen (Chapter 21)
    neonatal hypothyroidism
    condition characterized by cognitive deficits, short stature, and other signs and symptoms in people born to people who were iodine-deficient during pregnancy (Chapter 17)
    nephrons
    functional units of the kidney that carry out all filtration and modification to produce urine; consist of renal corpuscles, proximal and distal convoluted tubules, and descending and ascending loops of Henle; drain into collecting ducts (Chapter 25)
    nerve
    cord-like bundle of axons located in the peripheral nervous system that transmits sensory input and response output to and from the central nervous system (Chapter 12)
    nerve plexus
    network of nerves without neuronal cell bodies included (Chapter 13)
    nervi vasorum
    small nerve fibers found in arteries and veins that trigger contraction of the smooth muscle in their walls (Chapter 20)
    nervous tissue
    type of tissue that is capable of sending and receiving impulses through electrochemical signals. (Chapter 4)
    net filtration pressure (NFP)
    force driving fluid out of the capillary and into the tissue spaces; equal to the difference of the capillary hydrostatic pressure and the blood colloidal osmotic pressure (Chapter 20)
    net filtration pressure (NFP)
    pressure of fluid across the glomerulus; calculated by taking the hydrostatic pressure of the capillary and subtracting the colloid osmotic pressure of the blood and the hydrostatic pressure of Bowman’s capsule (Chapter 25)
    neural crest
    tissue that detaches from the edges of the neural groove and migrates through the embryo to develop into peripheral structures of both nervous and non-nervous tissues (Chapter 13)
    neural fold
    elevated edge of the neural groove (Chapter 13)
    neural groove
    region of the neural plate that folds into the dorsal surface of the embryo and closes off to become the neural tube (Chapter 13)
    neural plate
    thickened layer of neuroepithelium that runs longitudinally along the dorsal surface of an embryo and gives rise to nervous system tissue (Chapter 13)
    neural tube
    precursor to structures of the central nervous system, formed by the invagination and separation of neuroepithelium (Chapter 13)
    neural tunic
    layer of the eye that contains nervous tissue, namely the retina (Chapter 14)
    neuraxis
    central axis to the nervous system, from the posterior to anterior ends of the neural tube; the inferior tip of the spinal cord to the anterior surface of the cerebrum (Chapter 13)
    neurogenic shock
    type of shock that occurs with cranial or high spinal injuries that damage the cardiovascular centers in the medulla oblongata or the nervous fibers originating from this region (Chapter 20)
    neuroglia
    supportive neural cells (Chapter 4)
    neurological exam
    clinical assessment tool that can be used to quickly evaluate neurological function and determine if specific parts of the nervous system have been affected by damage or disease (Chapter 16)
    neuromuscular junction (NMJ)
    synapse between the axon terminal of a motor neuron and the section of the membrane of a muscle fiber with receptors for the acetylcholine released by the terminal (Chapter 10)
    neuron
    excitable neural cell that transfer nerve impulses (Chapter 4)
    neuron
    neural tissue cell that is primarily responsible for generating and propagating electrical signals into, within, and out of the nervous system (Chapter 12)
    neuropeptide
    neurotransmitter type that includes protein molecules and shorter chains of amino acids (Chapter 12)
    neurotransmitter
    signaling chemical released by nerve terminals that bind to and activate receptors on target cells (Chapter 10)
    neurotransmitter
    chemical signal that is released from the synaptic end bulb of a neuron to cause a change in the target cell (Chapter 12)
    neurulation
    embryonic process that establishes the central nervous system (Chapter 28)
    neutralization
    inactivation of a virus by the binding of specific antibody (Chapter 21)
    neutron
    heavy subatomic particle having no electrical charge and found in the atom’s nucleus (Chapter 2)
    neutrophil
    phagocytic white blood cell recruited from the bloodstream to the site of infection via the bloodstream (Chapter 21)
    neutrophils
    granulocytes that stain with a neutral dye and are the most numerous of the leukocytes; especially active against bacteria (Chapter 18)
    nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NAD)
    coenzyme used to produce NADH (Chapter 24)
    nicotinic receptor
    type of acetylcholine receptor protein that is characterized by also binding to nicotine and is an ionotropic receptor (Chapter 12)

    receptor cell that senses pain stimuli (Chapter 14)

    gap between two myelinated regions of an axon, allowing for strengthening of the electrical signal as it propagates down the axon (Chapter 12)
    nonshivering thermogenesis
    process of breaking down brown adipose tissue to produce heat in the absence of a shivering response (Chapter 28)
    nonspecific channel
    channel that is not specific to one ion over another, such as a nonspecific cation channel that allows any positively charged ion across the membrane (Chapter 12)
    norepinephrine
    signaling molecule released as a neurotransmitter by most postganglionic sympathetic fibers as part of the sympathetic response, or as a hormone into the bloodstream from the adrenal medulla (Chapter 15)
    norepinephrine
    secondary catecholamine hormone secreted by the adrenal medulla in response to short-term stress; also called noradrenaline (Chapter 17)
    normal range
    range of values around the set point that do not cause a reaction by the control center (Chapter 1)
    notochord
    rod-like structure along dorsal side of the early embryo; largely disappears during later development but does contribute to formation of the intervertebral discs (Chapter 7)
    notochord
    rod-shaped, mesoderm-derived structure that provides support for growing fetus (Chapter 28)
    nuchal ligament
    expanded portion of the supraspinous ligament within the posterior neck; interconnects the spinous processes of the cervical vertebrae and attaches to the base of the skull (Chapter 7)
    nuclear envelope
    membrane that surrounds the nucleus; consisting of a double lipid-bilayer (Chapter 3)
    nuclear pore
    one of the small, protein-lined openings found scattered throughout the nuclear envelope (Chapter 3)
    nucleolus
    small region of the nucleus that functions in ribosome synthesis (Chapter 3)
    nucleosidase
    brush border enzyme that digests nucleotides (Chapter 23)
    nucleosome
    unit of chromatin consisting of a DNA strand wrapped around histone proteins (Chapter 3)
    nucleotide
    class of organic compounds composed of one or more phosphate groups, a pentose sugar, and a base (Chapter 2)
    nucleus
    cell’s central organelle; contains the cell’s DNA (Chapter 3)
    nucleus
    in the nervous system, a localized collection of neuron cell bodies that are functionally related; a “center” of neural function (Chapter 12)
    nucleus ambiguus
    brain-stem nucleus that contains neurons that project through the vagus nerve to terminal ganglia in the thoracic cavity; specifically associated with the heart (Chapter 15)
    nucleus cuneatus
    medullary nucleus at which first-order neurons of the dorsal column system synapse specifically from the upper body and arms (Chapter 14)
    nucleus gracilis
    medullary nucleus at which first-order neurons of the dorsal column system synapse specifically from the lower body and legs (Chapter 14)
    nucleus pulposus
    gel-like central region of an intervertebral disc; provides for padding, weight-bearing, and movement between adjacent vertebrae (Chapter 7)
    nutrient
    chemical obtained from foods and beverages that is critical to human survival (Chapter 1)
    nutrient foramen
    small opening in the middle of the external surface of the diaphysis, through which an artery enters the bone to provide nourishment (Chapter 6)

    disorder in children caused when abnormally low levels of GH result in growth retardation (Chapter 17)
    pituitary gland
    bean-sized organ suspended from the hypothalamus that produces, stores, and secretes hormones in response to hypothalamic stimulation (also called hypophysis) (Chapter 17)
    pivot joint
    synovial joint at which the rounded portion of a bone rotates within a ring formed by a ligament and an articulating bone; functionally classified as uniaxial joint (Chapter 9)
    placenta
    organ that forms during pregnancy to nourish the developing fetus; also regulates waste and gas exchange between mother and fetus (Chapter 28)
    placenta previa
    low placement of fetus within uterus causes placenta to partially or completely cover the opening of the cervix as it grows (Chapter 28)
    placentation
    formation of the placenta; complete by weeks 14–16 of pregnancy (Chapter 28)
    plane
    imaginary two-dimensional surface that passes through the body (Chapter 1)
    plane joint
    synovial joint formed between the flattened articulating surfaces of adjacent bones; functionally classified as a multiaxial joint (Chapter 9)
    plantar aponeurosis
    muscle that supports the longitudinal arch of the foot (Chapter 11)
    plantar arch
    formed from the anastomosis of the dorsalis pedis artery and medial and plantar arteries; branches supply the distal portions of the foot and digits (Chapter 20)
    plantar flexion
    foot movement at the ankle in which the heel is lifted off of the ground (Chapter 9)
    plantar group
    four-layered group of intrinsic foot muscles (Chapter 11)
    plantar reflex
    superficial reflex initiated by gentle stimulation of the sole of the foot (Chapter 16)
    plantar veins

    plantar venous arch
    formed from the plantar veins; leads to the anterior and posterior tibial veins through anastomoses (Chapter 20)
    plantaris
    muscle that runs obliquely between the gastrocnemius and the soleus (Chapter 11)
    plasma
    in blood, the liquid extracellular matrix composed mostly of water that circulates the formed elements and dissolved materials throughout the cardiovascular system (Chapter 18)
    plasma cell
    differentiated B cell that is actively secreting antibody (Chapter 21)
    plasma osmolality
    ratio of solutes to a volume of solvent in the plasma; plasma osmolality reflects a person’s state of hydration (Chapter 26)
    plasmin
    blood protein active in fibrinolysis (Chapter 18)
    platelet plug
    accumulation and adhesion of platelets at the site of blood vessel injury (Chapter 18)
    platelets
    (also, thrombocytes) one of the formed elements of blood that consists of cell fragments broken off from megakaryocytes (Chapter 18)
    pleura
    serous membrane that lines the pleural cavity and covers the lungs (Chapter 1)
    pleural cavity
    space between the visceral and parietal pleurae (Chapter 22)
    pleural fluid
    substance that acts as a lubricant for the visceral and parietal layers of the pleura during the movement of breathing (Chapter 22)
    plexus
    network of nerves or nervous tissue (Chapter 13)
    pluripotent
    describes the condition of being able to differentiate into a large variety of cell types (Chapter 3)
    pluripotent stem cell
    stem cell that derives from totipotent stem cells and is capable of differentiating into many, but not all, cell types (Chapter 18)
    pneumotaxic center
    network of neurons within the pons that inhibit the activity of the neurons in the dorsal respiratory group; controls rate of breathing (Chapter 22)
    podocytes
    cells forming finger-like processes; form the visceral layer of Bowman’s capsule; pedicels of the podocytes interdigitate to form a filtration membrane (Chapter 25)
    polar body
    smaller cell produced during the process of meiosis in oogenesis (Chapter 27)
    polar molecule
    molecule with regions that have opposite charges resulting from uneven numbers of electrons in the nuclei of the atoms participating in the covalent bond (Chapter 2)
    pollex
    (also, thumb) digit 1 of the hand (Chapter 8)
    polyclonal response

    polymorphonuclear

    polypeptide

    polyribosome

    polysaccharide
    compound consisting of more than two carbohydrate monomers bonded by dehydration synthesis via glycosidic bonds (Chapter 2)
    polysaccharides

    polyspermy
    penetration of an oocyte by more than one sperm (Chapter 28)
    polyuria
    urine production in excess of 2.5 L/day; may be caused by diabetes insipidus, diabetes mellitus, or excessive use of diuretics (Chapter 25)
    popliteal artery
    continuation of the femoral artery posterior to the knee; branches into the anterior and posterior tibial arteries (Chapter 20)
    popliteal fossa
    diamond-shaped space at the back of the knee (Chapter 11)
    popliteal vein
    continuation of the femoral vein behind the knee; drains the region behind the knee and forms from the fusion of the fibular and anterior and posterior tibial veins (Chapter 20)
    popliteus
    muscle that flexes the leg at the knee and creates the floor of the popliteal fossa (Chapter 11)
    porta hepatis
    “gateway to the liver” where the hepatic artery and hepatic portal vein enter the liver (Chapter 23)
    portal triad
    bile duct, hepatic artery branch, and hepatic portal vein branch (Chapter 23)
    positive chemotaxis
    process in which a cell is attracted to move in the direction of chemical stimuli (Chapter 18)
    positive feedback
    mechanism that intensifies a change in the body’s physiological condition in response to a stimulus (Chapter 1)
    positive inotropic factors

    positive selection
    selection of thymocytes within the thymus that interact with self, but not non-self, MHC molecules (Chapter 21)
    positron emission tomography (PET)
    medical imaging technique in which radiopharmaceuticals are traced to reveal metabolic and physiological functions in tissues (Chapter 1)
    postabsorptive state
    also called the fasting state; the metabolic state occurring after digestion when food is no longer the body’s source of energy and it must rely on stored glycogen (Chapter 24)
    postcentral gyrus
    ridge just posterior to the central sulcus, in the parietal lobe, where somatosensory processing initially takes place in the cerebrum (Chapter 13)
    posterior
    describes the back or direction toward the back of the body; also referred to as dorsal (Chapter 1)
    posterior (dorsal) sacral foramen

    posterior arch

    posterior cardiac vein
