    posterior cavity
    posterior body cavity that houses the brain and spinal cord; also referred to as dorsal cavity (Chapter 1)
    posterior cerebral artery
    branch of the basilar artery that forms a portion of the posterior segment of the arterial circle; supplies blood to the posterior portion of the cerebrum and brain stem (Chapter 20)
    posterior columns
    white matter of the spinal cord that lies between the posterior horns of the gray matter, sometimes referred to as the dorsal column; composed of axons of ascending tracts that carry sensory information up to the brain (Chapter 13)
    posterior communicating artery
    branch of the posterior cerebral artery that forms part of the posterior portion of the arterial circle; supplies blood to the brain (Chapter 20)
    posterior compartment of the leg
    region that includes the superficial gastrocnemius, soleus, and plantaris, and the deep popliteus, flexor digitorum longus, flexor hallucis longus, and tibialis posterior (Chapter 11)
    posterior compartment of the thigh
    region that includes muscles that flex the leg and extend the thigh (Chapter 11)
    posterior cranial fossa
    deepest and most posterior cranial fossa; extends from the petrous ridge to the occipital bone (Chapter 7)
    posterior cruciate ligament
    intracapsular ligament of the knee; extends from the posterior, superior surface of the tibia to the inner aspect of the medial condyle of the femur; prevents anterior displacement of the femur when the knee is flexed and weight bearing (Chapter 9)
    posterior horn
    gray matter region of the spinal cord in which sensory input arrives, sometimes referred to as the dorsal horn (Chapter 13)
    posterior inferior iliac spine
    small, bony projection located at the inferior margin of the auricular surface on the posterior ilium (Chapter 8)
    posterior interventricular artery
    (also, posterior descending artery) branch of the right coronary artery that runs along the posterior portion of the interventricular sulcus toward the apex of the heart and gives rise to branches that supply the interventricular septum and portions of both ventricles (Chapter 19)
    posterior interventricular sulcus
    sulcus located between the left and right ventricles on the posterior surface of the heart (Chapter 19)
    posterior longitudinal ligament
    ligament that runs the length of the vertebral column, uniting the posterior sides of the vertebral bodies (Chapter 7)
    posterior median sulcus
    midline feature of the posterior spinal cord, marking the separation between right and left sides of the cord (Chapter 13)
    posterior sacroiliac ligament
    strong ligament spanning the sacrum and ilium of the hip bone that supports the posterior side of the sacroiliac joint (Chapter 8)
    posterior scalene
    smallest scalene muscle, located posterior to the middle scalene (Chapter 11)
    posterior superior iliac spine
    rounded, posterior end of the iliac crest (Chapter 8)
    posterior talofibular ligament
    intrinsic ligament located on the lateral side of the ankle joint, between the talus bone and lateral malleolus of the fibula; supports the talus at the talocrural joint and resists excess inversion of the foot (Chapter 9)
    posterior tibial artery
    branch from the popliteal artery that gives rise to the fibular or peroneal artery; supplies blood to the posterior tibial region (Chapter 20)
    posterior tibial vein
    forms from the dorsal venous arch; drains the area near the posterior surface of the tibia and leads to the popliteal vein (Chapter 20)
    posterolateral sulcus
    feature of the posterior spinal cord marking the entry of posterior nerve roots and the separation between the posterior and lateral columns of the white matter (Chapter 13)
    postganglionic fiber
    axon from a ganglionic neuron in the autonomic nervous system that projects to and synapses with the target effector; sometimes referred to as a postganglionic neuron (Chapter 15)
    postsynaptic potential (PSP)
    graded potential in the postsynaptic membrane caused by the binding of neurotransmitter to protein receptors (Chapter 12)
    potential energy
    stored energy matter possesses because of the positioning or structure of its components (Chapter 2)
    power stroke
    action of myosin pulling actin inward (toward the M line) (Chapter 10)
    PP cell
    minor cell type in the pancreas that secretes the hormone pancreatic polypeptide (Chapter 17)
    praxis
    in a neurological exam, the act of doing something using ready knowledge or skills in response to verbal instruction (Chapter 16)
    precapillary sphincters
    circular rings of smooth muscle that surround the entrance to a capillary and regulate blood flow into that capillary (Chapter 20)
    precentral gyrus
    ridge just anterior to the central sulcus, in the frontal lobe, the primary motor cortex is located (Chapter 13)
    precentral gyrus of the frontal cortex
    region of the cerebral cortex responsible for generating motor commands, where the upper motor neuron cell body is located (Chapter 12)
    prefrontal lobe
    specific region of the frontal lobe anterior to the more specific motor function areas, which can be related to the early planning of movements and intentions to the point of being personality-type functions (Chapter 13)
    preganglionic fiber
    axon from a central neuron in the autonomic nervous system that projects to and synapses with a ganglionic neuron; sometimes referred to as a preganglionic neuron (Chapter 15)
    preload
    (also, end diastolic volume) amount of blood in the ventricles at the end of atrial systole just prior to ventricular contraction (Chapter 19)
    premolar
    (also, bicuspid) transitional tooth used for mastication, crushing, and grinding food (Chapter 23)
    premotor area
    region of the frontal lobe responsible for planning movements that will be executed through the primary motor cortex (Chapter 13)
    premotor cortex
    cortical area anterior to the primary motor cortex that is responsible for planning movements (Chapter 14)
    prepotential depolarization
    (also, spontaneous depolarization) mechanism that accounts for the autorhythmic property of cardiac muscle; the membrane potential increases as sodium ions diffuse through the always-open sodium ion channels and causes the electrical potential to rise (Chapter 19)
    prepuce
    (also, foreskin) flap of skin that forms a collar around, and thus protects and lubricates, the glans penis; also referred as the foreskin (Chapter 27)
    pressure
    force exerted by a substance in contact with another substance (Chapter 1)
    prevertebral ganglia
    autonomic ganglia that are anterior to the vertebral column and functionally related to the sympathetic chain ganglia (Chapter 13)
    primary adaptive response
    immune system’s response to the first exposure to a pathogen (Chapter 21)
    primary curve
    anteriorly concave curvatures of the thoracic and sacrococcygeal regions that are retained from the original fetal curvature of the vertebral column (Chapter 7)
    primary follicles
    ovarian follicles with a primary oocyte and one layer of cuboidal granulosa cells (Chapter 27)
    primary lymphoid organ
    site where lymphocytes mature and proliferate; red bone marrow and thymus gland (Chapter 21)
    primary ossification center
    region, deep in the periosteal collar, where bone development starts during endochondral ossification (Chapter 6)
    primary sensory cortex
    region of the cerebral cortex that initially receives sensory input from an ascending pathway from the thalamus and begins the processing that will result in conscious perception of that modality (Chapter 14)
    primary union
    condition of a wound where the wound edges are close enough to be brought together and fastened if necessary, allowing quicker and more thorough healing (Chapter 4)
    primary vesicle
    initial enlargements of the anterior neural tube during embryonic development that develop into the forebrain, midbrain, and hindbrain (Chapter 13)
    prime mover
    (also, agonist) principle muscle involved in an action (Chapter 11)
    primitive atrium
    portion of the primitive heart tube that eventually becomes the anterior portions of both the right and left atria, and the two auricles (Chapter 19)
    primitive heart tube
    singular tubular structure that forms from the fusion of the two endocardial tubes (Chapter 19)
    primitive streak
    indentation along the dorsal surface of the epiblast through which cells migrate to form the endoderm and mesoderm during gastrulation (Chapter 28)
    primitive ventricle
    portion of the primitive heart tube that eventually forms the left ventricle (Chapter 19)
    primordial follicles
    least developed ovarian follicles that consist of a single oocyte and a single layer of flat (squamous) granulosa cells (Chapter 27)
    principal cell
    found in collecting ducts and possess channels for the recovery or loss of sodium and potassium; under the control of aldosterone; also have aquaporin channels under ADH control to regulate recovery of water (Chapter 25)
    procedural memory
    memory of how to perform a specific task (Chapter 16)
    process
    in cells, an extension of a cell body; in the case of neurons, this includes the axon and dendrites (Chapter 12)
    product
    one or more substances produced by a chemical reaction (Chapter 2)
    progesterone
    predominantly female sex hormone important in regulating the female reproductive cycle and the maintenance of pregnancy (Chapter 17)
    projection
    bone markings where part of the surface sticks out above the rest of the surface, where tendons and ligaments attach (Chapter 6)
    prolactin
    pituitary hormone that establishes and maintains the supply of breast milk; also important for the mobilization of maternal micronutrients for breast milk (Chapter 28)
    prolactin (PRL)
    anterior pituitary hormone that promotes development of the mammary glands and the production of breast milk (Chapter 17)
    proliferative phase
    phase of the menstrual cycle in which the endometrium proliferates (Chapter 27)
    proliferative zone
    region of the epiphyseal plate that makes new chondrocytes to replace those that die at the diaphyseal end of the plate and contributes to longitudinal growth of the epiphyseal plate (Chapter 6)
    promoter
    region of DNA that signals transcription to begin at that site within the gene (Chapter 3)
    pronated position
    forearm position in which the palm faces backward (Chapter 9)
    pronation
    forearm motion that moves the palm of the hand from the palm forward to the palm backward position (Chapter 9)
    pronator drift
    sign of contralateral corticospinal lesion when the one arm will drift into a pronated position when held straight out with the palms facing upward (Chapter 16)
    pronator quadratus
    pronator that originates on the ulna and inserts on the radius (Chapter 11)
    pronator teres
    pronator that originates on the humerus and inserts on the radius (Chapter 11)
    prone
    face down (Chapter 1)
    propagation
    movement of an action potential along the length of an axon (Chapter 12)
    prophase
    first stage of mitosis (and meiosis), characterized by breakdown of the nuclear envelope and condensing of the chromatin to form chromosomes (Chapter 3)
    proprioception
    general sensory perceptions providing information about location and movement of body parts; the “sense of the self” (Chapter 13)
    proprioception
    sense of position and movement of the body (Chapter 14)
    proprioceptor
    receptor cell that senses changes in the position and kinesthetic aspects of the body (Chapter 14)
    propulsion
    voluntary process of swallowing and the involuntary process of peristalsis that moves food through the digestive tract (Chapter 23)
    prosencephalon
    primary vesicle of the embryonic brain that develops into the forebrain, which includes the cerebrum and diencephalon (Chapter 13)
    prostaglandin
    lipid compound derived from fatty acid chains and important in regulating several body processes (Chapter 2)
    prostate gland
    doughnut-shaped gland at the base of the bladder surrounding the urethra and contributing fluid to semen during ejaculation (Chapter 27)
    protein
    class of organic compounds that are composed of many amino acids linked together by peptide bonds (Chapter 2)
    protein kinase
    enzyme that initiates a phosphorylation cascade upon activation (Chapter 17)
    proteolysis
    process of breaking proteins into smaller peptides (Chapter 24)
    proteome
    full complement of proteins produced by a cell (determined by the cell’s specific gene expression) (Chapter 3)
    proton
    heavy subatomic particle having a positive charge and found in the atom’s nucleus (Chapter 2)
    protraction
    anterior motion of the scapula or mandible (Chapter 9)
    proximal
    describes a position nearer to the point of attachment or the trunk of the body (Chapter 1)
    proximal convoluted tubules (PCTs)
    tortuous tubules receiving filtrate from Bowman’s capsule; most active part of the nephron in reabsorption and secretion (Chapter 25)
    proximal radioulnar joint
    articulation formed by the radial notch of the ulna and the head of the radius (Chapter 8)
    proximal radioulnar joint
    articulation between head of radius and radial notch of ulna; uniaxial pivot joint that allows for rotation of radius during pronation/supination of forearm (Chapter 9)
    proximal tibiofibular joint
    articulation between the head of the fibula and the inferior aspect of the lateral condyle of the tibia (Chapter 8)
    pseudostratified columnar epithelium
    tissue that consists of a single layer of irregularly shaped and sized cells that give the appearance of multiple layers; found in ducts of certain glands and the upper respiratory tract (Chapter 4)
    psoas major
    muscle that, along with the iliacus, makes up the iliopsoas (Chapter 11)
    psychoneuroimmunology
    study of the connections between the immune, nervous, and endocrine systems (Chapter 21)
    pterion
    H-shaped suture junction region that unites the frontal, parietal, temporal, and sphenoid bones on the lateral side of the skull (Chapter 7)
    puberty
    life stage during which a male or female adolescent becomes anatomically and physiologically capable of reproduction (Chapter 27)
    pubic arch
    bony structure formed by the pubic symphysis, and the bodies and inferior pubic rami of the right and left pubic bones (Chapter 8)
    pubic body
    enlarged, medial portion of the pubis region of the hip bone (Chapter 8)
    pubic symphysis
    joint formed by the articulation between the pubic bodies of the right and left hip bones (Chapter 8)
    pubic tubercle
    small bump located on the superior aspect of the pubic body (Chapter 8)
    pubis
    anterior portion of the hip bone (Chapter 8)
    pubococcygeus
    muscle that makes up the levator ani along with the iliococcygeus (Chapter 11)
    pubofemoral ligament
    intrinsic ligament spanning from the pubis of the hip bone to the femur, on the anterior-inferior aspect of the hip joint (Chapter 9)
    pulmonary arteries
    left and right branches of the pulmonary trunk that carry deoxygenated blood from the heart to each of the lungs (Chapter 19)
    pulmonary artery
    one of two branches, left and right, that divides off from the pulmonary trunk and leads to smaller arterioles and eventually to the pulmonary capillaries (Chapter 20)
    pulmonary artery
    artery that arises from the pulmonary trunk and carries deoxygenated, arterial blood to the alveoli (Chapter 22)
    pulmonary capillaries
    capillaries surrounding the alveoli of the lungs where gas exchange occurs: carbon dioxide exits the blood and oxygen enters (Chapter 19)
    pulmonary circuit
    blood flow to and from the lungs (Chapter 19)
    pulmonary circuit
    system of blood vessels that provide gas exchange via a network of arteries, veins, and capillaries that run from the heart, through the body, and back to the lungs (Chapter 20)
    pulmonary plexus
    network of autonomic nervous system fibers found near the hilum of the lung (Chapter 22)
    pulmonary surfactant
    substance composed of phospholipids and proteins that reduces the surface tension of the alveoli; made by type II alveolar cells (Chapter 22)
    pulmonary trunk
    large arterial vessel that carries blood ejected from the right ventricle; divides into the left and right pulmonary arteries (Chapter 19)
    pulmonary trunk
    single large vessel exiting the right ventricle that divides to form the right and left pulmonary arteries (Chapter 20)
    pulmonary valve
    (also, pulmonary semilunar valve, the pulmonic valve, or the right semilunar valve) valve at the base of the pulmonary trunk that prevents backflow of blood into the right ventricle; consists of three flaps (Chapter 19)
    pulmonary veins
    veins that carry highly oxygenated blood into the left atrium, which pumps the blood into the left ventricle, which in turn pumps oxygenated blood into the aorta and to the many branches of the systemic circuit (Chapter 19)
    pulmonary veins
    two sets of paired vessels, one pair on each side, that are formed from the small venules leading away from the pulmonary capillaries that flow into the left atrium (Chapter 20)
    pulmonary ventilation
    exchange of gases between the lungs and the atmosphere; breathing (Chapter 22)
    pulp cavity
    deepest portion of a tooth, containing nerve endings and blood vessels (Chapter 23)
    pulse
    alternating expansion and recoil of an artery as blood moves through the vessel; an indicator of heart rate (Chapter 20)
    pulse pressure
    difference between the systolic and diastolic pressures (Chapter 20)
    Punnett square
    grid used to display all possible combinations of alleles transmitted by parents to offspring and predict the mathematical probability of offspring inheriting a given genotype (Chapter 28)
    pupil
    open hole at the center of the iris that light passes through into the eye (Chapter 14)
    purine
    nitrogen-containing base with a double ring structure; adenine and guanine (Chapter 2)
    Purkinje fibers
    specialized myocardial conduction fibers that arise from the bundle branches and spread the impulse to the myocardial contraction fibers of the ventricles (Chapter 19)
    putamen
    nucleus deep in the cerebrum that is part of the basal nuclei; along with the caudate, it is part of the striatum (Chapter 13)
    pyloric antrum
    wider, more superior part of the pylorus (Chapter 23)
    pyloric canal
    narrow, more inferior part of the pylorus (Chapter 23)
    pyloric sphincter
    sphincter that controls stomach emptying (Chapter 23)
    pylorus
    lower, funnel-shaped part of the stomach that is continuous with the duodenum (Chapter 23)
    pyramidal decussation
    location at which corticospinal tract fibers cross the midline and segregate into the anterior and lateral divisions of the pathway (Chapter 14)
    pyramids
    segment of the descending motor pathway that travels in the anterior position of the medulla (Chapter 14)
    pyrimidine
    nitrogen-containing base with a single ring structure; cytosine, thiamine, and uracil (Chapter 2)
    pyruvate
    three-carbon end product of glycolysis and starting material that is converted into acetyl CoA that enters the Krebs cycle (Chapter 24)
    pyruvic acid
    product of glycolysis that can be used in aerobic respiration or converted to lactic acid (Chapter 10)

    Q¶

    QRS complex
    component of the electrocardiogram that represents the depolarization of the ventricles and includes, as a component, the repolarization of the atria (Chapter 19)
    quadratus femoris
    muscle deep to the gluteus maximus on the lateral surface of the thigh that laterally rotates the femur at the hip (Chapter 11)
    quadratus lumborum
    posterior part of the abdominal wall that helps with posture and stabilization of the body (Chapter 11)
    quadriceps femoris group
    four muscles, that extend and stabilize the knee (Chapter 11)
    quadriceps tendon
    (also, patellar tendon) tendon common to all four quadriceps muscles, inserts into the patella (Chapter 11)
    quickening
    fetal movements that are strong enough to be felt by the mother (Chapter 28)
    quiet breathing
    (also, eupnea) mode of breathing that occurs at rest and does not require the cognitive thought of the individual (Chapter 22)

    R¶

    radial artery
    formed at the bifurcation of the brachial artery; parallels the radius; gives off smaller branches until it reaches the carpal region where it fuses with the ulnar artery to form the superficial and deep palmar arches; supplies blood to the lower arm and carpal region (Chapter 20)
    radial collateral ligament
    intrinsic ligament on the lateral side of the elbow joint; runs from the lateral epicondyle of humerus to merge with the annular ligament (Chapter 9)
    radial fossa
    small depression located on the anterior humerus above the capitulum; this space receives the head of the radius when the elbow is maximally flexed (Chapter 8)
    radial nerve
    systemic nerve of the arm, the distal component of which is located near the radial bone (Chapter 13)
    radial notch of the ulna
    small, smooth area on the lateral side of the proximal ulna; articulates with the head of the radius as part of the proximal radioulnar joint (Chapter 8)
    radial tuberosity
    oval-shaped, roughened protuberance located on the medial side of the proximal radius (Chapter 8)
    radial vein
    parallels the radius and radial artery; arises from the palmar venous arches and leads to the brachial vein (Chapter 20)
    radiation
    transfer of heat via infrared waves (Chapter 24)
    radioactive isotope
    unstable, heavy isotope that gives off subatomic particles, or electromagnetic energy, as it decays; also called radioisotopes (Chapter 2)
    radiocarpal joint
    wrist joint, located between the forearm and hand regions of the upper limb; articulation formed proximally by the distal end of the radius and the fibrocartilaginous pad that unites the distal radius and ulna bone, and distally by the scaphoid, lunate, and triquetrum carpal bones (Chapter 8)
    radius
    bone located on the lateral side of the forearm (Chapter 8)
    ramus of the mandible
    vertical portion of the mandible (Chapter 7)
    reabsorption
    in the cardiovascular system, the movement of material from the interstitial fluid into the capillaries (Chapter 20)
    reactant
    one or more substances that enter into the reaction (Chapter 2)
    reactive oxygen species (ROS)
    a group of extremely reactive peroxides and oxygen-containing radicals that may contribute to cellular damage (Chapter 3)
    receptive aphasia
    loss of the ability to understand received language, such as what is spoken to the subject or given in written form (Chapter 16)
    receptor
    protein molecule that contains a binding site for another specific molecule (called a ligand) (Chapter 3)
    receptor cell
    cell that transduces environmental stimuli into neural signals (Chapter 14)
    receptor potential
    graded potential in a specialized sensory cell that directly causes the release of neurotransmitter without an intervening action potential (Chapter 12)
    receptor-mediated endocytosis
    endocytosis of ligands attached to membrane-bound receptors (Chapter 3)
    recessive
    describes a trait that is only expressed in homozygous form and is masked in heterozygous form (Chapter 28)
    recessive lethal
    inheritance pattern in which individuals with two copies of a lethal allele do not survive in utero or have a shortened life span (Chapter 28)
    recruitment
    increase in the number of motor units involved in contraction (Chapter 10)
    rectal valve
    one of three transverse folds in the rectum where feces is separated from flatus (Chapter 23)
    rectum
    part of the large intestine between the sigmoid colon and anal canal (Chapter 23)
    rectus
    straight (Chapter 11)
    rectus abdominis
    long, linear muscle that extends along the middle of the trunk (Chapter 11)
    rectus femoris
    quadricep muscle on the anterior aspect of the thigh (Chapter 11)
    rectus sheaths
    tissue that makes up the linea alba (Chapter 11)
    red blood cells (RBCs)
    (also, erythrocytes) one of the formed elements of blood that transports oxygen (Chapter 18)
    red marrow
    connective tissue in the interior cavity of a bone where hematopoiesis takes place (Chapter 6)
    red nucleus
    midbrain nucleus that sends corrective commands to the spinal cord along the rubrospinal tract, based on disparity between an original command and the sensory feedback from movement (Chapter 14)
    red nucleus
    nucleus in the midbrain that receives output from the cerebellum and projects onto the spinal cord in the rubrospinal tract (Chapter 16)
    reduction
    gaining of an electron (Chapter 24)
    referred pain
    the conscious perception of visceral sensation projected to a different region of the body, such as the left shoulder and arm pain as a sign for a heart attack (Chapter 15)
    reflex arc
    circuit of a reflex that involves a sensory input and motor output, or an afferent branch and an efferent branch, and an integrating center to connect the two branches (Chapter 15)
    refractory period
    time after the initiation of an action potential when another action potential cannot be generated (Chapter 12)
    regional anatomy
    study of the structures that contribute to specific body regions (Chapter 1)
    regulatory T cells (Treg)
    (also, suppressor T cells) class of CD4 T cells that regulates other T cell responses (Chapter 21)
    relative refractory period
    time during the refractory period when a new action potential can only be initiated by a stronger stimulus than the current action potential because voltage-gated K+ channels are not closed (Chapter 12)
    relaxation phase
    period after twitch contraction when tension decreases (Chapter 10)
    remodeling
    process by which osteoclasts resorb old or damaged bone at the same time as and on the same surface where osteoblasts form new bone to replace that which is resorbed (Chapter 6)
    renal artery
    branch of the abdominal aorta; supplies each kidney (Chapter 20)
    renal columns
    extensions of the renal cortex into the renal medulla; separates the renal pyramids; contains blood vessels and connective tissues (Chapter 25)
    renal corpuscle
    consists of the glomerulus and Bowman’s capsule (Chapter 25)
    renal cortex
    outer part of kidney containing all of the nephrons; some nephrons have loops of Henle extending into the medulla (Chapter 25)
    renal fat pad
    adipose tissue between the renal fascia and the renal capsule that provides protective cushioning to the kidney (Chapter 25)
    renal hilum
    recessed medial area of the kidney through which the renal artery, renal vein, ureters, lymphatics, and nerves pass (Chapter 25)
    renal papillae
    medullary area of the renal pyramids where collecting ducts empty urine into the minor calyces (Chapter 25)
    renal pyramids
    six to eight cone-shaped tissues in the medulla of the kidney containing collecting ducts and the loops of Henle of juxtamedullary nephrons (Chapter 25)
    renal vein
    largest vein entering the inferior vena cava; drains the kidneys and leads to the inferior vena cava (Chapter 20)
    renewal
    process by which worn-out cells are replaced (Chapter 1)
    renin
    enzyme produced by juxtaglomerular cells in response to decreased blood pressure or sympathetic nervous activity; catalyzes the conversion of angiotensinogen into angiotensin I (Chapter 25)
    repolarization
    return of the membrane potential to its normally negative voltage at the end of the action potential (Chapter 12)
    reposition
    movement of the thumb from opposition back to the anatomical position (next to index finger) (Chapter 9)
    reproduction
    process by which new organisms are generated (Chapter 1)
    reserve zone
    region of the epiphyseal plate that anchors the plate to the osseous tissue of the epiphysis (Chapter 6)
    residual volume (RV)
    amount of air that remains in the lungs after maximum exhalation (Chapter 22)
    resistance
    property of an axon that relates to the ability of particles to diffuse through the cytoplasm; this is inversely proportional to the fiber diameter (Chapter 12)
    resistance
    any condition or parameter that slows or counteracts the flow of blood (Chapter 20)
    respiratory acidosis
    condition wherein an excess of carbonic acid or CO2 causes the blood to be overly acidic (Chapter 26)
    respiratory alkalosis
    condition wherein a deficiency of carbonic acid/CO2 levels causes the blood to be overly alkaline (Chapter 26)
    respiratory bronchiole
    specific type of bronchiole that leads to alveolar sacs (Chapter 22)
    respiratory cycle
    one sequence of inspiration and expiration (Chapter 22)
    respiratory epithelium
    ciliated lining of much of the conducting zone that is specialized to remove debris and pathogens, and produce mucus (Chapter 22)
    respiratory membrane
    alveolar and capillary wall together, which form an air-blood barrier that facilitates the simple diffusion of gases (Chapter 22)
    respiratory pump
    increase in the volume of the thorax during inhalation that decreases air pressure, enabling venous blood to flow into the thoracic region, then exhalation increases pressure, moving blood into the atria (Chapter 20)
    respiratory rate
    total number of breaths taken each minute (Chapter 22)
    respiratory volume
    varying amounts of air within the lung at a given time (Chapter 22)
    respiratory zone
    includes structures of the respiratory system that are directly involved in gas exchange (Chapter 22)
    response
    nervous system function that causes a target tissue (muscle or gland) to produce an event as a consequence to stimuli (Chapter 12)
    responsiveness
    ability of an organisms or a system to adjust to changes in conditions (Chapter 1)
    rest and digest
    set of functions associated with the parasympathetic system that lead to restful actions and digestion (Chapter 15)
    resting membrane potential
    the difference in voltage measured across a cell membrane under steady-state conditions, typically -70 mV (Chapter 12)
    reticular fiber

    reticular formation

    reticular layer
    deeper layer of the dermis; it has a reticulated appearance due to the presence of abundant collagen and elastin fibers (Chapter 5)

    reticulocyte

    reticuloendothelial cell

    retinacula
    fibrous bands that sheath the tendons at the wrist (Chapter 11)
    retinal

    retrograde amnesia
    loss of memories before a particular event (Chapter 16)
    retroperitoneal

    retroperitoneal

    rhomboid minor
    muscle that attaches the vertebral border of the scapula to the spinous process of the thoracic vertebrae (Chapter 11)

    rickets

    use of a tuning fork to test conductive hearing loss versus sensorineural hearing loss (Chapter 16)

    rod photoreceptor

    Romberg test
    test of equilibrium that requires the patient to maintain a straight, upright posture without visual feedback of position (Chapter 16)
    root
    region of the external nose between the eyebrows (Chapter 22)
    root
    portion of a tooth embedded in the alveolar processes beneath the gum line (Chapter 23)
    rotation
    movement of a bone around a central axis (atlantoaxial joint) or around its long axis (proximal radioulnar joint; shoulder or hip joint); twisting of the vertebral column resulting from the summation of small motions between adjacent vertebrae (Chapter 9)
    rotator cuff
    strong connective tissue structure formed by the fusion of four rotator cuff muscle tendons to the articular capsule of the shoulder joint; surrounds and supports superior, anterior, lateral, and posterior sides of the humeral head (Chapter 9)

    S phase

    saddle joint
