    spleen
    secondary lymphoid organ that filters pathogens from the blood (white pulp) and removes degenerating or damaged blood cells (red pulp) (Chapter 21)
    splenic artery
    branch of the celiac trunk; supplies blood to the spleen (Chapter 20)
    splenius
    posterior neck muscles; includes the splenius capitis and splenius cervicis (Chapter 11)
    splenius capitis
    neck muscle that inserts into the head region (Chapter 11)
    splenius cervicis
    neck muscle that inserts into the cervical region (Chapter 11)
    spliceosome
    complex of enzymes that serves to splice out the introns of a pre-mRNA transcript (Chapter 3)
    splicing
    the process of modifying a pre-mRNA transcript by removing certain, typically non-coding, regions (Chapter 3)
    spongy bone
    (also, cancellous bone) trabeculated osseous tissue that supports shifts in weight distribution (Chapter 6)
    spontaneous depolarization
    (also, prepotential depolarization) the mechanism that accounts for the autorhythmic property of cardiac muscle; the membrane potential increases as sodium ions diffuse through the always-open sodium ion channels and causes the electrical potential to rise (Chapter 19)
    squamous cell carcinoma
    type of skin cancer that originates from the stratum spinosum of the epidermis (Chapter 5)
    squamous suture
    joint that unites the parietal bone to the squamous portion of the temporal bone on the lateral side of the skull (Chapter 7)
    stage of exhaustion
    stage three of the general adaptation syndrome; the body’s long-term response to stress mediated by the hormones of the adrenal cortex (Chapter 17)
    stage of resistance
    stage two of the general adaptation syndrome; the body’s continued response to stress after stage one diminishes (Chapter 17)
    stapes
    (also, stirrup) ossicle of the middle ear that is attached to the inner ear (Chapter 14)
    stem cell
    cell that is oligo-, multi-, or pleuripotent that has the ability to produce additional stem cells rather than becoming further specialized (Chapter 3)
    stereocilia
    array of apical membrane extensions in a hair cell that transduce movements when they are bent (Chapter 14)
    stereognosis
    perception of common objects placed in the hand solely on the basis of manipulation of that object in the hand (Chapter 16)
    sternal angle
    junction line between manubrium and body of the sternum and the site for attachment of the second rib to the sternum (Chapter 7)
    sternal end of the clavicle
    medial end of the clavicle that articulates with the manubrium of the sternum (Chapter 8)
    sternoclavicular joint
    articulation between the manubrium of the sternum and the sternal end of the clavicle; forms the only bony attachment between the pectoral girdle of the upper limb and the axial skeleton (Chapter 8)
    sternocleidomastoid
    major muscle that laterally flexes and rotates the head (Chapter 11)
    sternohyoid
    muscle that depresses the hyoid bone (Chapter 11)
    sternothyroid
    muscle that depresses the larynx’s thyroid cartilage (Chapter 11)
    sternum
    flattened bone located at the center of the anterior chest (Chapter 7)
    steroid
    (also, sterol) lipid compound composed of four hydrocarbon rings bonded to a variety of other atoms and molecules (Chapter 2)
    stimulus
    an event in the external or internal environment that registers as activity in a sensory neuron (Chapter 12)
    stomach
    alimentary canal organ that contributes to chemical and mechanical digestion of food from the esophagus before releasing it, as chyme, to the small intestine (Chapter 23)
    straight sinus
    dural sinus that drains blood from the deep center of the brain to collect with the other sinuses (Chapter 13)
    straight sinus
    enlarged vein that drains blood from the brain; receives most of the blood from the great cerebral vein and flows into the left or right transverse sinus (Chapter 20)
    stratified columnar epithelium
    tissue that consists of two or more layers of column-like cells, contains glands and is found in some ducts (Chapter 4)
    stratified cuboidal epithelium
    tissue that consists of two or more layers of cube-shaped cells, found in some ducts (Chapter 4)
    stratified squamous epithelium
    tissue that consists of multiple layers of cells with the most apical being flat scale-like cells; protects surfaces from abrasion (Chapter 4)
    stratum basale
    deepest layer of the epidermis, made of epidermal stem cells (Chapter 5)
    stratum corneum
    most superficial layer of the epidermis (Chapter 5)
    stratum granulosum
    layer of the epidermis superficial to the stratum spinosum (Chapter 5)
    stratum lucidum
    layer of the epidermis between the stratum granulosum and stratum corneum, found only in thick skin covering the palms, soles of the feet, and digits (Chapter 5)
    stratum spinosum
    layer of the epidermis superficial to the stratum basale, characterized by the presence of desmosomes (Chapter 5)
    stress-relaxation response
    relaxation of smooth muscle tissue after being stretched (Chapter 10)
    stretch mark
    mark formed on the skin due to a sudden growth spurt and expansion of the dermis beyond its elastic limits (Chapter 5)
    stretch reflex
    response to activation of the muscle spindle stretch receptor that causes contraction of the muscle to maintain a constant length (Chapter 14)
    striation
    alignment of parallel actin and myosin filaments which form a banded pattern (Chapter 4)
    striatum
    the caudate and putamen collectively, as part of the basal nuclei, which receive input from the cerebral cortex (Chapter 13)
    stroke
    (also, cerebrovascular accident (CVA)) loss of neurological function caused by an interruption of blood flow to a region of the central nervous system (Chapter 16)
    stroke volume (SV)
    amount of blood pumped by each ventricle per contraction; also, the difference between EDV and ESV (Chapter 19)
    styloglossus
    muscle that originates on the styloid bone, and allows upward and backward motion of the tongue (Chapter 11)
    stylohyoid
    muscle that elevates the hyoid bone posteriorly (Chapter 11)
    styloid process
    downward projecting, elongated bony process located on the inferior aspect of the skull (Chapter 7)
    styloid process of the radius
    pointed projection located on the lateral end of the distal radius (Chapter 8)
    styloid process of the ulna
    short, bony projection located on the medial end of the distal ulna (Chapter 8)
    stylomastoid foramen
    opening located on inferior skull, between the styloid process and mastoid process (Chapter 7)
    subacromial bursa
    bursa that protects the supraspinatus muscle tendon and superior end of the humerus from rubbing against the acromion of the scapula (Chapter 9)
    subarachnoid space
    space between the arachnoid mater and pia mater that contains CSF and the fibrous connections of the arachnoid trabeculae (Chapter 13)
    subclavian artery
    right subclavian arises from the brachiocephalic artery, whereas the left subclavian artery arises from the aortic arch; gives rise to the internal thoracic, vertebral, and thyrocervical arteries; supplies blood to the arms, chest, shoulders, back, and central nervous system (Chapter 20)
    subclavian vein
    located deep in the thoracic cavity; becomes the axillary vein as it enters the axillary region; drains the axillary and smaller local veins near the scapular region; leads to the brachiocephalic vein (Chapter 20)
    subclavius
    muscle that stabilizes the clavicle during movement (Chapter 11)
    subcortical nucleus
    all the nuclei beneath the cerebral cortex, including the basal nuclei and the basal forebrain (Chapter 13)
    subcutaneous bursa
    bursa that prevents friction between skin and an underlying bone (Chapter 9)
    sublingual gland
    one of a pair of major salivary glands located beneath the tongue (Chapter 23)
    submandibular gland
    one of a pair of major salivary glands located in the floor of the mouth (Chapter 23)
    submodality
    specific sense within a broader major sense such as sweet as a part of the sense of taste, or color as a part of vision (Chapter 14)
    submucosa
    layer of dense connective tissue in the alimentary canal wall that binds the overlying mucosa to the underlying muscularis (Chapter 23)
    submucosal plexus
    (plexus of Meissner) nerve supply that regulates activity of glands and smooth muscle (Chapter 23)
    submuscular bursa
    bursa that prevents friction between bone and a muscle or between adjacent muscles (Chapter 9)
    subpubic angle
    inverted V-shape formed by the convergence of the right and left ischiopubic rami; this angle is greater than 80 degrees in females and less than 70 degrees in males (Chapter 8)
    subscapular bursa
    bursa that prevents rubbing of the subscapularis muscle tendon against the scapula (Chapter 9)
    subscapular fossa
    broad depression located on the anterior (deep) surface of the scapula (Chapter 8)
    subscapular vein
    drains blood from the subscapular region and leads to the axillary vein (Chapter 20)
    subscapularis
    muscle that originates on the anterior scapula and medially rotates the arm (Chapter 11)
    substantia nigra pars compacta
    nuclei within the basal nuclei that release dopamine to modulate the function of the striatum; part of the motor pathway (Chapter 13)
    substantia nigra pars reticulata
    nuclei within the basal nuclei that serve as an output center of the nuclei; part of the motor pathway (Chapter 13)
    substrate
    reactant in an enzymatic reaction (Chapter 2)
    subtalar joint
    articulation between the talus and calcaneus bones of the foot; allows motions that contribute to inversion/eversion of the foot (Chapter 9)
    subtendinous bursa
    bursa that prevents friction between bone and a muscle tendon (Chapter 9)
    subthalamus
    nucleus within the basal nuclei that is part of the indirect pathway (Chapter 13)
    sucrase
    brush border enzyme that breaks down sucrose into glucose and fructose (Chapter 23)
    sudoriferous gland
    sweat gland (Chapter 5)
    sulcus
    groove formed by convolutions in the surface of the cerebral cortex (Chapter 13)
    sulcus
    (plural = sulci) fat-filled groove visible on the surface of the heart; coronary vessels are also located in these areas (Chapter 19)
    summate
    to add together, as in the cumulative change in postsynaptic potentials toward reaching threshold in the membrane, either across a span of the membrane or over a certain amount of time (Chapter 12)
    superficial
    describes a position nearer to the surface of the body (Chapter 1)
    superficial anterior compartment of the forearm
    flexor carpi radialis, palmaris longus, flexor carpi ulnaris, flexor digitorum superficialis, and their associated blood vessels and nerves (Chapter 11)
    superficial posterior compartment of the forearm
    extensor radialis longus, extensor carpi radialis brevis, extensor digitorum, extensor digiti minimi, extensor carpi ulnaris, and their associated blood vessels and nerves (Chapter 11)
    superficial reflex
    reflexive contraction initiated by gentle stimulation of the skin (Chapter 16)
    superior
    describes a position above or higher than another part of the body proper; also referred to as cranial (Chapter 1)
    superior angle of the scapula
    corner of the scapula between the superior and medial borders of the scapula (Chapter 8)
    superior articular process
    bony process that extends upward from the vertebral arch of a vertebra that articulates with the inferior articular process of the next higher vertebra (Chapter 7)
    superior articular process of the sacrum
    paired processes that extend upward from the sacrum to articulate (join) with the inferior articular processes from the L5 vertebra (Chapter 7)
    superior border of the scapula
    superior margin of the scapula (Chapter 8)
    superior cerebellar peduncle (SCP)
    white-matter tract representing output of the cerebellum to the red nucleus of the midbrain (Chapter 16)
    superior cervical ganglion
    one of the paravertebral ganglia of the sympathetic system that projects to the head (Chapter 15)
    superior colliculus
    half of the midbrain tectum that is responsible for aligning visual, auditory, and somatosensory spatial perceptions (Chapter 13)
    superior colliculus
    structure in the midbrain that combines visual, auditory, and somatosensory input to coordinate spatial and topographic representations of the three sensory systems (Chapter 14)
    superior extensor retinaculum
    transverse ligament of the ankle (Chapter 11)
    superior gemellus
    muscle deep to the gluteus maximus on the lateral surface of the thigh that laterally rotates the femur at the hip (Chapter 11)
    superior mesenteric artery
    branch of the abdominal aorta; supplies blood to the small intestine (duodenum, jejunum, and ileum), the pancreas, and a majority of the large intestine (Chapter 20)
    superior mesenteric ganglion
    one of the collateral ganglia of the sympathetic system that projects to the digestive system (Chapter 15)
    superior nasal concha
    smallest and most superiorly located of the nasal conchae; formed by the ethmoid bone (Chapter 7)
    superior nuchal line
    paired bony lines on the posterior skull that extend laterally from the external occipital protuberance (Chapter 7)
    superior oblique
    extraocular muscle responsible for medial rotation of the eye (Chapter 14)
    superior orbital fissure
    irregularly shaped opening between the middle cranial fossa and the posterior orbit (Chapter 7)
    superior phrenic artery
    branch of the thoracic aorta; supplies blood to the superior surface of the diaphragm (Chapter 20)
    superior pubic ramus
    narrow segment of bone that passes laterally from the pubic body to join the ilium (Chapter 8)
    superior rectus
    extraocular muscle responsible for looking up (Chapter 14)
    superior rotation
    movement of the scapula during upper limb abduction in which the glenoid cavity of the scapula moves in an upward direction as the medial end of the scapular spine moves in a downward direction (Chapter 9)
    superior sagittal sinus
    dural sinus that runs along the top of the longitudinal fissure and drains blood from the majority of the outer cerebrum (Chapter 13)
    superior sagittal sinus
    enlarged vein located midsagittally between the meningeal and periosteal layers of the dura mater within the falx cerebri; receives most of the blood drained from the superior surface of the cerebrum and leads to the inferior jugular vein and the vertebral vein (Chapter 20)
    superior vena cava
    large systemic vein that returns blood to the heart from the superior portion of the body (Chapter 19)
    superior vena cava
    large systemic vein; drains blood from most areas superior to the diaphragm; empties into the right atrium (Chapter 20)
    supinated position
    forearm position in which the palm faces anteriorly (anatomical position) (Chapter 9)
    supination
    forearm motion that moves the palm of the hand from the palm backward to the palm forward position (Chapter 9)
    supinator
    muscle that moves the palm and forearm anteriorly (Chapter 11)
    supine
    face up (Chapter 1)
    supplemental motor area
    cortical area anterior to the primary motor cortex that is responsible for planning movements (Chapter 14)
    supportive connective tissue
    type of connective tissue that provides strength to the body and protects soft tissue (Chapter 4)
    suprachiasmatic nucleus
    hypothalamic target of the retina that helps to establish the circadian rhythm of the body on the basis of the presence or absence of daylight (Chapter 14)
    supraglenoid tubercle
    small bump located at the superior margin of the glenoid cavity (Chapter 8)
    suprahyoid muscles
    neck muscles that are superior to the hyoid bone (Chapter 11)
    supraorbital foramen
    opening located on anterior skull, at the superior margin of the orbit (Chapter 7)
    supraorbital margin
    superior margin of the orbit (Chapter 7)
    suprascapular notch
    small notch located along the superior border of the scapula, medial to the coracoid process (Chapter 8)
    supraspinatus
    muscle that abducts the arm (Chapter 11)
    supraspinous fossa
    narrow depression located on the posterior scapula, superior to the spine (Chapter 8)
    supraspinous ligament
    ligament that interconnects the spinous processes of the thoracic and lumbar vertebrae (Chapter 7)
    surgical neck
    region of the humerus where the expanded, proximal end joins with the narrower shaft (Chapter 8)
    suspension
    liquid mixture in which particles distributed in the liquid settle out over time (Chapter 2)
    suspensory ligaments
    bands of connective tissue that suspend the breast onto the chest wall by attachment to the overlying dermis (Chapter 27)
    sustentaculum tali
    bony ledge extending from the medial side of the calcaneus bone (Chapter 8)
    suture
    junction line at which adjacent bones of the skull are united by fibrous connective tissue (Chapter 7)
    suture
    fibrous joint that connects the bones of the skull (except the mandible); an immobile joint (synarthrosis) (Chapter 9)
    sympathetic chain ganglia
    autonomic ganglia in a chain along the anterolateral aspect of the vertebral column that are responsible for contributing to homeostatic mechanisms of the autonomic nervous system (Chapter 13)
    sympathetic chain ganglia
    series of ganglia adjacent to the vertebral column that receive input from central sympathetic neurons (Chapter 15)
    sympathetic division
    division of the autonomic nervous system associated with the fight-or-flight response (Chapter 15)
    sympatholytic drug
    drug that interrupts, or “lyses,” the function of the sympathetic system (Chapter 15)
    sympathomimetic drug
    drug that enhances or mimics the function of the sympathetic system (Chapter 15)
    symphysis
    type of cartilaginous joint where the bones are joined by fibrocartilage (Chapter 9)
    synapse
    narrow junction across which a chemical signal passes from neuron to the next, initiating a new electrical signal in the target cell (Chapter 12)
    synaptic cleft
    space between a nerve (axon) terminal and a motor end-plate (Chapter 10)
    synaptic cleft
    small gap between cells in a chemical synapse where neurotransmitter diffuses from the presynaptic element to the postsynaptic element (Chapter 12)
    synaptic end bulb
    swelling at the end of an axon where neurotransmitter molecules are released onto a target cell across a synapse (Chapter 12)
    synarthrosis
    immobile or nearly immobile joint (Chapter 9)
    synchondrosis
    type of cartilaginous joint where the bones are joined by hyaline cartilage (Chapter 9)
    syncytiotrophoblast
    superficial cells of the trophoblast that fuse to form a multinucleated body that digests endometrial cells to firmly secure the blastocyst to the uterine wall (Chapter 28)
    syndesmosis
    type of fibrous joint in which two separated, parallel bones are connected by an interosseous membrane (Chapter 9)
    synergist
    muscle whose contraction helps a prime mover in an action (Chapter 11)
    synostosis
    site at which adjacent bones or bony components have fused together (Chapter 9)
    synovial fluid
    thick, lubricating fluid that fills the interior of a synovial joint (Chapter 9)
    synovial joint
    joint at which the articulating surfaces of the bones are located within a joint cavity formed by an articular capsule (Chapter 9)
    synovial membrane
    connective tissue membrane that lines the cavities of freely movable joints, producing synovial fluid for lubrication (Chapter 4)
    synovial membrane
    thin layer that lines the inner surface of the joint cavity at a synovial joint; produces the synovial fluid (Chapter 9)
    synthesis reaction
    type of anabolic reaction in which two or more atoms or molecules bond, resulting in the formation of a larger molecule (Chapter 2)
    systemic anatomy
    study of the structures that contribute to specific body systems (Chapter 1)
    systemic circuit
    blood flow to and from virtually all of the tissues of the body (Chapter 19)
    systemic edema
    increased fluid retention in the interstitial spaces and cells of the body; can be seen as swelling over large areas of the body, particularly the lower extremities (Chapter 25)
    systemic nerve
    nerve in the periphery distal to a nerve plexus or spinal nerve (Chapter 13)
    systole
    period of time when the heart muscle is contracting (Chapter 19)
    systolic pressure
    larger number recorded when measuring arterial blood pressure; represents the maximum value following ventricular contraction (Chapter 20)

    T¶

    T cell
    lymphocyte that acts by secreting molecules that regulate the immune system or by causing the destruction of foreign cells, viruses, and cancer cells (Chapter 21)
    T cell tolerance
    process during T cell differentiation where most T cells that recognize antigens from one’s own body are destroyed (Chapter 21)
    T cell-dependent antigen
    antigen that binds to B cells, which requires signals from T cells to make antibody (Chapter 21)
    T cell-independent antigen
    binds to B cells, which do not require signals from T cells to make antibody (Chapter 21)
    T lymphocytes
    (also, T cells) lymphocytes that provide cellular-level immunity by physically attacking foreign or diseased cells (Chapter 18)
    T wave
    component of the electrocardiogram that represents the repolarization of the ventricles (Chapter 19)
    T-tubule
    projection of the sarcolemma into the interior of the cell (Chapter 10)
    talocrural joint
    ankle joint; articulation between the talus bone of the foot and medial malleolus of the tibia, distal tibia, and lateral malleolus of the fibula; a uniaxial hinge joint that allows only for dorsiflexion and plantar flexion of the foot (Chapter 9)
    talus
    tarsal bone that articulates superiorly with the tibia and fibula at the ankle joint; also articulates inferiorly with the calcaneus bone and anteriorly with the navicular bone (Chapter 8)
    target effector
    organ, tissue, or gland that will respond to the control of an autonomic or somatic or endocrine signal (Chapter 15)
    target heart rate
    range in which both the heart and lungs receive the maximum benefit from an aerobic workout (Chapter 19)
    tarsal bone
    one of the seven bones that make up the posterior foot; includes the calcaneus, talus, navicular, cuboid, medial cuneiform, intermediate cuneiform, and lateral cuneiform bones (Chapter 8)
    taste buds
    structures within a papilla on the tongue that contain gustatory receptor cells (Chapter 14)
    tectorial membrane
    component of the organ of Corti that lays over the hair cells, into which the stereocilia are embedded (Chapter 14)
    tectospinal tract
    extrapyramidal connections between the superior colliculus and spinal cord (Chapter 14)
    tectum
    region of the midbrain, thought of as the roof of the cerebral aqueduct, which is subdivided into the inferior and superior colliculi (Chapter 13)
    tegmentum
    region of the midbrain, thought of as the floor of the cerebral aqueduct, which continues into the pons and medulla as the floor of the fourth ventricle (Chapter 13)
    telencephalon
    secondary vesicle of the embryonic brain that develops into the cerebrum (Chapter 13)
    telogen
    resting phase of the hair growth cycle initiated with catagen and terminated by the beginning of a new anagen phase of hair growth (Chapter 5)
    telophase
    final stage of mitosis (and meiosis), preceding cytokinesis, characterized by the formation of two new daughter nuclei (Chapter 3)
    temporal bone
    paired bones that form the lateral, inferior portions of the skull, with squamous, mastoid, and petrous portions (Chapter 7)
    temporal fossa
    shallow space on the lateral side of the skull, above the level of the zygomatic arch (Chapter 7)
    temporal lobe
    region of the cerebral cortex directly beneath the temporal bone of the cranium (Chapter 13)
    temporal process of the zygomatic bone
    short extension from the zygomatic bone that forms the anterior portion of the zygomatic arch (Chapter 7)
    temporal summation
    combination of graded potentials at the same location on a neuron resulting in a strong signal from one input (Chapter 12)
    temporal vein
    drains blood from the temporal region and leads to the external jugular vein (Chapter 20)
    temporalis
    muscle that retracts the mandible (Chapter 11)
    temporomandibular joint (TMJ)
    articulation between the condyle of the mandible and the mandibular fossa and articular tubercle of the temporal bone of the skull; allows for depression/elevation (opening/closing of mouth), protraction/retraction, and side-to-side motions of the mandible (Chapter 9)
    tendinous intersections
    three transverse bands of collagen fibers that divide the rectus abdominis into segments (Chapter 11)
    tendon
    dense connective tissue structure that anchors a muscle to bone (Chapter 9)
    tendon sheath
    connective tissue that surrounds a tendon at places where the tendon crosses a joint; contains a lubricating fluid to prevent friction and allow smooth movements of the tendon (Chapter 9)
    tenia coli
    one of three smooth muscle bands that make up the longitudinal muscle layer of the muscularis in all of the large intestine except the terminal end (Chapter 23)
    tensor fascia lata
    muscle that flexes and abducts the thigh (Chapter 11)
    teres major
    muscle that extends the arm and assists in adduction and medial rotation of it (Chapter 11)
    teres minor
    muscle that laterally rotates and extends the arm (Chapter 11)
    terminal electron acceptor
    oxygen, the recipient of the free hydrogen at the end of the electron transport chain (Chapter 24)
    terminal ganglia
    ganglia of the parasympathetic division of the autonomic system, which are located near or within the target effector, the latter also known as intramural ganglia (Chapter 15)
    terminal ganglion
    autonomic ganglia that are near or within the walls of organs that are responsible for contributing to homeostatic mechanisms of the autonomic nervous system (Chapter 13)
    tertiary follicles
    (also, antral follicles) ovarian follicles with a primary or secondary oocyte, multiple layers of granulosa cells, and a fully formed antrum (Chapter 27)
    testes
    (singular = testis) male gonads (Chapter 27)
    testicular artery
    branch of the abdominal aorta; will ultimately travel outside the body cavity to the testes and form one component of the spermatic cord (Chapter 20)
    testicular vein
    drains the testes and forms part of the spermatic cord; the right testicular vein empties directly into the inferior vena cava and the left testicular vein empties into the left renal vein (Chapter 20)
    testosterone
    steroid hormone secreted by the testes and important in the maturation of sperm cells, growth and development of the reproductive system, and the development of secondary sex characteristics (Chapter 17)
    tetanus
    a continuous fused contraction (Chapter 10)
    Th1 cells
    cells that secrete cytokines that enhance the activity of macrophages and other cells (Chapter 21)
    Th2 cells
    cells that secrete cytokines that induce B cells to differentiate into antibody-secreting plasma cells (Chapter 21)
    thalamus
    region of the central nervous system that acts as a relay for sensory pathways (Chapter 12)
    thalamus
    major region of the diencephalon that is responsible for relaying information between the cerebrum and the hindbrain, spinal cord, and periphery (Chapter 13)
    thalassemia
    inherited blood disorder in which maturation of RBCs does not proceed normally, leading to abnormal formation of hemoglobin and the destruction of RBCs (Chapter 18)
    theca cells
    estrogen-producing cells in a maturing ovarian follicle (Chapter 27)
    thenar
    group of muscles on the lateral aspect of the palm (Chapter 11)
    thenar eminence
    rounded contour of muscle at the base of the thumb (Chapter 11)
    thermoneutral
    external temperature at which the body does not expend any energy for thermoregulation, about 84 °F (Chapter 24)
    thermoreceptor
    type of sensory receptor capable of transducing temperature stimuli into neural action potentials (Chapter 12)
    thermoreceptor
    sensory receptor specialized for temperature stimuli (Chapter 14)
    thermoregulation
    process of regulating the temperature of the body (Chapter 24)
    thick filament
    the thick myosin strands and their multiple heads projecting from the center of the sarcomere toward, but not all to way to, the Z-discs (Chapter 10)
    thigh
    portion of the lower limb located between the hip and knee joints (Chapter 8)
    thin filament
    thin strands of actin and its troponin-tropomyosin complex projecting from the Z-discs toward the center of the sarcomere (Chapter 10)
    third ventricle
    portion of the ventricular system that is in the region of the diencephalon (Chapter 13)
    third-degree burn
    burn that penetrates and destroys the full thickness of the skin (epidermis and dermis) (Chapter 5)
    thoracic aorta
    portion of the descending aorta superior to the aortic hiatus (Chapter 20)
    thoracic cage
    consists of 12 pairs of ribs and sternum (Chapter 7)
    thoracic cavity
    division of the anterior (ventral) cavity that houses the heart, lungs, esophagus, and trachea (Chapter 1)
    thoracic curve
    anteriorly concave curvature of the thoracic vertebral column region; a primary curve of the vertebral column (Chapter 7)
    thoracic duct
    large duct that drains lymph from the lower limbs, left thorax, left upper limb, and the left side of the head (Chapter 21)
    thoracic vertebrae
    twelve vertebrae numbered as T1–T12 that are located in the thoracic region (upper back) of the vertebral column (Chapter 7)
    thoracic wall compliance
    ability of the thoracic wall to stretch while under pressure (Chapter 22)
    thoracolumbar system
    alternate name for the sympathetic division of the autonomic nervous system that is based on the anatomical location of central neurons in the lateral horn of the thoracic and upper lumbar spinal cord (Chapter 15)
    thoroughfare channel
    continuation of the metarteriole that enables blood to bypass a capillary bed and flow directly into a venule, creating a vascular shunt (Chapter 20)
    threshold
    membrane voltage at which an action potential is initiated (Chapter 12)
    thrombin
    enzyme essential for the final steps in formation of a fibrin clot (Chapter 18)
    thrombocytes
    platelets, one of the formed elements of blood that consists of cell fragments broken off from megakaryocytes (Chapter 18)
    thrombocytopenia
    condition in which there are too few platelets, resulting in abnormal bleeding (hemophilia) (Chapter 18)
    thrombocytosis
    condition in which there are too many platelets, resulting in abnormal clotting (thrombosis) (Chapter 18)
    thrombopoietin
    hormone secreted by the liver and kidneys that prompts the development of megakaryocytes into thrombocytes (platelets) (Chapter 18)
    thrombosis
    excessive clot formation (Chapter 18)
    thrombus
    aggregation of fibrin, platelets, and erythrocytes in an intact artery or vein (Chapter 18)
    thymocyte
    immature T cell found in the thymus (Chapter 21)
    thymosins
    hormones produced and secreted by the thymus that play an important role in the development and differentiation of T cells (Chapter 17)
    thymus
    organ that is involved in the development and maturation of T-cells and is particularly active during infancy and childhood (Chapter 17)
    thymus
    primary lymphoid organ; where T lymphocytes proliferate and mature (Chapter 21)
    thyrocervical artery
    arises from the subclavian artery; supplies blood to the thyroid, the cervical region, the upper back, and shoulder (Chapter 20)
    thyrohyoid
    muscle that depresses the hyoid bone and elevates the larynx’s thyroid cartilage (Chapter 11)
    thyroid cartilage
    largest piece of cartilage that makes up the larynx and consists of two lamine (Chapter 22)
    thyroid gland
    large endocrine gland responsible for the synthesis of thyroid hormones (Chapter 17)
    thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH)
    anterior pituitary hormone that triggers secretion of thyroid hormones by the thyroid gland (also called thyrotropin) (Chapter 17)
    thyroxine
    (also, tetraiodothyronine, T4) amino acid–derived thyroid hormone that is more abundant but less potent than T3and often converted to T3 by target cells (Chapter 17)
    tibia
    shin bone; the large, weight-bearing bone located on the medial side of the leg (Chapter 8)
    tibial collateral ligament
    extrinsic ligament of knee joint that spans from the medial epicondyle of the femur to the medial tibia; resists hyperextension and rotation of extended knee (Chapter 9)
    tibial nerve
    systemic nerve of the posterior leg that begins as part of the sciatic nerve (Chapter 13)
    tibial tuberosity
    elevated area on the anterior surface of the proximal tibia (Chapter 8)
    tibialis anterior
    muscle located on the lateral surface of the tibia (Chapter 11)
    tibialis posterior
    muscle that plantar flexes and inverts the foot (Chapter 11)
    tidal volume (TV)
    amount of air that normally enters the lungs during quiet breathing (Chapter 22)
    tight junction
    forms an impermeable barrier between cells (Chapter 4)
    tissue
    group of similar or closely related cells that act together to perform a specific function (Chapter 1)
    tissue
    group of cells that are similar in form and perform related functions (Chapter 4)
    tissue factor
    protein thromboplastin, which initiates the extrinsic pathway when released in response to tissue damage (Chapter 18)
    tissue membrane
    thin layer or sheet of cells that covers the outside of the body, organs, and internal cavities (Chapter 4)
    tissue typing
    typing of MHC molecules between a recipient and donor for use in a potential transplantation procedure (Chapter 21)
    tongue
    accessory digestive organ of the mouth, the bulk of which is composed of skeletal muscle (Chapter 23)
    tonsils
    lymphoid nodules associated with the nasopharynx (Chapter 21)
    topographical
    relating to positional information (Chapter 14)
    total dead space
    sum of the anatomical dead space and alveolar dead space (Chapter 22)
    total lung capacity (TLC)
    total amount of air that can be held in the lungs; sum of TV, ERV, IRV, and RV (Chapter 22)
    total pressure
    sum of all the partial pressures of a gaseous mixture (Chapter 22)
    totipotent
    embryonic cells that have the ability to differentiate into any type of cell and organ in the body (Chapter 3)
    totipotent stem cell
    embryonic stem cell that is capable of differentiating into any and all cells of the body; enabling the full development of an organism (Chapter 18)
    trabeculae
    (singular = trabecula) spikes or sections of the lattice-like matrix in spongy bone (Chapter 6)
    trabeculae carneae
    ridges of muscle covered by endocardium located in the ventricles (Chapter 19)
    trachea
    tube composed of cartilaginous rings and supporting tissue that connects the lung bronchi and the larynx; provides a route for air to enter and exit the lung (Chapter 22)
    trachealis muscle
    smooth muscle located in the fibroelastic membrane of the trachea (Chapter 22)
    tract
    bundle of axons in the central nervous system having the same function and point of origin (Chapter 12)
    trait
    variation of an expressed characteristic (Chapter 28)
    transamination
    transfer of an amine group from one molecule to another as a way to turn nitrogen waste into ammonia so that it can enter the urea cycle (Chapter 24)
    transcription
    process of producing an mRNA molecule that is complementary to a particular gene of DNA (Chapter 3)
    transcription factor
    one of the proteins that regulate the transcription of genes (Chapter 3)
    transduction
    process of changing an environmental stimulus into the electrochemical signals of the nervous system (Chapter 14)
    transfer RNA (tRNA)
    molecules of RNA that serve to bring amino acids to a growing polypeptide strand and properly place them into the sequence (Chapter 3)
    transferrin
    plasma protein that binds reversibly to iron and distributes it throughout the body (Chapter 18)
    transient ischemic attack (TIA)
    temporary disruption of blood flow to the brain in which symptoms occur rapidly but last only a short time (Chapter 16)
    transient ischemic attack (TIA)
    temporary loss of neurological function caused by a brief interruption in blood flow; also known as a mini-stroke (Chapter 20)
    transitional epithelium
    form of stratified epithelium found in the urinary tract, characterized by an apical layer of cells that change shape in response to the presence of urine (Chapter 4)
    translation
    process of producing a protein from the nucleotide sequence code of an mRNA transcript (Chapter 3)
    transpulmonary pressure
    pressure difference between the intrapleural and intra-alveolar pressures (Chapter 22)
    transverse colon
    part of the colon between the ascending colon and the descending colon (Chapter 23)
    transverse foramen
    opening found only in the transverse processes of cervical vertebrae (Chapter 7)
    transverse plane
    two-dimensional, horizontal plane that divides the body or organ into superior and inferior portions (Chapter 1)
    transverse process
    paired bony processes that extends laterally from the vertebral arch of a vertebra (Chapter 7)
    transverse sinuses
    dural sinuses that drain along either side of the occipital–cerebellar space (Chapter 13)
    transverse sinuses
    pair of enlarged veins near the lambdoid suture that drain the occipital, sagittal, and straight sinuses, and leads to the sigmoid sinuses (Chapter 20)
    transversospinales
    muscles that originate at the transverse processes and insert at the spinous processes of the vertebrae (Chapter 11)
    transversus abdominis
    deep layer of the abdomen that has fascicles arranged transversely around the abdomen (Chapter 11)
    trapezium
    from the lateral side, the first of the four distal carpal bones; articulates with the scaphoid proximally, the first and second metacarpals distally, and the trapezoid medially (Chapter 8)
    trapezius
    muscle that stabilizes the upper part of the back (Chapter 11)
    trapezoid
    from the lateral side, the second of the four distal carpal bones; articulates with the scaphoid proximally, the second metacarpal distally, the trapezium laterally, and the capitate medially (Chapter 8)
    treppe
    stepwise increase in contraction tension (Chapter 10)
    tri
    three (Chapter 11)
    triad
    the grouping of one T-tubule and two terminal cisternae (Chapter 10)
    tricarboxylic acid cycle (TCA)
    also called the Krebs cycle or the citric acid cycle; converts pyruvate into CO2 and high-energy FADH2, NADH, and ATP molecules (Chapter 24)
    triceps brachii
    three-headed muscle that extends the forearm (Chapter 11)
    tricuspid valve
    term used most often in clinical settings for the right atrioventricular valve (Chapter 19)
    trigeminal ganglion
    sensory ganglion that contributes sensory fibers to the trigeminal nerve (Chapter 13)
    trigeminal nerve
    fifth cranial nerve; responsible for cutaneous sensation of the face and contraction of the muscles of mastication (Chapter 13)
    triglyceride
    lipid compound composed of a glycerol molecule bonded with three fatty acid chains (Chapter 2)
    triglycerides
    lipids, or fats, consisting of three fatty acid chains attached to a glycerol backbone (Chapter 24)
    trigone
    area at the base of the bladder marked by the two ureters in the posterior–lateral aspect and the urethral orifice in the anterior aspect oriented like points on a triangle (Chapter 25)
    triiodothyronine
    (also, T3) amino acid–derived thyroid hormone that is less abundant but more potent than T4 (Chapter 17)
    trimester
    division of the duration of a pregnancy into three 3-month terms (Chapter 28)
    triplet
    consecutive sequence of three nucleotides on a DNA molecule that, when transcribed into an mRNA codon, corresponds to a particular amino acid (Chapter 3)
    triquetrum
    from the lateral side, the third of the four proximal carpal bones; articulates with the lunate laterally, the hamate distally, and has a facet for the pisiform (Chapter 8)
    trochlea
    pulley-shaped region located medially at the distal end of the humerus; articulates at the elbow with the trochlear notch of the ulna (Chapter 8)
    trochlea
    cartilaginous structure that acts like a pulley for the superior oblique muscle (Chapter 14)
    trochlear nerve
    fourth cranial nerve; responsible for contraction of one of the extraocular muscles (Chapter 13)
    trochlear notch
    large, C-shaped depression located on the anterior side of the proximal ulna; articulates at the elbow with the trochlea of the humerus (Chapter 8)
    trophoblast
    fluid-filled shell of squamous cells destined to become the chorionic villi, placenta, and associated fetal membranes (Chapter 28)
    tropomyosin
    regulatory protein that covers myosin-binding sites to prevent actin from binding to myosin (Chapter 10)
    troponin
    regulatory protein that binds to actin, tropomyosin, and calcium (Chapter 10)
    true labor
    regular contractions that immediately precede childbirth; they do not abate with hydration or rest, and they become more frequent and powerful with time (Chapter 28)
    true ribs
    vertebrosternal ribs 1–7 that attach via their costal cartilage directly to the sternum (Chapter 7)
    true vocal cord
    one of the pair of folded, white membranes that have a free inner edge that oscillates as air passes through to produce sound (Chapter 22)
    truncus arteriosus
    portion of the primitive heart that will eventually divide and give rise to the ascending aorta and pulmonary trunk (Chapter 19)
    trunk
    large vessel that gives rise to smaller vessels (Chapter 20)
    trypsin
    pancreatic enzyme that activates chymotrypsin and digests protein (Chapter 24)
    trypsinogen
    proenzyme form of trypsin (Chapter 24)
    tubercle of the rib
    small bump on the posterior side of a rib for articulation with the transverse process of a thoracic vertebra (Chapter 7)
    tubuloglomerular feedback
    feedback mechanism involving the JGA; macula densa cells monitor Na+ concentration in the terminal portion of the ascending loop of Henle and act to cause vasoconstriction or vasodilation of afferent and efferent arterioles to alter GFR (Chapter 25)
    tunica externa
    (also, tunica adventitia) outermost layer or tunic of a vessel (except capillaries) (Chapter 20)
    tunica intima
    (also, tunica interna) innermost lining or tunic of a vessel (Chapter 20)
    tunica media
    middle layer or tunic of a vessel (except capillaries) (Chapter 20)
    twitch
    single contraction produced by one action potential (Chapter 10)
    tympanic membrane
    ear drum (Chapter 14)
    type I alveolar cell
    squamous epithelial cells that are the major cell type in the alveolar wall; highly permeable to gases (Chapter 22)
    type I hypersensitivity
    immediate response mediated by mast cell degranulation caused by the crosslinking of the antigen-specific IgE molecules on the mast cell surface (Chapter 21)
    type II alveolar cell
    cuboidal epithelial cells that are the minor cell type in the alveolar wall; secrete pulmonary surfactant (Chapter 22)
    type II hypersensitivity
    cell damage caused by the binding of antibody and the activation of complement, usually against red blood cells (Chapter 21)
    type III hypersensitivity
    damage to tissues caused by the deposition of antibody-antigen (immune) complexes followed by the activation of complement (Chapter 21)

    U¶

    ulna
    bone located on the medial side of the forearm (Chapter 8)
    ulnar artery
    formed at the bifurcation of the brachial artery; parallels the ulna; gives off smaller branches until it reaches the carpal region where it fuses with the radial artery to form the superficial and deep palmar arches; supplies blood to the lower arm and carpal region (Chapter 20)
    ulnar collateral ligament
    intrinsic ligament on the medial side of the elbow joint; spans from the medial epicondyle of the humerus to the medial ulna (Chapter 9)
    ulnar nerve
    systemic nerve of the arm located close to the ulna, a bone of the forearm (Chapter 13)
    ulnar notch of the radius
    shallow, smooth area located on the medial side of the distal radius; articulates with the head of the ulna at the distal radioulnar joint (Chapter 8)
    ulnar tuberosity
    roughened area located on the anterior, proximal ulna inferior to the coronoid process (Chapter 8)
    ulnar vein
    parallels the ulna and ulnar artery; arises from the palmar venous arches and leads to the brachial vein (Chapter 20)
    ultrasonography
    application of ultrasonic waves to visualize subcutaneous body structures such as tendons and organs (Chapter 1)
    umami
    taste submodality for sensitivity to the concentration of amino acids; also called the savory sense (Chapter 14)
    umbilical arteries
    pair of vessels that runs within the umbilical cord and carries fetal blood low in oxygen and high in waste to the placenta for exchange with maternal blood (Chapter 20)
    umbilical cord
    connection between the developing conceptus and the placenta; carries deoxygenated blood and wastes from the fetus and returns nutrients and oxygen from the mother (Chapter 28)
    umbilical vein
    single vessel that originates in the placenta and runs within the umbilical cord, carrying oxygen- and nutrient-rich blood to the fetal heart (Chapter 20)
    uniaxial joint
    type of diarthrosis; joint that allows for motion within only one plane (one axis) (Chapter 9)
    unipennate
    pennate muscle that has fascicles located on one side of the tendon (Chapter 11)
    unipolar
    shape of a neuron which has only one process that includes both the axon and dendrite (Chapter 12)
    unipotent
    describes the condition of being committed to a single specialized cell type (Chapter 3)
    universal donor
    individual with type O− blood (Chapter 18)
    universal recipient
    individual with type AB+ blood (Chapter 18)
    upper esophageal sphincter
    skeletal muscle sphincter that regulates food movement from the pharynx to the esophagus (Chapter 23)
    upper motor neuron
    first neuron in the motor command pathway with its cell body in the cerebral cortex that synapses on the lower motor neuron in the spinal cord (Chapter 12)
    upregulation
    increase in the number of hormone receptors, typically in response to chronically reduced levels of a hormone (Chapter 17)
    urea cycle
    process that converts potentially toxic nitrogen waste into urea that can be eliminated through the kidneys (Chapter 24)
    urethra
    transports urine from the bladder to the outside environment (Chapter 25)
    urinalysis
    analysis of urine to diagnose disease (Chapter 25)
    urochrome
    heme-derived pigment that imparts the typical yellow color of urine (Chapter 25)
    urogenital triangle
    anterior triangle of the perineum that includes the external genitals (Chapter 11)
    uterine tubes
    (also, fallopian tubes or oviducts) ducts that facilitate transport of an ovulated oocyte to the uterus (Chapter 27)
    uterus
    muscular hollow organ in which a fertilized egg develops into a fetus (Chapter 27)
    utricle
    structure of the inner ear responsible for transducing linear acceleration in the horizontal plane (Chapter 14)

    V¶

    vagina
    tunnel-like organ that provides access to the uterus for the insertion of semen and from the uterus for the birth of a baby (Chapter 27)
    vagus nerve
    tenth cranial nerve; responsible for the autonomic control of organs in the thoracic and upper abdominal cavities (Chapter 13)
    valence shell
    outermost electron shell of an atom (Chapter 2)
    Valsalva’s maneuver
    voluntary contraction of the diaphragm and abdominal wall muscles and closing of the glottis, which increases intra-abdominal pressure and facilitates defecation (Chapter 23)
    valve
    in the cardiovascular system, a specialized structure located within the heart or vessels that ensures one-way flow of blood (Chapter 19)
    variable region domain
    part of a lymphocyte antigen receptor that varies considerably between different receptor types (Chapter 21)
    varicosity
    enlargement of neurons that release neurotransmitters into synaptic clefts (Chapter 10)
    varicosity
    structure of some autonomic connections that is not a typical synaptic end bulb, but a string of swellings along the length of a fiber that makes a network of connections with the target effector (Chapter 15)
    vasa recta
    branches of the efferent arterioles that parallel the course of the loops of Henle and are continuous with the peritubular capillaries; with the glomerulus, form a portal system (Chapter 25)
    vasa vasorum
    small blood vessels located within the walls or tunics of larger vessels that supply nourishment to and remove wastes from the cells of the vessels (Chapter 20)
    vascular shock
    type of shock that occurs when arterioles lose their normal muscular tone and dilate dramatically (Chapter 20)
    vascular shunt
    continuation of the metarteriole and thoroughfare channel that allows blood to bypass the capillary beds to flow directly from the arterial to the venous circulation (Chapter 20)
    vascular spasm
    initial step in hemostasis, in which the smooth muscle in the walls of the ruptured or damaged blood vessel contracts (Chapter 18)
    vascular tone
    contractile state of smooth muscle in a blood vessel (Chapter 20)
    vascular tubes
    rudimentary blood vessels in a developing fetus (Chapter 20)
    vascular tunic
    middle layer of the eye primarily composed of connective tissue with a rich blood supply (Chapter 14)
    vasoconstriction
    constriction of the smooth muscle of a blood vessel, resulting in a decreased vascular diameter (Chapter 20)
    vasodilation
    widening of blood vessels (Chapter 4)
    vasodilation
    relaxation of the smooth muscle in the wall of a blood vessel, resulting in an increased vascular diameter (Chapter 20)
    vasomotion
    irregular, pulsating flow of blood through capillaries and related structures (Chapter 20)
    vasomotor nerves
    preganglionic sympathetic fibers that cause the constriction of blood vessels in response to signals from the cardiovascular center (Chapter 15)
    vastus intermedius
    quadricep muscle that is between the vastus lateralis and vastus medialis and is deep to the rectus femoris (Chapter 11)
    vastus lateralis
    quadricep muscle on the lateral aspect of the thigh (Chapter 11)
    vastus medialis
    quadricep muscle on the medial aspect of the thigh (Chapter 11)
    vein
    blood vessel that conducts blood toward the heart (Chapter 20)
    venous reserve
    volume of blood contained within systemic veins in the integument, bone marrow, and liver that can be returned to the heart for circulation, if needed (Chapter 20)
    ventilation
    movement of air into and out of the lungs; consists of inspiration and expiration (Chapter 22)
    ventral
    describes the front or direction toward the front of the body; also referred to as anterior (Chapter 1)
    ventral (anterior) nerve root
    axons emerging from the anterior or lateral horns of the spinal cord (Chapter 13)
    ventral cavity
    larger body cavity located anterior to the posterior (dorsal) body cavity; includes the serous membrane-lined pleural cavities for the lungs, pericardial cavity for the heart, and peritoneal cavity for the abdominal and pelvic organs; also referred to as anterior body cavity (Chapter 1)
    ventral posterior nucleus
    nucleus in the thalamus that is the target of gustatory sensations and projects to the cerebral cortex (Chapter 14)
    ventral respiratory group (VRG)
    region of the medulla oblongata that stimulates the contraction of the accessory muscles involved in respiration to induce forced inspiration and expiration (Chapter 22)
    ventral stream
    connections between cortical areas from the occipital lobe to the temporal lobe that are responsible for identification of visual stimuli (Chapter 14)
    ventricle
    central cavity within the brain where CSF is produced and circulates (Chapter 12)
    ventricle
    one of the primary pumping chambers of the heart located in the lower portion of the heart; the left ventricle is the major pumping chamber on the lower left side of the heart that ejects blood into the systemic circuit via the aorta and receives blood from the left atrium; the right ventricle is the major pumping chamber on the lower right side of the heart that ejects blood into the pulmonary circuit via the pulmonary trunk and receives blood from the right atrium (Chapter 19)
    ventricles
    remnants of the hollow center of the neural tube that are spaces for cerebrospinal fluid to circulate through the brain (Chapter 13)
    ventricular ejection phase
    second phase of ventricular systole during which blood is pumped from the ventricle (Chapter 19)
    venule
    small vessel leading from the capillaries to veins (Chapter 20)
    vermis
    prominent ridge along the midline of the cerebellum that is referred to as the spinocerebellum (Chapter 16)
    vernix caseosa
    waxy, cheese-like substance that protects the delicate fetal skin until birth (Chapter 28)
    vertebra
    individual bone in the neck and back regions of the vertebral column (Chapter 7)
    vertebral (spinal) canal
    bony passageway within the vertebral column for the spinal cord that is formed by the series of individual vertebral foramina (Chapter 7)
    vertebral arch
    bony arch formed by the posterior portion of each vertebra that surrounds and protects the spinal cord (Chapter 7)
    vertebral arteries
    arteries that ascend along either side of the vertebral column through the transverse foramina of the cervical vertebrae and enter the cranium through the foramen magnum (Chapter 13)
    vertebral artery
    arises from the subclavian artery and passes through the vertebral foramen through the foramen magnum to the brain; joins with the internal carotid artery to form the arterial circle; supplies blood to the brain and spinal cord (Chapter 20)
    vertebral column
    entire sequence of bones that extend from the skull to the tailbone (Chapter 7)
    vertebral foramen
    opening associated with each vertebra defined by the vertebral arch that provides passage for the spinal cord (Chapter 7)
    vertebral vein
    arises from the base of the brain and the cervical region of the spinal cord; passes through the intervertebral foramina in the cervical vertebrae; drains smaller veins from the cranium, spinal cord, and vertebrae, and leads to the brachiocephalic vein; counterpart of the vertebral artery (Chapter 20)
    vesicle
    membrane-bound structure that contains materials within or outside of the cell (Chapter 3)
    vestibular fold
    part of the folded region of the glottis composed of mucous membrane; supports the epiglottis during swallowing (Chapter 22)
    vestibular ganglion
    location of neuronal cell bodies that transmit equilibrium information along the eighth cranial nerve (Chapter 14)
    vestibular nuclei
    targets of the vestibular component of the eighth cranial nerve (Chapter 14)
    vestibule
    in the ear, the portion of the inner ear responsible for the sense of equilibrium (Chapter 14)
    vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR)
    reflex based on connections between the vestibular system and the cranial nerves of eye movements that ensures images are stabilized on the retina as the head and body move (Chapter 14)
    vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR)
    reflex based on connections between the vestibular system and the cranial nerves of eye movements that ensures that images are stabilized on the retina as the head and body move (Chapter 16)
    vestibulocerebellum
    flocculonodular lobe of the cerebellum named for the vestibular input from the eighth cranial nerve (Chapter 16)
    vestibulocochlear nerve
    eighth cranial nerve; responsible for the sensations of hearing and balance (Chapter 13)
    vestibulospinal tract
    extrapyramidal connections between the vestibular nuclei in the brain stem and spinal cord that modulate movement and contribute to balance on the basis of the sense of equilibrium (Chapter 14)
    villus
    projection of the mucosa of the small intestine (Chapter 23)
    visceral branches
    branches of the descending aorta that supply blood to the viscera (Chapter 20)
    visceral muscle
    smooth muscle found in the walls of visceral organs (Chapter 10)
    visceral pleura
    innermost layer of the pleura that is superficial to the lungs and extends into the lung fissures (Chapter 22)
    visceral reflex
    reflex involving an internal organ as the effector, under the control of the autonomic nervous system (Chapter 15)
    visceral sense
    sense associated with the internal organs (Chapter 14)
    vision
    special sense of sight based on transduction of light stimuli (Chapter 14)
    visual acuity
    property of vision related to the sharpness of focus, which varies in relation to retinal position (Chapter 14)
    vital capacity (VC)
    sum of TV, ERV, and IRV, which is all the volumes that participate in gas exchange (Chapter 22)
    vitamin D
    compound that aids absorption of calcium and phosphates in the intestine to improve bone health (Chapter 5)
    vitamins
    organic compounds required by the body to perform biochemical reactions like metabolism and bone, cell, and tissue growth (Chapter 24)
    vitiligo
    skin condition in which melanocytes in certain areas lose the ability to produce melanin, possibly due an autoimmune reaction that leads to loss of color in patches (Chapter 5)
    vitreous humor
    viscous fluid that fills the posterior chamber of the eye (Chapter 14)
    voltage-gated channel
    ion channel that opens because of a change in the charge distributed across the membrane where it is located (Chapter 12)
    voltage-gated sodium channels
    membrane proteins that open sodium channels in response to a sufficient voltage change, and initiate and transmit the action potential as Na+ enters through the channel (Chapter 10)
    voluntary phase
    initial phase of deglutition, in which the bolus moves from the mouth to the oropharynx (Chapter 23)
    vomer bone
    unpaired bone that forms the inferior and posterior portions of the nasal septum (Chapter 7)
    vulva
    external female genitalia (Chapter 27)

    W¶

    wave summation
    addition of successive neural stimuli to produce greater contraction (Chapter 10)
    Weber test
    use of a tuning fork to test the laterality of hearing loss by placing it at several locations on the midline of the skull (Chapter 16)
    Wernicke’s area
    region at the posterior end of the lateral sulcus in which speech comprehension is localized (Chapter 16)
    white blood cells (WBCs)
    (also, leukocytes) one of the formed elements of blood that provides defense against disease agents and foreign materials (Chapter 18)
    white matter
    regions of the nervous system containing mostly myelinated axons, making the tissue appear white because of the high lipid content of myelin (Chapter 12)
    white rami communicantes
    (singular = ramus communicans) myelinated structures that provide a short connection from a sympathetic chain ganglion to the spinal nerve that contains the preganglionic sympathetic fiber (Chapter 15)
    Wolffian duct
    duct system present in the embryo that will eventually form the internal male reproductive structures (Chapter 27)
    working memory
    function of the prefrontal cortex to maintain a representation of information that is not in the immediate environment (Chapter 14)
    wound contraction
    process whereby the borders of a wound are physically drawn together (Chapter 4)

    X¶

    X-linked
    pattern of inheritance in which an allele is carried on the X chromosome of the 23rd pair (Chapter 28)
    X-linked dominant
    pattern of dominant inheritance that corresponds to a gene on the X chromosome of the 23rd pair (Chapter 28)
    X-linked recessive
    pattern of recessive inheritance that corresponds to a gene on the X chromosome of the 23rd pair (Chapter 28)
    X-ray
    form of high energy electromagnetic radiation with a short wavelength capable of penetrating solids and ionizing gases; used in medicine as a diagnostic aid to visualize body structures such as bones (Chapter 1)
    xiphoid process
    small process that forms the inferior tip of the sternum (Chapter 7)

    Y¶

    yellow marrow
    connective tissue in the interior cavity of a bone where fat is stored (Chapter 6)
    yolk sac
    membrane associated with primitive circulation to the developing embryo; source of the first blood cells and germ cells and contributes to the umbilical cord structure (Chapter 28)

    Z¶

    zona fasciculata
    intermediate region of the adrenal cortex that produce hormones called glucocorticoids (Chapter 17)
    zona glomerulosa
    most superficial region of the adrenal cortex, which produces the hormones collectively referred to as mineralocorticoids (Chapter 17)
    zona pellucida
    thick, gel-like glycoprotein membrane that coats the oocyte and must be penetrated by sperm before fertilization can occur (Chapter 28)
    zona reticularis
    deepest region of the adrenal cortex, which produces the steroid sex hormones called androgens (Chapter 17)
    zone of calcified matrix
    region of the epiphyseal plate closest to the diaphyseal end; functions to connect the epiphyseal plate to the diaphysis (Chapter 6)
    zone of maturation and hypertrophy
    region of the epiphyseal plate where chondrocytes from the proliferative zone grow and mature and contribute to the longitudinal growth of the epiphyseal plate (Chapter 6)
    zonule fibers
    fibrous connections between the ciliary body and the lens (Chapter 14)
    zygapophysial joints
    facet joints; plane joints between the superior and inferior articular processes of adjacent vertebrae that provide for only limited motions between the vertebrae (Chapter 9)
    zygomatic arch
    elongated, free-standing arch on the lateral skull, formed anteriorly by the temporal process of the zygomatic bone and posteriorly by the zygomatic process of the temporal bone (Chapter 7)
    zygomatic bone
    cheekbone; paired bones that contribute to the lateral orbit and anterior zygomatic arch (Chapter 7)
    zygomatic process of the temporal bone
    extension from the temporal bone that forms the posterior portion of the zygomatic arch (Chapter 7)
    zygote
    fertilized egg; a diploid cell resulting from the fertilization of haploid gametes from the male and female lines (Chapter 28)
